<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We resolve the planar Cohn–Elkies sharpness conjecture: the two-point Fourier bound attains the optimal circle-packing density $`\pi/(2\sqrt3)`$. We construct a radial Schwartz certificate and prove its global sign conditions using rigorous interval arithmetic and analytic estimates. The same certificate recovers the classical uniqueness of the triangular packing among periodic equality cases.

- **claim-sharp-certificate** (theorem, [source](#stmt-intro-main)): There is a real radial Schwartz function $f:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb R$ such that $$\label{intro:conditions} \widehat f(0)=1,\qquad f(0)=\frac{2}{\sqrt3},\qquad \widehat f(\xi)\ge0\quad(\xi\in\mathbb R^2),\qquad f(x)\le0\quad(\|x\|\ge1).$$
  - Explanation: Existence of a real radial Schwartz function in the plane with the normalizations, the nonnegative transform and the nonpositive values outside the unit radius that a sharp Fourier certificate needs for the planar packing bound.
- **claim-finite-measure-representation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-fourier-measure-lemma)): The function in <a href="#fourier:cardinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:cardinal">[fourier:cardinal]</a> extends to an entire function with the representation $$\label{fourier:measure} p(s)=\int_{-1}^{1}e^{i\pi ts}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu(t),\qquad s\in\mathbb C,$$ where $\mu$ is a finite complex measure, supported in $[-1,1]$, with $\mu(-E)=\overline{\mu(E)}$ for Borel sets $E$. Its total variation satisfies $$\label{fourier:variation} \left\lVert\mu\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}} \le25\left\lvert C\right\rvert+\frac{65\pi^2}{18}\sum_n\left\lvert c_n\right\rvert +\frac{32\pi}{3}\sum_n\left\lvert d_n\right\rvert.$$ The atoms of $\mu$ have mass $CP_j$ at $t_j$, for $-6\le j\le6$. For real-axis evaluations, the remaining measure can be folded onto $[0,1]$: on $t_{j-1}<t<t_j$, its folded density is $\sum_n(c_nu_n(t)+d_nv_n(t))$, where $$\label{fourier:folded} \begin{split} u_n(t)&=-2\pi^2e^{-i\pi tn} \sum_{l=j}^{6}(t_l-t)P_l e^{i\pi t_ln},\\ v_n(t)&=2i\pi e^{-i\pi tn} \sum_{l=j}^{6}P_l e^{i\pi t_ln}. \end{split}$$ The folded atoms have masses $CP_0$ at $0$ and $2CP_j$ at $t_j$, $1\le j\le6$. Integrate against this folded measure and take the real part to obtain $p(s)$ for real $s$, or any of its real-axis derivatives.
  - Explanation: The cardinal series with double and simple poles at the nodes extends to an entire function given by a finite complex measure on a bounded frequency interval, with an explicit total variation bound in terms of the coefficient list.
- **claim-gaussian-fourier-pairing** (theorem, [source](#stmt-fourier-transform)): The function $q_p$ is real, radial, and Schwartz on $\mathbb R^2$, and $$\widehat{q_p}(\xi)=e^{-\pi hs}K_p(s),\qquad s=b\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^2.$$ The function $K_p$ is entire and real on the real axis. Moreover, $$\label{fourier:damping} \operatorname{Im}z(t) =h\left(\frac{B}{t^2+h^2}-1\right) \ge\frac{26}{435}>0\qquad(-1\le t\le1).$$ Consequently every derivative of $K_p(s)$ decays exponentially as $s\to+\infty$.
  - Explanation: The Gaussian-damped cardinal function is real, radial and Schwartz, and its transform is again a Gaussian times an entire kernel whose frequencies have strictly positive imaginary part, giving exponential decay of all derivatives.
- **claim-coefficient-form-interpolation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-fourier-equivalence)): For two real $\ell^1$ coefficient lists, conditions <a href="#fourier:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:interpolation">[fourier:interpolation]</a> are equivalent to $$\label{fourier:coefficient-equations} \begin{split} c_{i,n}&=-\frac{K_{3-i}(n)}{Q_n},\\ d_{i,n}&=\frac{D_nK_{3-i}(n)-K_{3-i}'(n)}{Q_n} -\mathbf1_{\{i=1,\ n=1\}} \qquad(i=1,2,\ n\in\mathcal N). \end{split}$$
  - Explanation: The prescribed zeros and slopes of the two coupled functions at the nodes are equivalent to explicit formulas expressing each coefficient through the value and derivative of the partner kernel at that node.
- **claim-finite-certificates** (bound, [source](#stmt-interpolation-finite)): For each $\eta\in\{1,-1\}$, the matrix $I-\eta D$ is invertible, and $$\label{interpolation:finite-matrices} \left\lVert(I-\eta D)^{-1}\right\rVert<32,\qquad \left\lVert(I-\eta D)^{-1}S_{J,E}\right\rVert<3.7,\qquad \left\lVert S_{E\cap[7,16],J}\right\rVert<.09.$$ For $i=1,2$ and $n\in I_f$, the table satisfies $$\label{interpolation:finite-residuals} \begin{split} \left\lvert\widetilde c_{i,n}+\frac{\widetilde K_{3-i}(n)}{Q_n}\right\rvert &<10^{-9},\\ \left\lvert\widetilde d_{i,n}+\mathbf 1_{\{i=1,\ n=1\}} +\frac{\widetilde K_{3-i}'(n)-D_n\widetilde K_{3-i}(n)}{Q_n}\right\rvert &<10^{-9}. \end{split}$$ For each $i$, the norm of the tabulated list, including node zero, is less than $2.29$, and its norm on nodes greater than $40$ is less than $.00002$.
  - Explanation: Interval-arithmetic certificate that the small block matrices are invertible with controlled norms, that the tabulated coefficient lists satisfy the coefficient equations up to tiny residuals, and that the tabulated lists have bounded norm.
- **claim-inverse-whole-space** (bound, [source](#stmt-interpolation-inverse)): For each $\eta\in\{1,-1\}$, $I-\eta S$ has a bounded inverse on $X$. If $r=r_f+r_t$, with $r_f$ supported on $I_f$ and $r_t$ on $\mathcal N\cap(100,\infty)$, then $$\label{interpolation:inverse-bound} \left\lVert(I-\eta S)^{-1}r\right\rVert_1 \le91\left\lVert r_f\right\rVert_1+2540\left\lVert r_t\right\rVert_1.$$
  - Explanation: The coupled operator shifted by the identity has a bounded inverse on the full unweighted sequence space, with separate weights for finite-block and tail residuals, obtained by two Schur complement eliminations and Neumann series.
- **claim-exact-interpolation-solution** (theorem, [source](#stmt-interpolation-solution)): There are unique real lists $x_1,x_2\in X$ satisfying <a href="#fourier:coefficient-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:coefficient-equations">[fourier:coefficient-equations]</a> with the fixed data <a href="#fourier:fixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:fixed">[fourier:fixed]</a>. For each $i=1,2$, $$\label{interpolation:coefficient-error} \sum_{n\in\mathcal N} \bigl(\left\lvert c_{i,n}-\widetilde c_{i,n}\right\rvert +\left\lvert d_{i,n}-\widetilde d_{i,n}\right\rvert\bigr)<.00003.$$ The norm of each exact list, including its node-zero coefficients, is less than $2.3$. The corresponding functions satisfy all the interpolation conditions <a href="#fourier:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:interpolation">[fourier:interpolation]</a>.
  - Explanation: Within the chosen coefficient space and fixed zero-node data there is exactly one pair of exact coefficient lists solving the interpolation equations, lying within a small sequence-norm distance of the tabulated lists.
- **claim-bernstein-half-gap** (bound, [source](#stmt-signs-bernstein)): For every specified triple $(i,m,y)$, put $$\label{signs:power-coefficients} T_r=\sigma_i y^r \frac{\widetilde H_i^{(r+\nu)}(m)}{(r+\nu)!}, \qquad 0\le r\le28.$$ Then, for $0\le k\le28$, $$\label{signs:bernstein-bound} B_k:=\sum_{r=0}^k \frac{\binom{k}{r}}{\binom{28}{r}}T_r >\begin{cases} 0.74,&m=0,\\ 0.18,&m=1,\\ 0.02,&m=3,4,\\ 0.009,&4<m\le40. \end{cases}$$
  - Explanation: Every Bernstein coefficient of the truncated Taylor quotient on each specified half-gap exceeds an explicit positive threshold depending on the center node, certifying positivity of that polynomial on the whole half-gap.
- **claim-p-quadratic-lower-bound** (bound, [source](#stmt-signs-p-lower-bound)): If $m\in12\mathbb Z+A$ is a nearest zero of $P$ to a real number $s$, then $$P(s)\ge0.68(s-m)^2.$$
  - Explanation: The trigonometric vanishing factor is bounded below by a fixed multiple of the squared distance to its nearest zero, proved by logarithmic concavity on half-gaps and exact midpoint values.
- **claim-global-signs-zeros** (classification, [source](#stmt-signs-global)): The exact functions have the zero sets $$\label{signs:zero-sets} \{s\ge1:H_1(s)=0\}=\mathcal N,\qquad \{s\ge0:H_2(s)=0\}=\mathcal N,$$ and satisfy the strict inequalities $$H_1(s)<0\quad(s\ge1,\ s\notin\mathcal N),\qquad H_2(s)>0\quad(s\ge0,\ s\notin\mathcal N).$$ The zero of $H_1$ at $1$ is simple. Its zeros at $n\in\mathcal N$ with $n>1$, and the zeros of $H_2$ at every $n\in\mathcal N$, have order exactly two as zeros of the entire functions of $s$. Consequently $F(x)\le0$ when $b\left\lvert x\right\rvert^2\ge1$, and $\widehat F(\xi)\ge0$ for every $\xi\in\mathbb R^2$.
  - Explanation: The two exact coupled functions have precisely the node set as zero set on their sign domains, strict signs elsewhere, a simple zero at the first node for one of them and exactly double zeros otherwise, which gives the required signs of the pair.
- **claim-exact-origin-value** (theorem, [source](#stmt-normalization-origin-lemma)): The Fourier pair constructed above satisfies $$\label{normalization:origin} F(0)=\widehat F(0)=6Q_1e^{-\pi h}>0.$$
  - Explanation: Poisson summation on the covolume-one lattice and its differentiated dilation identity show the two origin values coincide and are strictly positive, without any numerical estimate.
- **claim-periodic-equality-case** (classification, [source](#stmt-normalization-periodic-uniqueness)): If a periodic packing of disks of radius $1/2$ has covered-area density $\pi/(2\sqrt3)$, then its center set is the image of $\mathbb Z(1,0)+\mathbb Z(1/2,b)$ under a Euclidean isometry.
  - Explanation: Any periodic disk packing attaining the optimal covered-area density has centers forming a triangular lattice up to a Euclidean isometry, derived from integrality of squared distances and a classification of even integral rank-two lattices.
- **claim-second-poisson-identity** (theorem, [source](#stmt-normalization-second-poisson)): The exact pair satisfies the absolutely convergent identity $$\label{normalization:curvature-identity} \sum_{n\in\mathcal N}r_\Lambda(n)n^2 \bigl(G_1''(n)-G_2''(n)\bigr)=2G_2(0).$$ Using the global signs, it follows that $$\label{normalization:curvature-inequality} H_1''(1)-H_2''(1)\ge2(1-\pi h)Q_1.$$
  - Explanation: A second differentiation of the dilation identity gives an absolutely convergent relation among the curvatures at the nodes, and with the global signs a lower bound for the curvature difference at the first node.
- **lemma-quadrature-moment-error** (bound, [source](#stmt-cert-moment-error)): Let $p=PR$ have a real coefficient list supported in $\{0\}\cup I_f$, with $\\|(c,d)\\|_1\le3$, and let $C\in\mathbb R$ satisfy $\|C\|\le1$. Let $m,y\in\mathbb R$ and $l\in\{0,\ldots,30\}$ satisfy $0\le m\le100$ and $\|y\|\le3/2$. The quadrature rule <a href="#cert:rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:rule">[cert:rule]</a>, with exact evaluations at its nodes and at the atoms, has error less than $10^{-22}$ in each moment $U_{p,l}(m,y)$ and $X_{p,l}(m,y)$.
  - Explanation: The first Fejér quadrature rule with exact evaluations has a tiny uniform error in every direct and transformed moment over the stated finite input domain, proved by analytic continuation to disks and a Cauchy tail estimate.
- **lemma-rounded-exponential** (bound, [source](#stmt-cert-rounded-exponential)): For every $u\in\mathbb Q(i)$ satisfying $\Re(ip_*u)\le0$ and $\|p_*u\|\le1000$, the recursion <a href="#cert:estar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:estar">[cert:estar]</a> satisfies $$\|e_*(u)-e^{ip_*u}\|<3.542\cdot10^{-34}<10^{-32}.$$
  - Explanation: A finite rational recursion with truncated Taylor series, floors and repeated squaring approximates the complex exponential with a rigorous tiny error on the stated bounded left half-plane domain.
- **lemma-rational-moment-error** (bound, [source](#stmt-cert-rational-moment-error)): Let $p=PR$ have a rational coefficient list supported in $\{0\}\cup I_f$, with $\\|(c,d)\\|_1\le3$, and let $C\in\mathbb Q$ satisfy $\|C\|\le1$. Let $m,y\in\mathbb Q$ and $l\in\{0,\ldots,30\}$ satisfy $0\le m\le100$ and $\|y\|\le3/2$. The prescription in Section <a href="#cert:rational" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:rational">7.2</a> gives rational numbers $U_{p,l}^{\#}$ and $X_{p,l}^{\#}$ such that $$\|U_{p,l}^{\#}-U_{p,l}\|<10^{-14},\qquad \|X_{p,l}^{\#}-X_{p,l}\|<10^{-14}.$$ Separately, for every node $n\in12\mathbb Z+A$, using its residue $a\in A$ in <a href="#fourier:node-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-data">[fourier:node-data]</a>, the prescribed rational values satisfy $$\|Q_n^{\#}-Q_n\|<10^{-25},\qquad \|D_n^{\#}-D_n\|<10^{-25}.$$
  - Explanation: Replacing exact trigonometric and exponential values by the rational prescription keeps every direct and transformed moment, and every node constant, within explicit small errors.
- **lemma-rational-first-block-error** (bound, [source](#stmt-cert-rational-small-error)): For each basis input $c_n=1$ or $d_n=1$, $n\in J$, with all other data zero, and each output node $m\in J$, the rational program computes both entries of $\mathcal S_m(p)$ with absolute error less than $10^{-12}$.
  - Explanation: An independent exact-rational program with a smaller quadrature rule computes every entry of the first block of the operator within a small absolute error.
- **lemma-taylor-average-enclosure** (bound, [source](#stmt-ind-taylor-average)): Suppose $F(c_j+x)=\sum_{k\ge0}A_kx^k$ is analytic on a neighborhood of $\|x\|\le R$ and $\|F\|\le M$ there. Let $D\ge0$ be an integer. For $0\le k\le D$, suppose $\widehat A_k\in\mathbb Q(i)$ and $e_k\in\mathbb Q_{\ge0}$ satisfy $\|A_k-\widehat A_k\|\le e_k$. Then $$\begin{aligned} \label{ind:taylor-average-error} \left\|6\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_j}F(t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt- \sum_{2r\le D}\widehat A_{2r}\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}\right\| \le M2^{-D}+\sum_{2r\le D}e_{2r}\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}. \end{aligned}$$ If the final sum of radii is at most $\delta$ for each of the six pieces, their combined integral has error at most $M2^{-D}+\delta$. Discrete atoms are not included in this estimate and are to be enclosed separately.
  - Explanation: The average of an analytic function over a piece is enclosed by its even Taylor coefficients about the midpoint plus a geometric tail and the weighted coefficient radii.
- **claim-taylor-budgets** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-ind-taylor-budgets)): The following choices give sufficient rational error enclosures for the same finite quantities used in Appendix <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">6</a>.* *For a basis input at $n\in J=\{1,3,4\}$, a row $m\in J$, $y=1$, and $\ell=0,1$, take $D=63$, $h_D=32$, $M=10^6$, and $\delta=10^{-14}$ in <a href="#ind:coefficient-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:coefficient-budget">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>. There are no atoms. If $Q_m,D_m$ have rational errors at most $10^{-25}$, either entry of <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a> has error less than $2.13\cdot10^{-13}<10^{-12}$.* *For the full rational finite-input domain at the start of this subsection, take $D=255$, $h_D=128$, $M=10^{53}$, and $\delta=4\cdot10^{-15}$ in <a href="#ind:coefficient-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:coefficient-budget">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>, and require each of the seven atom-product radii in <a href="#ind:atom-radii" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:atom-radii">[ind:atom-radii]</a> to be at most $4\cdot10^{-15}/7$. Each $U$ and $X$ moment then has error less than $10^{-14}$. For node rows $m\in I_f$, with rational $Q_m,D_m$ errors at most $10^{-25}$, either entry of <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a> has error less than $1.8\cdot10^{-14}$.
  - Explanation: Two explicit choices of Taylor degree, majorant and coefficient budget give sufficient rational error enclosures for the first-block entries and for the full finite-input moments and pair entries.
- **claim-node-taylor-representation** (theorem, [source](#stmt-ind-node-taylor-proposition)): Let $p=PR$ have a real $\ell^1$ coefficient list as in <a href="#fourier:list-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:list-norm">[fourier:list-norm]</a>. Define $$\label{ind:node-P-coefficients} A_k(m)=\frac{P^{(k)}(m)}{k!} =\frac1{k!}\sum_{v=-6}^6P_v(i\pi t_v)^k e^{i\pi t_vm}.$$ Then $A_0(m)=A_1(m)=0$. The regular series has coefficients $$\label{ind:node-regular-coefficients} r_j(m)=C\mathbf1_{\{j=0\}}+ (-1)^j\sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal N_0\\n\ne m}} \left(\frac{(j+1)c_n}{(m-n)^{j+2}} +\frac{d_n}{(m-n)^{j+1}}\right),$$ and converges for $\|u\|<\rho_m$. For $0<\|u\|<\rho_m$, the Laurent expansion is $$R(m+u)=\frac{c_m}{u^2}+\frac{d_m}{u} +\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}r_j(m)u^j.$$ For every integer $\ell\ge0$ the direct derivative is therefore $$\label{ind:node-derivatives} \frac{p^{(\ell)}(m)}{\ell!} =c_mA_{\ell+2}(m)+d_mA_{\ell+1}(m) +\sum_{k=2}^{\ell}A_k(m)r_{\ell-k}(m),$$ where the last sum is empty for $\ell=0,1$.
  - Explanation: Around each node the direct cardinal function has an explicit Laurent expansion whose regular part converges within the distance to the nearest other node, giving closed formulas for all its derivatives at nodes.

      "exact_statement": "For two real $\\ell^1$ coefficient lists, conditions <a href=\"#fourier:interpolation\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"fourier:interpolation\">[fourier:interpolation]</a> are equivalent to $$\\label{fourier:coefficient-equations}\n \\begin{split}\n c_{i,n}&=-\\frac{K_{3-i}(n)}{Q_n},\\\\\n d_{i,n}&=\\frac{D_nK_{3-i}(n)-K_{3-i}'(n)}{Q_n}\n          -\\mathbf1_{\\{i=1,\\ n=1\\}}\n \\qquad(i=1,2,\\ n\\in\\mathcal N).\n \\end{split}$$",
      "explanation": "The prescribed zeros and slopes of the two coupled functions at the nodes are equivalent to explicit formulas expressing each coefficient through the value and derivative of the partner kernel at that node.",

      "hypotheses": [

    },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-interpolation-finite",
        "exact_quote": "For each $\\eta\\in\\{1,-1\\}$, the matrix $I-\\eta D$ is invertible, and $$\\label{interpolation:finite-matrices}\n \\left\\lVert(I-\\eta D)^{-1}\\right\\rVert<32,\\qquad\n \\left\\lVert(I-\\eta D)^{-1}S_{J,E}\\right\\rVert<3.7,\\qquad\n \\left\\lVert S_{E\\cap[7,16],J}\\right\\rVert<.09.$$ For $i=1,2$ and $n\\in I_f$, the table satisfies $$\\label{interpolation:finite-residuals}\n \\begin{split}\n \\left\\lvert\\widetilde c_{i,n}+\\frac{\\widetilde K_{3-i}(n)}{Q_n}\\right\\rvert\n &<10^{-9},\\\\\n \\left\\lvert\\widetilde d_{i,n}+\\mathbf 1_{\\{i=1,\\ n=1\\}}\n       +\\frac{\\widetilde K_{3-i}'(n)-D_n\\widetilde K_{3-i}(n)}{Q_n}\\right\\rvert\n &<10^{-9}.\n \\end{split}$$ For each $i$, the norm of the tabulated list, including node zero, is less than $2.29$, and its norm on nodes greater than $40$ is less than $.00002$.",

      },
      "evidence": [

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-signs-bernstein",
        "exact_quote": "For every specified triple $(i,m,y)$, put $$\\label{signs:power-coefficients}\n  T_r=\\sigma_i y^r\n       \\frac{\\widetilde H_i^{(r+\\nu)}(m)}{(r+\\nu)!},\n  \\qquad 0\\le r\\le28.$$ Then, for $0\\le k\\le28$, $$\\label{signs:bernstein-bound}\n  B_k:=\\sum_{r=0}^k\n       \\frac{\\binom{k}{r}}{\\binom{28}{r}}T_r\n  >\\begin{cases}\n      0.74,&m=0,\\\\\n      0.18,&m=1,\\\\\n      0.02,&m=3,4,\\\\\n      0.009,&4<m\\le40.\n    \\end{cases}$$",
        "source_id": "PM-090.3"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-090.3"

        "exact_quote": "The exact functions have the zero sets $$\\label{signs:zero-sets}\n \\{s\\ge1:H_1(s)=0\\}=\\mathcal N,\\qquad\n \\{s\\ge0:H_2(s)=0\\}=\\mathcal N,$$ and satisfy the strict inequalities $$H_1(s)<0\\quad(s\\ge1,\\ s\\notin\\mathcal N),\\qquad\n H_2(s)>0\\quad(s\\ge0,\\ s\\notin\\mathcal N).$$ The zero of $H_1$ at $1$ is simple. Its zeros at $n\\in\\mathcal N$ with $n>1$, and the zeros of $H_2$ at every $n\\in\\mathcal N$, have order exactly two as zeros of the entire functions of $s$.         "source_id": "PM-090.3"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-normalization-second-poisson",

        "source_id": "PM-090.3"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-cert-moment-error",
        "exact_quote": "Let $p=PR$ have a real coefficient list supported in $\\{0\\}\\cup I_f$, with $\\|(c,d)\\|_1\\le3$, and let $C\\in\\mathbb R$ satisfy $|C|\\le1$. Let $m,y\\in\\mathbb R$ and $l\\in\\{0,\\ldots,30\\}$ satisfy $0\\le m\\le100$ and $|y|\\le3/2$. The quadrature rule <a href=\"#cert:rule\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"cert:rule\">[cert:rule]</a>, with exact evaluations at its nodes and at the atoms, has error less than $10^{-22}$ in each moment $U_{p,l}(m,y)$ and $X_{p,l}(m,y)$.",
        "source_id": "PM-090.3"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ind-taylor-average",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose $F(c_j+x)=\\sum_{k\\ge0}A_kx^k$ is analytic on a neighborhood of $|x|\\le R$ and $|F|\\le M$ there. Let $D\\ge0$ be an integer. For $0\\le k\\le D$, suppose $\\widehat A_k\\in\\mathbb Q(i)$ and $e_k\\in\\mathbb Q_{\\ge0}$ satisfy $|A_k-\\widehat A_k|\\le e_k$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n\\label{ind:taylor-average-error}\n \\left|6\\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_j}F(t)\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm dt-\n       \\sum_{2r\\le D}\\widehat A_{2r}\\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}\\right|\n \\le M2^{-D}+\\sum_{2r\\le D}e_{2r}\\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}.\n\\end{aligned}$$ If the final sum of radii is at most $\\delta$ for each of the six pieces, their combined integral has error at most $M2^{-D}+\\delta$. Discrete atoms are not included in this estimate and are to be enclosed separately.",
        "source_id": "PM-090.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-ind-taylor-average",
          "exact_quote": "Suppose $F(c_j+x)=\\sum_{k\\ge0}A_kx^k$ is analytic on a neighborhood of $|x|\\le R$ and $|F|\\le M$ there. Let $D\\ge0$ be an integer. For $0\\le k\\le D$, suppose $\\widehat A_k\\in\\mathbb Q(i)$ and $e_k\\in\\mathbb Q_{\\ge0}$ satisfy $|A_k-\\widehat A_k|\\le e_k$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n\\label{ind:taylor-average-error}\n \\left|6\\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_j}F(t)\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm dt-\n       \\sum_{2r\\le D}\\widehat A_{2r}\\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}\\right|\n \\le M2^{-D}+\\sum_{2r\\le D}e_{2r}\\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}.\n\\end{aligned}$$ If the final sum of radii is at most $\\delta$ for each of the six pieces, their combined integral has error at most $M2^{-D}+\\delta$. Discrete atoms are not included in this estimate and are to be enclosed separately.",
          "source_id": "PM-090.3"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "Suppose $F(c_j+x)=\\sum_{k\\ge0}A_kx^k$ is analytic on a neighborhood of $|x|\\le R$ and $|F|\\le M$ there. Let $D\\ge0$ be an integer. For $0\\le k\\le D$, suppose $\\widehat A_k\\in\\mathbb Q(i)$ and $e_k\\in\\mathbb Q_{\\ge0}$ satisfy $|A_k-\\widehat A_k|\\le e_k$. Then $$\\begin{aligned}\n\\label{ind:taylor-average-error}\n \\left|6\\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_j}F(t)\\,\\mathop{}\\!\\mathrm dt-\n       \\sum_{2r\\le D}\\widehat A_{2r}\\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}\\right|\n \\le M2^{-D}+\\sum_{2r\\le D}e_{2r}\\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}.\n\\end{aligned}$$ If the final sum of radii is at most $\\delta$ for each of the six pieces, their combined integral has error at most $M2^{-D}+\\delta$. Discrete atoms are not included in this estimate and are to be enclosed separately.",
      "explanation": "The average of an analytic function over a piece is enclosed by its even Taylor coefficients about the midpoint plus a geometric tail and the weighted coefficient radii.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ind-taylor-budgets",
        "exact_quote": "The following choices give sufficient rational error enclosures for the same finite quantities used in Appendix <a href=\"#sec:certificates\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sec:certificates\">6</a>.*\n\n*For a basis input at $n\\in J=\\{1,3,4\\}$, a row $m\\in J$, $y=1$, and $\\ell=0,1$, take $D=63$, $h_D=32$, $M=10^6$, and $\\delta=10^{-14}$ in <a href=\"#ind:coefficient-budget\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ind:coefficient-budget\">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>. There are no atoms. If $Q_m,D_m$ have rational errors at most $10^{-25}$, either entry of <a href=\"#cert:pair\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"cert:pair\">[cert:pair]</a> has error less than $2.13\\cdot10^{-13}<10^{-12}$.*\n\n*For the full rational finite-input domain at the start of this subsection, take $D=255$, $h_D=128$, $M=10^{53}$, and $\\delta=4\\cdot10^{-15}$ in <a href=\"#ind:coefficient-budget\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ind:coefficient-budget\">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>, and require each of the seven atom-product radii in <a href=\"#ind:atom-radii\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ind:atom-radii\">[ind:atom-radii]</a> to be at most $4\\cdot10^{-15}/7$. Each $U$ and $X$ moment then has error less than $10^{-14}$. For node rows $m\\in I_f$, with rational $Q_m,D_m$ errors at most $10^{-25}$, either entry of <a href=\"#cert:pair\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"cert:pair\">[cert:pair]</a> has error less than $1.8\\cdot10^{-14}$.",
        "source_id": "PM-090.3"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-ind-taylor-budgets",
          "exact_quote": "The following choices give sufficient rational error enclosures for the same finite quantities used in Appendix <a href=\"#sec:certificates\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sec:certificates\">6</a>.*\n\n*For a basis input at $n\\in J=\\{1,3,4\\}$, a row $m\\in J$, $y=1$, and $\\ell=0,1$, take $D=63$, $h_D=32$, $M=10^6$, and $\\delta=10^{-14}$ in <a href=\"#ind:coefficient-budget\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ind:coefficient-budget\">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>. There are no atoms. If $Q_m,D_m$ have rational errors at most $10^{-25}$, either entry of <a href=\"#cert:pair\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"cert:pair\">[cert:pair]</a> has error less than $2.13\\cdot10^{-13}<10^{-12}$.*\n\n*For the full rational finite-input domain at the start of this subsection, take $D=255$, $h_D=128$, $M=10^{53}$, and $\\delta=4\\cdot10^{-15}$ in <a href=\"#ind:coefficient-budget\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ind:coefficient-budget\">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>, and require each of the seven atom-product radii in <a href=\"#ind:atom-radii\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ind:atom-radii\">[ind:atom-radii]</a> to be at most $4\\cdot10^{-15}/7$. Each $U$ and $X$ moment then has error less than $10^{-14}$.           "source_id": "PM-090.3"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "The following choices give sufficient rational error enclosures for the same finite quantities used in Appendix <a href=\"#sec:certificates\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"sec:certificates\">6</a>.*\n\n*For a basis input at $n\\in J=\\{1,3,4\\}$, a row $m\\in J$, $y=1$, and $\\ell=0,1$, take $D=63$, $h_D=32$, $M=10^6$, and $\\delta=10^{-14}$ in <a href=\"#ind:coefficient-budget\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ind:coefficient-budget\">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>. If $Q_m,D_m$ have rational errors at most $10^{-25}$, either entry of <a href=\"#cert:pair\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"cert:pair\">[cert:pair]</a> has error less than $2.13\\cdot10^{-13}<10^{-12}$.*\n\n*For the full rational finite-input domain at the start of this subsection, take $D=255$, $h_D=128$, $M=10^{53}$, and $\\delta=4\\cdot10^{-15}$ in <a href=\"#ind:coefficient-budget\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ind:coefficient-budget\">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>, and require each of the seven atom-product radii in <a href=\"#ind:atom-radii\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"ind:atom-radii\">[ind:atom-radii]</a> to be at most $4\\cdot10^{-15}/7$.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-ind-node-taylor-proposition",
        "exact_quote": "Let $p=PR$ have a real $\\ell^1$ coefficient list as in <a href=\"#fourier:list-norm\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"fourier:list-norm\">[fourier:list-norm]</a>. Define $$\\label{ind:node-P-coefficients}\n A_k(m)=\\frac{P^{(k)}(m)}{k!}\n =\\frac1{k!}\\sum_{v=-6}^6P_v(i\\pi t_v)^k e^{i\\pi t_vm}.$$ Then $A_0(m)=A_1(m)=0$. The regular series has coefficients $$\\label{ind:node-regular-coefficients}\n r_j(m)=C\\mathbf1_{\\{j=0\\}}+\n (-1)^j\\sum_{\\substack{n\\in\\mathcal N_0\\\\n\\ne m}}\n \\left(\\frac{(j+1)c_n}{(m-n)^{j+2}}\n       +\\frac{d_n}{(m-n)^{j+1}}\\right),$$ and converges for $|u|<\\rho_m$. For $0<|u|<\\rho_m$, the Laurent expansion is $$R(m+u)=\\frac{c_m}{u^2}+\\frac{d_m}{u}\n       +\\sum_{j=0}^{\\infty}r_j(m)u^j.$$ For every integer $\\ell\\ge0$ the direct derivative is therefore $$\\label{ind:node-derivatives}\n \\frac{p^{(\\ell)}(m)}{\\ell!}\n =c_mA_{\\ell+2}(m)+d_mA_{\\ell+1}(m)\n       +\\sum_{k=2}^{\\ell}A_k(m)r_{\\ell-k}(m),$$ where the last sum is empty for $\\ell=0,1$.",
        "source_id": "PM-090.3"
      },

The regular series has coefficients $$\\label{ind:node-regular-coefficients}\n r_j(m)=C\\mathbf1_{\\{j=0\\}}+\n (-1)^j\\sum_{\\substack{n\\in\\mathcal N_0\\\\n\\ne m}}\n \\left(\\frac{(j+1)c_n}{(m-n)^{j+2}}\n       +\\frac{d_n}{(m-n)^{j+1}}\\right),$$ and converges for $|u|<\\rho_m$. For $0<|u|<\\rho_m$, the Laurent expansion is $$R(m+u)=\\frac{c_m}{u^2}+\\frac{d_m}{u}\n       +\\sum_{j=0}^{\\infty}r_j(m)u^j.$$ For every integer $\\ell\\ge0$ the direct derivative is therefore $$\\label{ind:node-derivatives}\n \\frac{p^{(\\ell)}(m)}{\\ell!}\n =c_mA_{\\ell+2}(m)+d_mA_{\\ell+1}(m)\n       +\\sum_{k=2}^{\\ell}A_k(m)r_{\\ell-k}(m),$$ where the last sum is empty for $\\ell=0,1$.",

For $0<|u|<\\rho_m$, the Laurent expansion is $$R(m+u)=\\frac{c_m}{u^2}+\\frac{d_m}{u}\n       +\\sum_{j=0}^{\\infty}r_j(m)u^j.$$ For every integer $\\ell\\ge0$ the direct derivative is therefore $$\\label{ind:node-derivatives}\n \\frac{p^{(\\ell)}(m)}{\\ell!}\n =c_mA_{\\ell+2}(m)+d_mA_{\\ell+1}(m)\n       +\\sum_{k=2}^{\\ell}A_k(m)r_{\\ell-k}(m),$$ where the last sum is empty for $\\ell=0,1$.",

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **upper covered-area density** ([source](#sec:introduction)): If $B_R$ is the disk of radius $R$ centered at the origin, we use the upper covered-area density $$\overline\delta(\mathcal C) =\limsup_{R\to\infty} \frac{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\bigl(B_R\cap\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal C}B(c,1/2)\bigr)} {\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}B_R}.$$
- **Fourier transform convention** ([source](#sec:introduction)): Throughout, the Fourier transform is $$\widehat f(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R^2}f(x)e^{-2\pi i x\cdot\xi}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx.$$
- **Schwartz function** ([source](#sec:introduction)): A Schwartz function is a smooth function whose derivatives decay faster than every inverse power of the radius; this regularity is stronger than the admissibility conditions in the Cohn–Elkies theorem.
- **periodic node set** ([source](#sec:fourier)): We impose interpolation conditions at the positive nodes of a periodic set: $$\label{fourier:nodes} A=\{0,1,3,4,7,9\},\qquad \mathcal N=(12\mathbb Z+A)\cap(0,\infty).$$
- **vanishing factor P** ([source](#fourier:polynomial)): For $-6\le j\le6$, put $t_j=j/6$, and define $$\label{fourier:polynomial} P(s)=\prod_{a\in A}\left(2\sin\frac{\pi(s-a)}{12}\right)^2 =\sum_{j=-6}^{6}P_j e^{i\pi t_js}.$$
- **node jet constants Q_n and D_n** ([source](#fourier:node-expansion)): Define $Q_n,D_n$ by $$\label{fourier:node-expansion} P(n+u)=Q_nu^2\bigl(1+D_nu+O(u^2)\bigr).$$
- **coefficient space X** ([source](#sec:interpolation)): Let $$X=\ell^1(\mathcal N;\mathbb R^2),\qquad \left\lVert x\right\rVert_1=\sum_{n\in\mathcal N}\bigl(\left\lvert c_n\right\rvert+\left\lvert d_n\right\rvert\bigr) \quad\text{for }x=((c_n,d_n))_{n\in\mathcal N}.$$
- **half-gap** ([source](#pm-h-14)): Thus $y$ is signed, and the associated half-gap is $$I_{m,y}=\{m+yv:0\le v\le1\}.$$
- **rounded exponential** ([source](#cert:estar)): Define an approximation $e_*(u)$ to $e^{i\pi u}$ by the following finite recursion: $$\label{cert:estar} v_0=\left[\sum_{q=0}^{32}\frac{(ip_*u/1024)^q}{q!}\right]_{40}, \qquad v_{a+1}=[v_a^2]_{40}\ (0\le a<10),\qquad e_*(u)=v_{10}.$$
- **forty-digit floor** ([source](#cert:estar)): Define $$[w]_{40}=10^{-40}\lfloor10^{40}w\rfloor$$ for real $w$, and apply it componentwise to complex numbers.

| q-scale-b | lattice scaling constant b | = | $\frac{\sqrt3}{2}$ | fixed constant |

| q-node-q-lower | node constant Q_n | > | $\frac74$ | uniform in the node |
| q-node-d-upper | absolute value of D_n | < | $\frac{53}{25}$ | uniform in the node |
| q-damping-imaginary | imaginary part of the frequency curve | ≥ | $\frac{26}{435}$ | uniform on the whole interval |
| q-tail-norm-all | operator norm of all positive output rows | < | $28$ | all input columns |
| q-tail-norm-100 | operator norm of output rows beyond the last finite node | < | $5\cdot10^{-10}$ | all input columns |
| q-finite-inverse-norm | norm of the inverse of the small block | < | $32$ | both signs of the shift |
| q-inverse-weight-finite | inverse weight on finite-block residual | ≤ | $91$ | residual supported on the finite block |

| q-bernstein-origin | Bernstein coefficient threshold at the origin node | > | $0.74$ | center node zero |
| q-bernstein-far | Bernstein coefficient threshold at far nodes | > | $0.009$ | center nodes beyond four up to the finite cutoff |
| q-p-lower-constant | quadratic lower bound constant for the vanishing factor | ≥ | $0.68$ | nearest zero in the periodic set |
| q-tail-margin | quadratic margin on the unbounded region | ≥ | $0.00448$ | both functions |
| q-origin-value | common origin value of the pair | = | $6Q_1e^{-\pi h}$ | exact coefficient lists |
| q-radial-slope | radial derivative at the exclusion radius | = | $-\frac{2}{3\sqrt3}$ | radius one |
| q-quadrature-nodes | number of quadrature nodes per piece | = | $256$ | primary interval calculation |
| q-quadrature-error | quadrature moment error | < | $10^{-22}$ | direct and transformed moments |
| q-rounded-exponential-error | rounded exponential error | < | $3.542\cdot10^{-34}$ | bounded modulus |
| q-rational-moment-error | rational moment error | < | $10^{-14}$ | direct and transformed moments |
| q-first-block-entry-error | first-block entry error of the independent rational program | < | $10^{-12}$ | basis inputs and output nodes in the block |

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **step-measure-representation** [claim](#stmt-fourier-measure-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fourier-measure-lemma): Write each pole term as an integral of exponentials via division identities, sum with total variation control, and conclude entireness from uniform bounds. -> entire function represented by a finite complex measure
- **step-gaussian-pairing** [claim](#stmt-fourier-transform) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fourier-transform): Insert the measure representation, transform each Gaussian explicitly, and use the positive imaginary part of the frequency curve for decay. -> Gaussian pairing and exponential decay of the kernel
- **step-coefficient-form** [claim](#stmt-fourier-equivalence) / [proof](#proof-stmt-fourier-equivalence): Use node values and derivatives of the cardinal function to rewrite the interpolation conditions as coefficient equations. -> equivalent coefficient equations
- **step-finite-certificates** [claim](#stmt-interpolation-finite) / [proof](#proof-stmt-interpolation-finite): Cite the primary interval evaluation of matrix, residual and list-norm bounds, supported by the quadrature remainder lemma. -> finite block inverse and residual bounds
- **step-inverse-whole-space** [claim](#stmt-interpolation-inverse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-interpolation-inverse): Eliminate the small block, then the middle block, then the tail by Schur complements, each inverted by a Neumann series. -> bounded inverse with explicit weights
- **step-exact-solution** [claim](#stmt-interpolation-solution) / [proof](#proof-stmt-interpolation-solution): Sum and difference of the coupled equations reduce to the two shifted operators; residuals of the tabulated lists are fed into the inverse bound. -> unique exact lists within a small distance of the table
- **step-bernstein** [claim](#stmt-signs-bernstein) / [proof](#proof-stmt-signs-bernstein): Enclose the derivatives of the tabulated functions by the primary interval calculation and read off the grouped thresholds. -> positive Bernstein coefficients on every half-gap
- **step-p-lower-bound** [claim](#stmt-signs-p-lower-bound) / [proof](#proof-stmt-signs-p-lower-bound): Show logarithmic concavity of the quotient on each half-gap and evaluate midpoint values by trigonometric identities. -> quadratic lower bound for the vanishing factor
- **step-global-signs** [claim](#stmt-signs-global) / [proof](#proof-stmt-signs-global): Combine the finite-region quotient bounds from Bernstein coefficients with the tail margin from the rational part, the second-derivative estimate and the vanishing-factor bound. -> zero sets, orders and strict signs
- **step-origin-value** [claim](#stmt-normalization-origin-lemma) / [proof](#proof-stmt-normalization-origin-lemma): Differentiate the lattice Poisson dilation identity at unit dilation, where only the first shell survives. -> common positive origin value
- **step-main-theorem** [claim](#stmt-intro-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-normalization-origin-lemma-2): Dilate and normalize the exact pair and transfer the global signs to the four required conditions. -> sharp Fourier certificate
- **step-periodic-uniqueness** [claim](#stmt-normalization-periodic-uniqueness) / [proof](#proof-stmt-normalization-periodic-uniqueness): Equality forces vanishing defect, hence integral squared distances; scale to an even integral lattice and classify it by Gram determinant. -> triangular lattice up to isometry
- **step-second-poisson** [claim](#stmt-normalization-second-poisson) / [proof](#proof-stmt-normalization-second-poisson): Differentiate the dilation identity a second time and use the signs of the second derivatives at nodes. -> curvature identity and inequality
- **step-quadrature-error** [claim](#stmt-cert-moment-error) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cert-moment-error): Continue the integrands analytically to disks avoiding the pole, bound them uniformly and apply a Cauchy tail estimate to an exact positive quadrature rule. -> uniform moment error bound
- **step-rounded-exponential** [claim](#stmt-cert-rounded-exponential) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cert-rounded-exponential): Bound the series remainder and floor error and iterate the squaring error recurrence exactly. -> error bound for the rounded exponential
- **step-rational-moment-error** [claim](#stmt-cert-rational-moment-error) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cert-rational-moment-error): Check the call hypotheses of the rounded exponential, then propagate errors through nodes, weights, densities, kernels and atoms. -> rational moment and node error bounds
- **step-rational-first-block** [claim](#stmt-cert-rational-small-error) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cert-rational-small-error): Separate quadrature error from rounding error for the smaller rule on the first block and combine the pair stability estimate. -> first-block entry error
- **step-taylor-average** [claim](#stmt-ind-taylor-average) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ind-taylor-average): Average even powers exactly and bound the Taylor tail by Cauchy estimates. -> enclosure of piece averages
- **step-taylor-budgets** [claim](#stmt-ind-taylor-budgets) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ind-taylor-budgets): Insert the budgets into the Taylor average lemma and the pair stability estimate. -> sufficient rational error enclosures
- **step-node-taylor** [claim](#stmt-ind-node-taylor-proposition) / [proof](#proof-stmt-ind-node-taylor-proposition): Expand each pole term geometrically, justify summation by absolute convergence inside the nearest-node radius, and multiply with the Taylor series of the vanishing factor. -> closed formulas for derivatives at nodes

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **inv-node-set-covers-shells**: Every positive value of the triangular quadratic form j^2+jk+k^2 lies in the periodic node set, so its residues modulo 12 are confined to the six allowed classes.
- **inv-node-set-strict**: The node set is strictly larger than the set of lattice shell values: the value 15 is a node but is never attained by the quadratic form.
- **inv-first-shell-six**: Exactly six lattice vectors lie on the first shell s_a=1.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **anti-finite-window-proof**: Treat the finite-window residue check as a proof that the node set contains all shell values for arbitrary integer pairs. -> A bounded window only samples finitely many pairs; the general statement rests on the congruence argument, not on enumeration.
- **anti-nodes-equal-shells**: Assume every node of the interpolation set is a lattice shell coordinate. -> The certificate then appears to vanish only on lattice shells, but it also vanishes at extra nodes such as 15.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The paper contains a finite elementary construction: the residue classes of the triangular quadratic form, the strictness of the node-set inclusion and the six first-shell vectors can be recomputed by finite enumeration. The infinite interpolation, operator inversion and global sign statements are not finite and are not covered.

```python
# INPUTS: one integer M >= 0 on stdin, the half-width of the window of integer pairs (j,k).
# PRECONDITIONS: M is a non-negative integer.
# OUTPUTS: three lines: sorted residues modulo 12 of j^2+jk+k^2 over the window, the number of pairs with value 1, and 1 if value 15 occurs else 0.
# TIME: O(M^2) pair evaluations.
# SPACE: O(1) beyond a set of at most twelve residues.
# ARITHMETIC: exact integer arithmetic.
# FAILURE: malformed input raises an exception and the run is rejected.
import sys

def main():
    M = int(sys.stdin.read().split()[0])
    res = set()
    ones = 0
    hit15 = 0
    for j in range(-M, M + 1):
        for k in range(-M, M + 1):
            s = j * j + j * k + k * k
            res.add(s % 12)
            if s == 1:
                ones += 1
            if s == 15:
                hit15 = 1
    print(",".join(str(r) for r in sorted(res)))
    print(ones)
    print(hit15)

main()
```

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper gives no software artefact; its finite evidence is described only through the formulas of an appendix. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Nothing in the paper concerns simulation, game physics or real-time geometry. The disk-packing statements are theorems about densities. |
| tools | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No stand-alone tool is specified. The interval enclosure method is cited from the literature, and its local environment is explicitly not certified by that citation. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The appendix fixes a finite check: from the power coefficients of a half-gap, build every Bernstein coefficient with an explicit recurrence and compare each one with a rational threshold. This is a bounded-input validation routine rather than a general solver, so it can be adapted to similar sign certificates. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The paper introduces no data structure; the coefficient lists and node sets are mathematical sequences used inside proofs. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No hardware mapping or parallel schedule is discussed; the quadrature sums are described only as finite interval computations. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The residues modulo twelve and the lattice structure are used for interpolation nodes and packing, not for any hardness assumption or protocol. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | There is no learning, fitting or training step. The coefficient lists are the unique solution of a linear operator equation on a sequence space. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The Cohn–Elkies linear programming bound is the motivating setting, but the paper proves that a single certificate exists rather than giving a solver or a search procedure for it. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | No concurrency, communication or replication appears anywhere in the paper. |
| other | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The remaining uses are purely mathematical: a sharp certificate for the planar packing bound and the periodic equality case. It does not extend to other dimensions, to non-periodic equality cases, or to the extra zero-set requirement of a further conjecture of the same authors. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-coefficient-space-uniqueness** (formal_scope): Uniqueness of the interpolation coefficients is asserted only inside the chosen unweighted summable coefficient space, with the fixed zero-node data and constants. It says nothing about other radial Schwartz functions with the same shell data. Blocked use: ['Concluding that the certificate is the only radial Schwartz function with these zeros', 'Reading the uniqueness as a statement about all functions with double zeros on the lattice shells']
- **limit-periodic-equality-only** (hypothesis): The identification of equality cases with the triangular lattice is proved only for periodic packings, where the lattice index argument is available. Blocked use: ['Claiming every densest disk packing, periodic or not, is a triangular lattice', 'Applying the lattice classification to aperiodic or random packings']
- **limit-node-set-strict-superset** (edge_case): The periodic node set is strictly larger than the set of lattice shell values, so the certificate vanishes at some nodes that are not lattice radii. Blocked use: ['Presenting the certificate as vanishing exactly on the triangular lattice shells', 'Using it to satisfy a requirement that zeros lie only on lattice shells']
- **limit-quadrature-domain** (hypothesis): The uniform quadrature error bound holds only for finite coefficient lists with bounded norm and constant, and for the stated ranges of the node and half-gap parameters and derivative order. Blocked use: ['Applying the moment error bound to an arbitrary summable list of unbounded norm', 'Using it for parameters outside the stated ranges of node, half-gap length or derivative order']
- **limit-rounded-exponential-domain** (hypothesis): The rounded exponential error bound requires a rational argument in the stated left half-plane region with bounded modulus; the recursion has no guarantee elsewhere. Blocked use: ['Evaluating the recursion at arguments with positive growth exponent and expecting the same error', 'Using it for arguments of larger modulus than the stated bound']
- **limit-vanishing-factor-nearest-zero** (hypothesis): The quadratic lower bound for the vanishing factor is stated relative to a nearest zero of the factor; it does not bound the factor by the distance to an arbitrary node. Blocked use: ['Using the bound with a zero that is not nearest to the evaluation point', 'Using it as a lower bound for the full cardinal function rather than the vanishing factor alone']
- **limit-finite-block-certificate** (formal_scope): The finite block invertibility and residual bounds rest on a primary interval calculation described in the appendix; the text asserts the outcome and does not reproduce each enclosure by hand. Blocked use: ['Treating the finite block bounds as hand-checkable without rerunning the interval computation', 'Assuming the same bounds for other node sets or other tabulated lists']
- **limit-taylor-budgets-conditional** (approximation): The Taylor budgets give sufficient rational error enclosures for stated finite quantities only; they are margins for a refinement method and do not assert that the matrix, residual or Bernstein values have been evaluated. Blocked use: ['Citing the budgets as evidence that the certificate values were computed by the Taylor method', 'Applying the budgets to inputs outside the stated finite domains']
- **limit-node-taylor-list-class** (hypothesis): The local node expansion is an exact convergence statement for the direct cardinal function only; computable truncation of its node sum needs a separate tail bound, and the transformed kernel is not covered. Blocked use: ['Using the expansion to represent the transformed function', 'Truncating the node sum with a guaranteed error without a tail estimate for the list']

### Formal statement (Lean 4)

Corpus status: formalized in the source corpus (toolchain `leanprover/lean4:v4.34.1`, Mathlib `d13f23b723b8a846827a245b89c10fc7d3f11612`). Not re-checked here; the statements give the exact quantifiers and hypotheses, not a proof.

Scope note of family 090 (`lean/docs/090.md`, verbatim):

> The following describes the scope of the Lean formalization related to the following accompanying paper(s):
>
> - [An atomic certificate for triangular-lattice universal optimality](../../preprints/An-atomic-certificate-for-triangular-lattice-universal-optimality-September-26-2026/paper.pdf)
> - [A sharp Fourier certificate for planar circle packing](../../preprints/A-sharp-Fourier-certificate-for-planar-circle-packing-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> The formalization proves that the density-one triangular lattice minimizes lower energy per particle among all locally finite planar configurations of centered-disk density one, for every nonnegative completely monotone function of squared distance. Infinite energies are allowed in the comparison.
>
> It also constructs sharp radial Schwartz minorants for every Gaussian potential: each minorant lies below the Gaussian, has nonnegative real Fourier transform, agrees with the Gaussian at nonzero triangular-lattice points, and vanishes on nonzero dual-lattice points after Fourier transformation. For the stated parameter range, the formalization includes the explicit atomic interpolation construction.
>
> The planar Cohn–Elkies sharpness conjecture asks whether the two-point Fourier method attains the optimal circle-packing density. The formalization constructs a radial Schwartz function $f$ on $\mathbb R^2$ with $\widehat f(0)=1$, $f(0)=2/\sqrt3$, $\widehat f$ real and nonnegative everywhere, and $f(x)\le0$ whenever $\|x\|\ge1$. These are the sharp Fourier certificate conditions yielding density $\pi/(2\sqrt3)$. The separate uniqueness statement for periodic equality cases is outside this selected theorem.

Comparator source `PlanarPacking.lean` (1196 bytes, sha256 `6f2ffada083cfcceddcfbcfc01409e046d7fdb729994443d285cfd9552c1c202`), verbatim:

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

/-!
# A sharp Fourier certificate for planar circle packing

The normalization and Fourier convention are those of
*A sharp Fourier certificate for planar circle packing* (OpenAI, 2026).
-/

open MeasureTheory

namespace SharpPlanar

abbrev Plane := EuclideanSpace ℝ (Fin 2)

/-- Radiality of a real function on the Euclidean plane. -/
def Radial (f : Plane → ℝ) : Prop :=
  ∀ x y, ‖x‖ = ‖y‖ → f x = f y

/-- Fourier transformation with kernel `exp (-2πi⟪x, ξ⟫)`. -/
noncomputable def fourier (f : Plane → ℝ) (ξ : Plane) : ℂ :=
  ∫ x : Plane, (f x : ℂ) *
    Complex.exp (((-2 * Real.pi * inner ℝ x ξ : ℝ) : ℂ) * Complex.I)

/-- A normalized sharp certificate for disks of radius one half. -/
def SharpCertificate (f : SchwartzMap Plane ℝ) : Prop :=
  Radial f ∧ fourier f 0 = 1 ∧ f 0 = 2 / Real.sqrt 3 ∧
    (∀ ξ, (fourier f ξ).im = 0 ∧ 0 ≤ (fourier f ξ).re) ∧
    (∀ x, 1 ≤ ‖x‖ → f x ≤ 0)

end SharpPlanar

/-- The sharp Fourier certificate theorem. -/
theorem sharp_fourier_certificate :
    ∃ f : SchwartzMap SharpPlanar.Plane ℝ, SharpPlanar.SharpCertificate f := by
  sorry

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We resolve the planar Cohn–Elkies sharpness conjecture: the two-point Fourier bound attains the optimal circle-packing density $`\pi/(2\sqrt3)`$. We construct a radial Schwartz certificate and prove its global sign conditions using rigorous interval arithmetic and analytic estimates. The same certificate recovers the classical uniqueness of the triangular packing among periodic equality cases.

<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The planar circle-packing problem asks what fraction of the plane can be occupied by congruent disks with disjoint interiors. Scale their radius to $1/2$, so their centers form a set $\mathcal C\subset\mathbb R^2$ with $|x-y|\ge1$ for distinct centers. If $B_R$ is the disk of radius $R$ centered at the origin, we use the upper covered-area density $$\overline\delta(\mathcal C)
 =\limsup_{R\to\infty}
 \frac{\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}\bigl(B_R\cap\bigcup_{c\in\mathcal C}B(c,1/2)\bigr)}
      {\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}B_R}.$$ The triangular lattice attains $\pi/(2\sqrt3)$, and Thue’s theorem gives the matching upper bound. Hales presents an elementary geometric proof based on an idea of Rogers (Hales 2000, 442–43) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Hales2000](#ref-Hales2000)].

The Fourier-analytic approach of Cohn and Elkies (Cohn and Elkies 2003) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[CohnElkies2003](#ref-CohnElkies2003)] asks whether the same optimum can be certified by one function with compatible signs before and after Fourier transformation. The density theorem alone does not supply such a function. Our result is a sharp Fourier certificate for the classical optimum.

Throughout, the Fourier transform is $$\widehat f(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb R^2}f(x)e^{-2\pi i x\cdot\xi}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dx.$$ For real radial integrable functions it is real and radial. A Schwartz function is a smooth function whose derivatives decay faster than every inverse power of the radius; this regularity is stronger than the admissibility conditions in the Cohn–Elkies theorem.

<a id="stmt-intro-main"></a>
<div id="intro:main" class="theorem">

<a id="intro:conditions"></a>
**Theorem 1** (Sharp Fourier certificate). *There is a real radial Schwartz function $f:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb R$ such that $$\label{intro:conditions}
  \widehat f(0)=1,\qquad f(0)=\frac{2}{\sqrt3},\qquad
  \widehat f(\xi)\ge0\quad(\xi\in\mathbb R^2),\qquad
  f(x)\le0\quad(|x|\ge1).$$*

</div>

Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a> gives an affirmative answer to the two-dimensional case of Cohn and Elkies’s Conjecture 7.3 (Cohn and Elkies 2003) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[CohnElkies2003](#ref-CohnElkies2003)], concerning sharp auxiliary functions for their linear programming bound. Their Definition 2.2 and Theorem 3.1 apply to this Schwartz function and to arbitrary packings, not only periodic ones. Their opposite Fourier phase gives the same transform for these even functions. For disks of radius $1/2$ they give $$\mathop{\mathrm{vol}}B(0,1/2)\,\frac{f(0)}{\widehat f(0)}
  =\frac{\pi}{4}\frac{2}{\sqrt3}
  =\frac{\pi}{2\sqrt3}.$$ This is the density of the lattice generated by $(1,0)$ and $(1/2,\sqrt3/2)$. Thus the theorem identifies an extremizer for this analytic bound. To put it in the equal-origin formulation of Cohn and Elkies’s Theorem 3.2, which their Conjecture 7.3 invokes, set $$g(x)=\frac{\sqrt3}{2}\,
       f\bigl((\sqrt3/2)^{1/2}x\bigr).$$ The two-dimensional scaling rule gives $g(0)=\widehat g(0)=1$, $\widehat g\ge0$, and $g(x)\le0$ for $|x|\ge(2/\sqrt3)^{1/2}$, exactly the requested planar witness. The sign estimates also determine the zeros in their required domains. For $f$, every zero with $|x|\ge1$ has integral squared radius. This restriction recovers a classical equality consequence: among periodic packings, equality in the density bound forces the triangular lattice (Corollary <a href="#normalization:periodic-uniqueness" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="normalization:periodic-uniqueness">14</a>). The equal-origin witness $g$ has zeros outside the shells of the triangular lattice of covolume one, so it does not meet the additional zero-set requirement of Cohn and Elkies’s Conjecture 8.1. Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a> gives an explicit extra node, and Section <a href="#sec:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:normalization">5</a> records the zero sets in their respective sign domains and final radial coordinates.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Fourier certificates and interpolation

Cohn and Elkies formulated their sharpness conjecture in dimensions $2$, $8$, and $24$ (Cohn and Elkies 2003, Conjecture 7.3) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[CohnElkies2003](#ref-CohnElkies2003)]. Their finite Laguerre–Gaussian approximations already combine prescribed double roots, numerical searches for the remaining parameters, and rigorous sign checks by rational arithmetic and Sturm root counts (Cohn and Elkies 2003, sec. 7, pp. 704–705) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[CohnElkies2003](#ref-CohnElkies2003)]. Exact attainment of the conjectured sharp bound remained unresolved in that approach. Viazovska constructed the required function in dimension $8$ (Viazovska 2017, Theorems 3 and 4) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Viazovska2017](#ref-Viazovska2017)], and Cohn, Kumar, Miller, Radchenko, and Viazovska constructed its dimension-$24$ counterpart (Cohn et al. 2017, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[CKMRV2017](#ref-CKMRV2017)]. Their constructions use modular forms to control a function and its Fourier transform simultaneously. Cohn and Elkies report that Gorbachev independently obtained essentially the same linear programming bound (Cohn and Elkies 2003, 694) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[CohnElkies2003](#ref-CohnElkies2003)].

Interpolation gives a broader setting for these constructions. Radchenko and Viazovska recovered even Schwartz functions on the real line from their values and Fourier values at the square roots of nonnegative integers (Radchenko and Viazovska 2019, Theorem 1) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[RadchenkoViazovska2019](#ref-RadchenkoViazovska2019)]. Cohn, Kumar, Miller, Radchenko, and Viazovska developed interpolation using values and first derivatives in dimensions $8$ and $24$ and used it to prove universal optimality of the $E_8$ and Leech lattices (Cohn et al. 2022, Theorems 1.4, 1.7, and 1.9) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[CKMRV2022](#ref-CKMRV2022)].

The planar shell data have a different feature. Cohn, Kumar, Miller, Radchenko, and Viazovska conjectured that values and first derivatives of a radial Schwartz function and its Fourier transform on the nonzero shells of the triangular lattice normalized to covolume one do not determine the function uniquely (Cohn et al. 2022, Conjecture 7.5) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[CKMRV2022](#ref-CKMRV2022)]. Sardari proved this nonuniqueness by constructing infinitely many linearly independent radial Schwartz functions with double zeros, before and after Fourier transformation, on those shells (Talebizadeh Sardari 2021, Theorem 1.7) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[Sardari2021](#ref-Sardari2021)]. Thus prescribing the shell data alone does not single out a planar certificate. We solve a specified interpolation problem within an explicit family of Gaussian-damped cardinal functions and establish the signs separately.

A 2026 bachelor’s thesis abstract by Zhitniaia (Zhitniaia 2026) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[Zhitniaia2026](#ref-Zhitniaia2026)] announces an integral representation of a magic function for the hexagonal lattice using weakly holomorphic modular forms and theta functions for $\Gamma_0(12)$. Our comparison is limited to the institutional abstract, which does not specify the complete sign, normalization, and regularity statement. The precise relation of that claim to Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a> remains to be determined from the full thesis.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The construction

For a sharp Fourier bound on a lattice, Poisson summation forces the auxiliary function and its Fourier transform to vanish on the nonzero shells of the lattice and its dual, respectively (Cohn and Elkies 2003, sec. 5) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[CohnElkies2003](#ref-CohnElkies2003)]. After normalizing the triangular lattice to covolume one, it is isometric to its dual, so the two sets of shell radii coincide. This normalization makes the coupled Fourier equations symmetric. At the end we dilate the resulting function to put the first physical-space shell at the exclusion radius one in Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a>. We cover their squared radii, in a fixed scaled coordinate, by a periodic set of interpolation nodes. Periodic supersets of the values of the triangular quadratic form also occur in Sardari’s construction (Talebizadeh Sardari 2021, sec. 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Sardari2021](#ref-Sardari2021)]; here a fixed periodic set permits an explicit trigonometric cardinal family.

An interior zero of a differentiable function with a fixed sign must also have zero first derivative. We therefore impose zeros of order at least two on the Fourier side and at every physical-space shell beyond the first. The first physical-space shell is the boundary of the required sign region, so a simple zero is possible there; its prescribed negative derivative will fix the normalization.

A trigonometric polynomial vanishes doubly on the chosen set. Dividing it by linear and quadratic factors produces functions that prescribe values and first derivatives at individual nodes. This Hermite-cardinal pattern has a precedent in the Gaussian Beurling–Selberg constructions of Carneiro, Littmann, and Vaaler (Carneiro, Littmann, and Vaaler 2013, formulas (1.2)–(1.3)) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013](#ref-CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013)]; the Fourier coupling and sign estimates below are proved for the present family. Multiplication by a fixed Gaussian gives radial Schwartz functions. Their Fourier transforms admit integral representations with additional Gaussian decay. We use that decay to solve the coupled interpolation equations: a finite matrix handles the first nodes, and explicit estimates control the remaining infinite system. The inversion acts on the full unweighted $\ell^1$ coefficient space. Its uniqueness concerns the prescribed coefficient lists within this chosen family.

The resulting functions have exact zeros, but the coefficient lists used for computation are finite approximations. An explicit per-list $\ell^1$ error bound connects these approximations to the exact solution. Near a zero, a raw error divided by its vanishing power would be singular. We instead subtract the lower Taylor terms of the error before division; positive Bernstein coefficients then certify the signs on complete half-gaps. A separate sine-product barrier and second-derivative estimate control the entire unbounded region. Poisson summation first equates the two origin values. Differentiating its dilation identity then determines their common positive value from the prescribed first-shell derivative, so the final normalization is exact.

Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a> develops the cardinal functions and their Fourier transforms. Section <a href="#sec:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interpolation">3</a> solves the interpolation equations, and Section <a href="#sec:signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:signs">4</a> proves the global signs and identifies their zeros. Section <a href="#sec:normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:normalization">5</a> establishes the exact normalization, performs the final dilation, and derives the packing consequences. Appendix <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">6</a> gives the finite data and the primary interval certificate, including the analytic integration error that connects its finite sums to exact moments. Appendix <a href="#ind:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:section">7</a> gathers the rational alternatives and independent checks, with their precise mathematical and computational scopes.

<a id="sec:fourier"></a>
### Gaussian cardinal functions

The construction uses a family of entire functions whose values and first derivatives can be prescribed at a periodic set of nodes. Multiplication by a fixed Gaussian turns these functions into radial Schwartz functions. Their Fourier transforms have strictly stronger Gaussian decay, which will make the coupled interpolation equations tractable.

<a id="fourier:lattice"></a>
<a id="fourier:shell-coordinate"></a>
Set <a id="fourier:parameters"></a>$$\label{fourier:parameters}
 b=\frac{\sqrt3}{2},\qquad h=\frac25,\qquad B=b^{-2}=\frac43,
 \qquad s=b\left\lvert x\right\rvert^2.$$ The same coordinate, $s=b\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^2$, is used on the Fourier side. To see the lattice geometry behind this choice, let $\Lambda$ have basis $$\label{fourier:lattice}
 u=\frac{1}{\sqrt b}(1,0),\qquad
 v=\frac{1}{\sqrt b}(1/2,b).$$ This oriented basis has determinant one. If $\mathcal R$ is counterclockwise rotation through $90$ degrees, its dual basis is $-\mathcal R v,\mathcal R u$. Hence $\Lambda^*=\mathcal R\Lambda$: the lattice and its dual have the same shell radii. For $a=ju+kv$, with $j,k\in\mathbb Z$, the squared radius in the common coordinate is $$\label{fourier:shell-coordinate}
 s_a:=b\left\lvert a\right\rvert^2=j^2+jk+k^2.$$

We impose interpolation conditions at the positive nodes of a periodic set: <a id="fourier:nodes"></a>$$\label{fourier:nodes}
 A=\{0,1,3,4,7,9\},\qquad
 \mathcal N=(12\mathbb Z+A)\cap(0,\infty).$$ The set $\mathcal N$ contains every positive value of $j^2+jk+k^2$. Indeed, $j^2+jk+k^2\equiv(j-k)^2\pmod3$, so it is never $2\pmod3$. Modulo $4$, it is $0$ when $j,k$ are both even and is odd otherwise. Combining these restrictions gives precisely the six allowed residues in $A$. The inclusion is strict: $15\in\mathcal N$, but $j^2+jk+k^2=15$ would imply $(2j+k)^2\equiv2k^2\pmod5$. Since $2$ is not a square modulo $5$, this forces $5\mid k$ and then $5\mid j$, which would make $25$ divide $15$. Thus we require zeros at some nodes that are not lattice shell coordinates. This deliberate enlargement uses a periodic residue pattern with an elementary trigonometric vanishing function. Covering planar shell values by periodic sets also occurs in Sardari’s Fourier interpolation construction (Talebizadeh Sardari 2021, sec. 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Sardari2021](#ref-Sardari2021)]; the Gaussian transformation and the coupled inversion needed here will be proved directly.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### The cardinal family

The values and first derivatives will be encoded by double-pole and simple-pole terms multiplied by a common vanishing factor. This is the same cardinal pattern that occurs in Gaussian Beurling–Selberg interpolation (Carneiro, Littmann, and Vaaler 2013, formulas (1.2)–(1.3)) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013](#ref-CarneiroLittmannVaaler2013)]. The nodes and simultaneous Fourier constraints here are different.

<a id="fourier:polynomial"></a>
<a id="fourier:coefficients"></a>
For $-6\le j\le6$, put $t_j=j/6$, and define $$\label{fourier:polynomial}
 P(s)=\prod_{a\in A}\left(2\sin\frac{\pi(s-a)}{12}\right)^2
     =\sum_{j=-6}^{6}P_j e^{i\pi t_js}.$$ Here $P_{-j}=\overline{P_j}$, and $$\label{fourier:coefficients}
 (P_0,P_1,\ldots,P_6)
 =\left(5,-1+2ib,1+2ib,-2,-\frac12+ib,-1-2ib,1\right).$$ For example, with $w=e^{i\pi s/6}$ and $\rho=e^{i\pi/6}$, each factor is $$\left(2\sin\frac{\pi(s-a)}{12}\right)^2
 =-\rho^{-a}w^{-1}(w-\rho^a)^2.$$ The six minus signs cancel, and $\rho^{-\sum_{a\in A}a}=\rho^{-24}=1$. Thus the product in <a href="#fourier:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:polynomial">[fourier:polynomial]</a> equals $$w^{-6}\prod_{a\in A}(w-\rho^a)^2.$$ Expanding this Laurent polynomial gives <a href="#fourier:coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:coefficients">[fourier:coefficients]</a>.

<a id="fourier:node-expansion"></a>
<a id="fourier:node-data"></a>
<a id="fourier:node-bounds"></a>
Every zero $n\in12\mathbb Z+A$ of $P$ is double. Define $Q_n,D_n$ by $$\label{fourier:node-expansion}
 P(n+u)=Q_nu^2\bigl(1+D_nu+O(u^2)\bigr).$$ If $a\in A$ is the residue of $n$ modulo $12$, then $$\label{fourier:node-data}
 \begin{split}
 Q_n&=\left(\frac\pi6\right)^2
       \prod_{\substack{a'\in A\\a'\ne a}}
       \left(2\sin\frac{\pi(a-a')}{12}\right)^2,\\
 D_n&=\frac\pi6\sum_{\substack{a'\in A\\a'\ne a}}
             \cot\frac{\pi(a-a')}{12}.
 \end{split}$$ Thus $Q_n,D_n$ are periodic in $n$, and their exact values are $$\begin{array}{c|c|c}
 a&Q_n&D_n\\\hline
 0&\pi^2/3&-7\pi\sqrt3/18\\
 1&(2\pi^2/3)(2-\sqrt3)&\pi(3+\sqrt3)/18\\
 3&(2\pi^2/3)(2-\sqrt3)&-\pi(3+\sqrt3)/18\\
 4&\pi^2/3&7\pi\sqrt3/18\\
 7&(2\pi^2/3)(2+\sqrt3)&-\pi(3-\sqrt3)/18\\
 9&(2\pi^2/3)(2+\sqrt3)&\pi(3-\sqrt3)/18
\end{array}$$ The expansion follows by isolating the vanishing sine factor; logarithmic differentiation of the remaining factors gives $D_n$. The table then uses the elementary trigonometric values at multiples of $\pi/12$. In particular, $$\label{fourier:node-bounds}
 Q_n>\frac74=1.75,\qquad \left\lvert D_n\right\rvert<\frac{53}{25}=2.12.$$ To check these inequalities with rational bounds, use $157/50<\pi<22/7$ and $\sqrt3<26/15$. The minimum of $Q_n$ is greater than $197192/112500>7/4$, and the maximum of $\left\lvert D_n\right\rvert$ is less than $286/135<53/25$.

<a id="fourier:list-norm"></a>
<a id="fourier:cardinal"></a>
Let $C\in\mathbb R$, and let $(c_n,d_n)_{n\in\{0\}\cup\mathcal N}$ be a real coefficient list with $$\label{fourier:list-norm}
 \left\lVert(c,d)\right\rVert_1
 :=\sum_{n\in\{0\}\cup\mathcal N}(\left\lvert c_n\right\rvert+\left\lvert d_n\right\rvert)<\infty.$$ Away from the nodes, define $$\label{fourier:cardinal}
 p(s)=P(s)R(s),\qquad
 R(s)=C+\sum_{n\in\{0\}\cup\mathcal N}
       \left(\frac{c_n}{(s-n)^2}+\frac{d_n}{s-n}\right).$$ We next show that the apparent singularities are removable, with uniform control of the resulting entire function.

<a id="stmt-fourier-measure-lemma"></a>
<div id="fourier:measure-lemma" class="lemma">

<a id="fourier:measure"></a>
<a id="fourier:variation"></a>
<a id="fourier:folded"></a>
**Lemma 2** (Finite-measure representation). *The function in <a href="#fourier:cardinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:cardinal">[fourier:cardinal]</a> extends to an entire function with the representation $$\label{fourier:measure}
 p(s)=\int_{-1}^{1}e^{i\pi ts}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu(t),\qquad s\in\mathbb C,$$ where $\mu$ is a finite complex measure, supported in $[-1,1]$, with $\mu(-E)=\overline{\mu(E)}$ for Borel sets $E$. Its total variation satisfies $$\label{fourier:variation}
 \left\lVert\mu\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \le25\left\lvert C\right\rvert+\frac{65\pi^2}{18}\sum_n\left\lvert c_n\right\rvert
                 +\frac{32\pi}{3}\sum_n\left\lvert d_n\right\rvert.$$ The atoms of $\mu$ have mass $CP_j$ at $t_j$, for $-6\le j\le6$. For real-axis evaluations, the remaining measure can be folded onto $[0,1]$: on $t_{j-1}<t<t_j$, its folded density is $\sum_n(c_nu_n(t)+d_nv_n(t))$, where $$\label{fourier:folded}
 \begin{split}
 u_n(t)&=-2\pi^2e^{-i\pi tn}
              \sum_{l=j}^{6}(t_l-t)P_l e^{i\pi t_ln},\\
 v_n(t)&=2i\pi e^{-i\pi tn}
              \sum_{l=j}^{6}P_l e^{i\pi t_ln}.
 \end{split}$$ The folded atoms have masses $CP_0$ at $0$ and $2CP_j$ at $t_j$, $1\le j\le6$. Integrate against this folded measure and take the real part to obtain $p(s)$ for real $s$, or any of its real-axis derivatives.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fourier-measure-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-fourier-measure-lemma</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a node $n$ and write $u=s-n$. For real $\tau$, the identities <a id="fourier:division-identities"></a>$$\label{fourier:division-identities}
 \begin{split}
 \frac{e^{i\pi\tau u}-1}{u}
   &=i\pi\int_0^\tau e^{i\pi tu}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt,\\
 \frac{e^{i\pi\tau u}-1-i\pi\tau u}{u^2}
   &=-\pi^2\int_0^\tau(\tau-t)e^{i\pi tu}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt
 \end{split}$$ hold for complex $u$, with the values at zero taken by continuity. When $\tau<0$, the integrals are oriented integrals. Inserting $\tau=t_l$ and multiplying by $P_le^{i\pi t_ln}$, the subtracted terms sum to zero because $P(n)=P'(n)=0$. On the positive interval $(t_{j-1},t_j)$, only frequencies with $l\ge j$ contribute. The resulting density for $P(s)/(s-n)^2$ is $u_n/2$, and that for $P(s)/(s-n)$ is $v_n/2$. The negative-frequency densities are their conjugate reflections, which proves <a href="#fourier:folded" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:folded">[fourier:folded]</a> and accounts for its factors of two.

All phase factors involving $n$ have modulus one on the real integration interval. Hence the measures for the two individual basis functions satisfy $$\begin{split}
 \int_0^1\left\lvert u_n(t)\right\rvert\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt
 &\le2\pi^2\sum_{l=1}^{6}\left\lvert P_l\right\rvert
                      \int_0^{t_l}(t_l-t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt
 =\pi^2\sum_{l=1}^{6}\left\lvert P_l\right\rvert t_l^2
 =\frac{65\pi^2}{18},\\
 \int_0^1\left\lvert v_n(t)\right\rvert\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt
 &\le2\pi\sum_{l=1}^{6}\left\lvert P_l\right\rvert t_l
 =\frac{32\pi}{3}.
 \end{split}$$ These are also bounds for the total variations of the full measures on $[-1,1]$. The atomic measure for $CP$ has total variation $\left\lvert C\right\rvert\sum_{j=-6}^{6}\left\lvert P_j\right\rvert=25\left\lvert C\right\rvert$. The coefficient summability therefore gives convergence of the sum of basis measures in total variation and proves <a href="#fourier:variation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:variation">[fourier:variation]</a>.

For any compact subset of $\mathbb C$, the integrand in <a href="#fourier:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:measure">[fourier:measure]</a>, and each of its derivatives with respect to $s$, is bounded uniformly in $t\in[-1,1]$. The integral is consequently entire, and the finite cardinal sums converge to it locally uniformly. Away from the nodes, the series in <a href="#fourier:cardinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:cardinal">[fourier:cardinal]</a> converges locally uniformly and has the same limit. Thus the integral supplies its entire extension. Conjugate symmetry of the measure makes that extension real on the real axis. ◻

</details>

<a id="fourier:node-values"></a>
In particular, for every integer $k\ge0$, <a id="fourier:derivative-bound"></a>$$\label{fourier:derivative-bound}
 \left\lvert p^{(k)}(s)\right\rvert\le\pi^k\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}
 \qquad(s\in\mathbb R).$$ The finite cardinal sums and their derivatives converge locally uniformly: the assertion for derivatives follows either directly from the measure bound or from Cauchy’s integral formula on a slightly larger compact set. Passing their node values and derivatives to the limit in <a href="#fourier:node-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-expansion">[fourier:node-expansion]</a> gives the cardinal identities $$\label{fourier:node-values}
 p(n)=Q_nc_n,\qquad p'(n)=Q_n(d_n+D_nc_n)
 \qquad(n\in\{0\}\cup\mathcal N).$$ The other summands in <a href="#fourier:cardinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:cardinal">[fourier:cardinal]</a> vanish to second order at $n$, which explains why these identities involve only its own pair of coefficients.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Fourier transformation

<a id="fourier:kernel"></a>
For a cardinal function $p$, let $$q_p(x)=e^{-\pi hs}p(s),\qquad s=b\left\lvert x\right\rvert^2.$$ Define, for real $-1\le t\le1$, <a id="fourier:frequencies"></a>$$\label{fourier:frequencies}
 \lambda(t)=\frac{i}{b(t+ih)},\qquad
 z(t)=-\frac{B}{t+ih}-ih,$$ and, using the measure from Lemma <a href="#fourier:measure-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fourier:measure-lemma">2</a>, put $$\label{fourier:kernel}
 K_p(s)=\int_{-1}^{1}\lambda(t)e^{i\pi z(t)s}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu(t).$$

<a id="stmt-fourier-transform"></a>
<div id="fourier:transform" class="proposition">

<a id="fourier:damping"></a>
**Proposition 3** (Gaussian Fourier pairing). *The function $q_p$ is real, radial, and Schwartz on $\mathbb R^2$, and $$\widehat{q_p}(\xi)=e^{-\pi hs}K_p(s),\qquad s=b\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^2.$$ The function $K_p$ is entire and real on the real axis. Moreover, $$\label{fourier:damping}
 \operatorname{Im}z(t)
   =h\left(\frac{B}{t^2+h^2}-1\right)
   \ge\frac{26}{435}>0\qquad(-1\le t\le1).$$ Consequently every derivative of $K_p(s)$ decays exponentially as $s\to+\infty$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fourier-transform"><summary>Proof of stmt-fourier-transform</summary>

*Proof.* By <a href="#fourier:measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:measure">[fourier:measure]</a>, $$q_p(x)=\int_{-1}^{1}e^{-\pi b(h-it)\left\lvert x\right\rvert^2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\mu(t).$$ Every spatial derivative of the integrand is a polynomial in $x$, whose coefficients are bounded uniformly for $t\in[-1,1]$, times a Gaussian of modulus $e^{-\pi bh\left\lvert x\right\rvert^2}$. The finite variation of $\mu$ justifies differentiation under the integral and bounds every Schwartz seminorm. Reality follows from the real values of $p$ on the real axis, and radiality from the dependence on $\left\lvert x\right\rvert^2$.

For $\operatorname{Re}a>0$, the two-dimensional Gaussian transform in our Fourier convention is $$\widehat{e^{-\pi a\left\lvert x\right\rvert^2}}(\xi)
   =a^{-1}e^{-\pi\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^2/a}.$$ This follows from the one-dimensional Gaussian integral by taking products and analytically continuing in $a$ to the right half-plane. Apply this with $a=b(h-it)$. Fubini’s theorem applies because the integrands have a common integrable Gaussian majorant. We have $$a^{-1}=\lambda(t),\qquad
 -\frac{\pi\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^2}{a}+\pi hs=i\pi z(t)s,$$ which gives <a href="#fourier:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:kernel">[fourier:kernel]</a> and the asserted Fourier transform. The curve $z([-1,1])$ is compact, so $K_p$ is entire by differentiation under its defining integral. Also, $$\lambda(-t)=\overline{\lambda(t)},\qquad
 z(-t)=-\overline{z(t)}.$$ Thus the same folded measure and real-part convention as in Lemma <a href="#fourier:measure-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fourier:measure-lemma">2</a> apply to $K_p$ and its real-axis derivatives.

Finally, the imaginary part in <a href="#fourier:damping" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:damping">[fourier:damping]</a> is smallest at $\left\lvert t\right\rvert=1$, where its value is $26/435$. Differentiating <a href="#fourier:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:kernel">[fourier:kernel]</a> gives, for $s\ge0$, $$\left\lvert K_p^{(k)}(s)\right\rvert
 \le\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}
