     \max_{\left\lvert t\right\rvert\le1}\bigl(\left\lvert\lambda(t)\right\rvert\,
                              (\pi\left\lvert z(t)\right\rvert)^k\bigr)
     e^{-26\pi s/435}.$$ This proves the decay assertion. ◻

</details>

No cardinal representation of $K_p$ is assumed. Only its entireness, its values and derivatives, and the estimates obtained from <a href="#fourier:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:kernel">[fourier:kernel]</a> will be used in the interpolation equations. In particular, the measure in that formula is supported on the real interval $[-1,1]$; later complex-disk estimates concern specified finite integrands, not an analytic continuation in the integration variable of an arbitrary $\ell^1$ density.

<a id="stmt-fourier-hankel"></a>
<div id="fourier:hankel" class="remark">

<a id="fourier:hankel-formula"></a>
*Remark 4* (A real-radius Hankel representation). For real $s\ge0$, the same transform has the representation $$\label{fourier:hankel-formula}
 K_p(s)=e^{\pi hs}\frac{\pi}{b}\int_0^\infty
       e^{-\pi hu}p(u)
       J_0\!\left(\frac{2\pi\sqrt{us}}{b}\right)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm du,$$ where $J_0(v)=(2\pi)^{-1}\int_0^{2\pi}e^{-iv\cos\theta}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm d\theta$ for real $v$. To prove this, use polar coordinates in the Fourier integral for $q_p$, perform the angular integral, and put $u=b r^2$. The resulting radial integral is $\widehat q_p(\xi)=(\pi/b)\int_0^\infty e^{-\pi hu}p(u)
J_0(2\pi\sqrt{us}/b)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm du$, with $s=b\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^2$. Absolute convergence follows from $\left\lvert p(u)\right\rvert\le\left\lVert\mu\right\rVert_{\mathrm{TV}}$ and $\left\lvert J_0(v)\right\rvert\le1$. Multiplication by $e^{\pi hs}$ gives <a href="#fourier:hankel-formula" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:hankel-formula">[fourier:hankel-formula]</a>. This is an identity on the nonnegative real axis; it is not used as a numerical sign certificate below.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### The coupled interpolation conditions

<a id="fourier:fixed"></a>
<a id="fourier:pair"></a>
We use two cardinal functions $p_1,p_2$, with coefficients $c_{i,n},d_{i,n},C_i$ for function index $i\in\{1,2\}$. Their coefficients at positive nodes will be determined later; fix $$\label{fourier:fixed}
 (c_{1,0},d_{1,0},C_1)=(1,0.44,-0.013),\qquad
 (c_{2,0},d_{2,0},C_2)=(0,-0.368,0.017).$$ All terminating decimals in the construction denote exact rational numbers. Write $K_i=K_{p_i}$ and $H_i=p_i+K_{3-i}$. The associated pair is $$\label{fourier:pair}
 F(x)=e^{-\pi hs}H_1(s),\qquad
 \widehat F(\xi)=e^{-\pi hs}H_2(s),
 \quad s=b\left\lvert x\right\rvert^2\ \text{or}\ b\left\lvert\xi\right\rvert^2,$$ respectively. Indeed, $F=q_{p_1}+\widehat{q_{p_2}}$, and Fourier inversion gives $\widehat F=\widehat{q_{p_1}}+q_{p_2}$, since the functions are even. Thus both functions in <a href="#fourier:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:pair">[fourier:pair]</a> are real, radial, and Schwartz for every pair of admissible coefficient lists.

<a id="fourier:interpolation"></a>
The required interpolation conditions are $$\label{fourier:interpolation}
 \begin{split}
 H_1(n)&=H_2(n)=0,\\
 H_2'(n)&=0,\qquad H_1'(n)=-Q_n\mathbf1_{\{n=1\}}
 \qquad(n\in\mathcal N).
 \end{split}$$ Here primes denote derivatives with respect to $s$. They give zeros of order at least two for both functions at every positive node except that $H_1$ has a prescribed simple zero at $1$.

<a id="stmt-fourier-equivalence"></a>
<div id="fourier:equivalence" class="lemma">

<a id="fourier:coefficient-equations"></a>
**Lemma 5** (Coefficient form of the interpolation). *For two real $\ell^1$ coefficient lists, conditions <a href="#fourier:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:interpolation">[fourier:interpolation]</a> are equivalent to $$\label{fourier:coefficient-equations}
 \begin{split}
 c_{i,n}&=-\frac{K_{3-i}(n)}{Q_n},\\
 d_{i,n}&=\frac{D_nK_{3-i}(n)-K_{3-i}'(n)}{Q_n}
          -\mathbf1_{\{i=1,\ n=1\}}
 \qquad(i=1,2,\ n\in\mathcal N).
 \end{split}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-fourier-equivalence"><summary>Proof of stmt-fourier-equivalence</summary>

*Proof.* By <a href="#fourier:node-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-values">[fourier:node-values]</a>, $$H_i(n)=Q_nc_{i,n}+K_{3-i}(n),\qquad
 H_i'(n)=Q_n(d_{i,n}+D_nc_{i,n})+K_{3-i}'(n).$$ The first equation in <a href="#fourier:coefficient-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:coefficient-equations">[fourier:coefficient-equations]</a> is therefore equivalent to $H_i(n)=0$. Substituting it into the second displayed identity gives the derivative condition in <a href="#fourier:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:interpolation">[fourier:interpolation]</a> exactly when the second coefficient equation holds. Division by $Q_n$ is legitimate by <a href="#fourier:node-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-bounds">[fourier:node-bounds]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:interpolation"></a>
### Solving the interpolation equations

The equations <a href="#fourier:coefficient-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:coefficient-equations">[fourier:coefficient-equations]</a> couple two coefficient lists through the transformed part of the construction. We solve them on $\ell^1$, using the strict damping of the Fourier kernel to control every row beyond a fixed finite set.

Let $$X=\ell^1(\mathcal N;\mathbb R^2),\qquad
 \left\lVert x\right\rVert_1=\sum_{n\in\mathcal N}\bigl(\left\lvert c_n\right\rvert+\left\lvert d_n\right\rvert\bigr)
 \quad\text{for }x=((c_n,d_n))_{n\in\mathcal N}.$$ When writing matrices, we order all $c$ coordinates first and all $d$ coordinates second, with increasing nodes within each group. All operator norms below are the induced $\ell^1$ norms, equivalently the maximum absolute column sum for a finite matrix. A subscript specifying a set of nodes includes both coordinates at each node; a star denotes all positive nodes.

<a id="interpolation:S"></a>
For $x\in X$, let $p_x$ be the function <a href="#fourier:cardinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:cardinal">[fourier:cardinal]</a> with these positive-node coefficients and with $C=c_0=d_0=0$. Define the real linear operator $S$ by $$\label{interpolation:S}
 (Sx)_{c,n}=-\frac{K_{p_x}(n)}{Q_n},\qquad
 (Sx)_{d,n}=\frac{D_nK_{p_x}(n)-K_{p_x}'(n)}{Q_n}.$$ The bounds proved below show, in particular, that $S$ is a bounded operator on $X$.

We use two successive decompositions of this operator. Set <a id="interpolation:node-blocks"></a>$$\label{interpolation:node-blocks}
 J=\{1,3,4\},\qquad E=\mathcal N\cap[7,100],\qquad
 I_f=J\cup E=\mathcal N\cap[1,100],\qquad D=S_{J,J}.$$ A finite inverse handles the coordinates on $J$. A first Schur complement then incorporates $E$, using separate bounds for its rows through $16$ and beyond $16$; a second incorporates the infinite tail beyond $100$. The tail bounds apply to output rows uniformly over all input columns. The first output rows require a separate, larger bound even for columns indexed by large nodes.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Uniform envelopes for the Fourier terms

We use the following rational upper and lower bounds. As throughout the construction, every finite decimal denotes its exact rational value.

<div id="interpolation:envelopes">

| $j$ | $b_j^*$ | $a_j^*$ | $g_j$ | $M_j$ | $\beta_j$ |
|:---:|--------:|--------:|------:|------:|----------:|
|  0  |   2.887 |   2.934 | 2.933 |     — |         — |
|  1  |   2.665 |   2.713 | 2.440 |    56 |         0 |
|  2  |   2.218 |   2.269 | 1.567 |    48 |       4.8 |
|  3  |   1.804 |   1.859 |  .900 |    35 |      3.17 |
|  4  |   1.486 |   1.548 |  .482 |    25 |     1.946 |
|  5  |   1.250 |   1.320 |  .224 |    19 |     1.217 |
|  6  |   1.073 |   1.151 | .0597 | 6.284 |      .792 |

Envelopes for the folded Fourier integrals.

</div>

<a id="interpolation:piece-envelopes"></a>
At each endpoint $t_j=j/6$, $$\left\lvert\lambda(t_j)\right\rvert\le b_j^*,\qquad
 \left\lvert z(t_j)\right\rvert\le a_j^*,\qquad
 \operatorname{Im}z(t_j)\ge g_j.$$ On the interval $t_{j-1}\le t\le t_j$, the corresponding bounds are $$\label{interpolation:piece-envelopes}
 \left\lvert\lambda(t)\right\rvert\le b_{j-1}^*,\quad
 \left\lvert z(t)\right\rvert\le a_{j-1}^*,\quad
 \operatorname{Im}z(t)\ge g_j+\beta_j(t_j-t),\quad
 \left\lvert u_n(t)\right\rvert,\left\lvert v_n(t)\right\rvert\le M_j.$$ The density bounds hold uniformly for $n\in\{0\}\cup\mathcal N$.

Here are direct ways to verify these estimates. The identities $$\left\lvert\lambda(t)\right\rvert=\frac{1}{b\sqrt{t^2+h^2}},\qquad
 \left\lvert z(t)\right\rvert^2=h^2+\frac{B^2-2Bh^2}{t^2+h^2},\qquad
 \operatorname{Im}z(t)=h\left(\frac{B}{t^2+h^2}-1\right)$$ show that the first two quantities decrease on $[0,1]$. The nonnegative quantity $2hBt/(t^2+h^2)^2$, which is minus the derivative of $\operatorname{Im}z$, has its minimum on each of the six intervals at an endpoint. Its only interior critical point on the positive axis is a maximum. These observations give the first three bounds in <a href="#interpolation:piece-envelopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:piece-envelopes">[interpolation:piece-envelopes]</a> by endpoint substitution. For the densities, $e^{i\pi t_l n}$ depends only on $n$ modulo $12$, whereas $\left\lvert e^{-i\pi tn}\right\rvert=1$ on the real interval. The norm of an affine function of $t$ is convex. Thus an upper bound for both folded densities on the $j$th interval is <a id="interpolation:density-check"></a>$$\label{interpolation:density-check}
 2\max_{a\in A}\max\left\{
 \pi\left\lvert\sum_{l=j}^6P_le^{i\pi t_la}\right\rvert,\quad
 \pi^2\max_{u\in\{t_{j-1},t_j\}}
       \left\lvert\sum_{l=j}^6(t_l-u)P_le^{i\pi t_la}\right\rvert
 \right\}.$$ Substitution gives a value less than the displayed $M_j$ in every case. For the constant input $C=1$, the absolute folded atom masses are $$(m_0^*,\ldots,m_6^*)=(5,4,4,4,2,4,2).$$

<a id="interpolation:L"></a>
<a id="interpolation:moment-bound"></a>
For $s\ge0$, set $$\label{interpolation:L}
 \begin{aligned}
 L_j(s)&=b_{j-1}^*e^{-\pi g_js}
       \min\left\{\frac16,\frac{1}{\pi\beta_js}\right\}
       &&(1\le j\le6),\\
 L_j^P(s)&=m_j^*b_j^*e^{-\pi g_js}
       &&(0\le j\le6).
 \end{aligned}$$ When $\beta_js=0$, the minimum in the first line is understood to be $1/6$. Integrating the exponential envelope in <a href="#interpolation:piece-envelopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:piece-envelopes">[interpolation:piece-envelopes]</a> over a piece of length $1/6$ gives its factor $L_j(s)$. Differentiating the kernel contributes $(i\pi z(t))^\ell$. Consequently, for every integer $\ell\ge0$ and every input $p$ of the form <a href="#fourier:cardinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:cardinal">[fourier:cardinal]</a>, $$\label{interpolation:moment-bound}
 \left\lvert K_p^{(\ell)}(s)\right\rvert
 \le w\sum_{j=1}^6 M_jL_j(s)(\pi a_{j-1}^*)^\ell
      +\left\lvert C\right\rvert\sum_{j=0}^6L_j^P(s)(\pi a_j^*)^\ell,
 \qquad s\ge0,$$ where $w=\sum_{n\in\{0\}\cup\mathcal N}(\left\lvert c_n\right\rvert+\left\lvert d_n\right\rvert)$. The argument applies first to finite lists and then to all such lists by the finite-measure representation and absolute summability.

<a id="interpolation:row-envelope"></a>
Both $Q_n$ and $D_n$ are $12$-periodic on the nodes. Moreover, $$L_j(n+12k)\le e^{-12k\pi g_j}L_j(n)\qquad(k\ge0),$$ because the minimum factor in <a href="#interpolation:L" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:L">[interpolation:L]</a> is nonincreasing. For every real cutoff $x\ge0$, the value and derivative terms in <a href="#interpolation:S" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:S">[interpolation:S]</a>, together with <a href="#interpolation:moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:moment-bound">[interpolation:moment-bound]</a>, therefore imply $$\label{interpolation:row-envelope}
 \left\lVert S_{>x,*}\right\rVert
 \le
 \sum_{n\in\mathcal N\cap(x,x+12]}
 \sum_{j=1}^6
 \frac{1+\left\lvert D_n\right\rvert+\pi a_{j-1}^*}{Q_n}\,
 \frac{M_jL_j(n)}{1-e^{-12\pi g_j}}.$$ Here $>x$ means a strict inequality for the row node. This is a bound uniform over all input columns. It applies as well to a single input coefficient $c_0$ or $d_0$.

For completeness, this estimate also justifies the operator definition on the whole space. Apply the bound first to one positive-node basis column, then sum with the absolute coefficients of a finitely supported list. The case $x=0$ bounds the resulting output norm by a constant times the input norm. Finite lists are dense in $X$, so this map has a unique bounded extension to $X$. The basis measures converge in total variation by Lemma <a href="#fourier:measure-lemma" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fourier:measure-lemma">2</a>; hence $K_{p_x}(n)$ and $K_{p_x}'(n)$ converge at each fixed node as the lists are truncated. The extended map therefore has exactly the coordinates in <a href="#interpolation:S" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:S">[interpolation:S]</a>, not merely the coordinates of an auxiliary matrix limit.

<a id="interpolation:tails"></a>
<a id="interpolation:atom-tail"></a>
For $x=0,4,16,100$, respectively, the right side of <a href="#interpolation:row-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:row-envelope">[interpolation:row-envelope]</a> is less than $$27.7,\qquad .272,\qquad .0145,\qquad 4.7\cdot10^{-10}.$$ We shall use the slightly weaker bounds $$\label{interpolation:tails}
 \left\lVert S_{>0,*}\right\rVert<28,\qquad
 \left\lVert S_{>4,*}\right\rVert<.28,\qquad
 \left\lVert S_{>16,*}\right\rVert<.015,\qquad
 \left\lVert S_{>100,*}\right\rVert<5\cdot10^{-10}.$$ For the input $C=1$, the analogue of <a href="#interpolation:row-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:row-envelope">[interpolation:row-envelope]</a> replaces $M_jL_j$ by $L_j^P$, uses $a_j^*$ in place of $a_{j-1}^*$, and sums over $0\le j\le6$. For rows greater than $100$, it gives the bound $$\label{interpolation:atom-tail}
 \sum_{n\in\mathcal N,\ n>100}
 \left(\frac{\left\lvert K_P(n)\right\rvert}{Q_n}
 +\frac{\left\lvert D_nK_P(n)-K_P'(n)\right\rvert}{Q_n}\right)
 <3.2\cdot10^{-8}.$$ The scalar substitutions in these envelope estimates use only the explicit constants and the node data. Section <a href="#cert:scalar-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:scalar-bounds">6.6</a> records the finite scalar bounds and the precise coverage of their supplied verifier.

<a id="stmt-interpolation-compactness"></a>
<div id="interpolation:compactness" class="remark">

*Remark 6* (Compactness of the interpolation operator). Let $P_M$ keep the output coordinates with node at most $M$. Then $P_MS$ has finite rank. The right side of <a href="#interpolation:row-envelope" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:row-envelope">[interpolation:row-envelope]</a> tends to zero as $x\to\infty$: there are only six nodes in any period, the periodic $Q_n,D_n$ are uniformly bounded with $Q_n$ bounded away from zero, and every $L_j(n)$ contains the factor $e^{-\pi g_jn}$ with $g_j>0$. Thus $\left\lVert S-P_MS\right\rVert\to0$. The operator $S$ is a norm limit of finite-rank operators and is compact on $X$. This property describes the tail structure; the explicit inverse below is obtained independently from the quantitative block estimates.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The finite certificates

There are $51$ nodes in $I_f$ and hence $102$ scalar coordinates. The approximate lists $\widetilde x_1,\widetilde x_2$ are given by Table <a href="#tab:coefficients" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:coefficients">2</a>, with the indicated factor $10^{-10}$, and are zero at positive nodes beyond $100$. Append the fixed data <a href="#fourier:fixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:fixed">[fourier:fixed]</a> to obtain $\widetilde p_i$, and write $\widetilde K_i=K_{\widetilde p_i}$.

<a id="stmt-interpolation-finite"></a>
<div id="interpolation:finite" class="lemma">

<a id="interpolation:finite-residuals"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Finite certificates). *For each $\eta\in\{1,-1\}$, the matrix $I-\eta D$ is invertible, and <a id="interpolation:finite-matrices"></a>$$\label{interpolation:finite-matrices}
 \left\lVert(I-\eta D)^{-1}\right\rVert<32,\qquad
 \left\lVert(I-\eta D)^{-1}S_{J,E}\right\rVert<3.7,\qquad
 \left\lVert S_{E\cap[7,16],J}\right\rVert<.09.$$ For $i=1,2$ and $n\in I_f$, the table satisfies $$\label{interpolation:finite-residuals}
 \begin{split}
 \left\lvert\widetilde c_{i,n}+\frac{\widetilde K_{3-i}(n)}{Q_n}\right\rvert
 &<10^{-9},\\
 \left\lvert\widetilde d_{i,n}+\mathbf 1_{\{i=1,\ n=1\}}
       +\frac{\widetilde K_{3-i}'(n)-D_n\widetilde K_{3-i}(n)}{Q_n}\right\rvert
 &<10^{-9}.
 \end{split}$$ For each $i$, the norm of the tabulated list, including node zero, is less than $2.29$, and its norm on nodes greater than $40$ is less than $.00002$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-interpolation-finite"><summary>Proof of stmt-interpolation-finite</summary>

*Proof.* The primary interval calculation in Appendix <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">6</a> proves the exact bounds <a href="#cert:matrix-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:matrix-table">[cert:matrix-table]</a>, <a href="#cert:exterior-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:exterior-block">[cert:exterior-block]</a>, and <a href="#cert:residual-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:residual-bounds">[cert:residual-bounds]</a>, using the quadrature assertion of Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a> for the analytic moment remainder. The elementary argument in Section <a href="#cert:matrix-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:matrix-bounds">6.4</a> gives the matrix conclusions, and <a href="#cert:table-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:table-norms">[cert:table-norms]</a> gives the list norms. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Inversion on the whole coefficient space

We first prove an inverse bound that keeps finite and tail residuals separate.

<a id="stmt-interpolation-inverse"></a>
<div id="interpolation:inverse" class="lemma">

<a id="interpolation:inverse-bound"></a>
**Lemma 8**. *For each $\eta\in\{1,-1\}$, $I-\eta S$ has a bounded inverse on $X$. If $r=r_f+r_t$, with $r_f$ supported on $I_f$ and $r_t$ on $\mathcal N\cap(100,\infty)$, then $$\label{interpolation:inverse-bound}
 \left\lVert(I-\eta S)^{-1}r\right\rVert_1
 \le91\left\lVert r_f\right\rVert_1+2540\left\lVert r_t\right\rVert_1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-interpolation-inverse"><summary>Proof of stmt-interpolation-inverse</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $\eta$ and first restrict to the finite block $I_f$. Put $$V=(I-\eta D)^{-1},\qquad L=S_{E,J},\qquad U=S_{J,E}.$$ The first block of tail estimates and the finite certificates give $$\left\lVert L\right\rVert
 \le\left\lVert S_{E\cap[7,16],J}\right\rVert+\left\lVert S_{E\cap(16,100],J}\right\rVert
 <.09+.015<.11.$$ Eliminating the $J$ coordinates from $(I-\eta S_{I_f,I_f})x_f=r_f$ leaves on $E$ the operator $$I-\eta S_{E,E}-LVU.$$ Its difference from the identity has norm less than $$\left\lVert S_{E,E}\right\rVert+\left\lVert L\right\rVert\left\lVert VU\right\rVert
 <.28+.11\cdot3.7=.687<1.$$ It is therefore invertible by a Neumann series. Solving this block and substituting into the $J$ equations yields an inverse $V_f$ for the whole finite compression, with <a id="interpolation:finite-inverse-bound"></a>$$\label{interpolation:finite-inverse-bound}
 \begin{split}
 \left\lVert V_f\right\rVert
 &\le\max\left\{
 32+\frac{(1+3.7)\cdot.11\cdot32}{1-.687},\quad
 \frac{1+3.7}{1-.687}\right\}\\
 &<90.
 \end{split}$$ Indeed, the two entries in the maximum bound the output norm arising from a unit input on $J$ and on $E$, respectively.

<a id="interpolation:tail-schur"></a>
Now write $X=X_f\oplus X_t$, where $X_t$ is the infinite tail space. The full equation $(I-\eta S)x=r$ is equivalent, after elimination of $x_f$, to $$\label{interpolation:tail-schur}
 \bigl(I-\eta S_{t,t}-S_{t,f}V_fS_{f,t}\bigr)x_t
 =r_t+\eta S_{t,f}V_fr_f.$$ Put $\delta=5\cdot10^{-10}$. By <a href="#interpolation:tails" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:tails">[interpolation:tails]</a>, $$\left\lVert S_{t,t}\right\rVert<\delta,\qquad
 \left\lVert S_{t,f}\right\rVert<\delta,\qquad
 \left\lVert S_{f,t}\right\rVert<28.$$ The operator subtracted from the identity in <a href="#interpolation:tail-schur" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:tail-schur">[interpolation:tail-schur]</a> thus has norm less than $$\delta(1+90\cdot28)=2521\delta<1.$$ Its Neumann series converges in the bounded operators on $X_t$. Together with the finite inverse this proves invertibility on the full space $X$, without any passage to a limit of finite-section solutions. Quantitatively, $$\left\lVert x_t\right\rVert_1
 \le\frac{\left\lVert r_t\right\rVert_1+90\delta\left\lVert r_f\right\rVert_1}{1-2521\delta},
 \qquad
 \left\lVert x_f\right\rVert_1\le90\left\lVert r_f\right\rVert_1+2520\left\lVert x_t\right\rVert_1.$$ Adding these inequalities gives coefficients less than $91$ and $2540$ for $\left\lVert r_f\right\rVert_1$ and $\left\lVert r_t\right\rVert_1$, respectively, which proves <a href="#interpolation:inverse-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:inverse-bound">[interpolation:inverse-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-interpolation-solution"></a>
<div id="interpolation:solution" class="proposition">

<a id="interpolation:coefficient-error"></a>
**Proposition 9** (Exact interpolation and coefficient enclosure). *There are unique real lists $x_1,x_2\in X$ satisfying <a href="#fourier:coefficient-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:coefficient-equations">[fourier:coefficient-equations]</a> with the fixed data <a href="#fourier:fixed" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:fixed">[fourier:fixed]</a>. For each $i=1,2$, $$\label{interpolation:coefficient-error}
 \sum_{n\in\mathcal N}
 \bigl(\left\lvert c_{i,n}-\widetilde c_{i,n}\right\rvert
       +\left\lvert d_{i,n}-\widetilde d_{i,n}\right\rvert\bigr)<.00003.$$ The norm of each exact list, including its node-zero coefficients, is less than $2.3$. The corresponding functions satisfy all the interpolation conditions <a href="#fourier:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:interpolation">[fourier:interpolation]</a>.*

</div>

The uniqueness here is within the specified coefficient space, with the fixed node-zero data and constants. Triangular-shell values and first derivatives do not determine an arbitrary radial Schwartz function and its Fourier transform uniquely (Talebizadeh Sardari 2021, Theorem 1.7) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Sardari2021](#ref-Sardari2021)].

<details id="proof-stmt-interpolation-solution"><summary>Proof of stmt-interpolation-solution</summary>

*Proof.* Let $p_i^0$ denote <a href="#fourier:cardinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:cardinal">[fourier:cardinal]</a> with the fixed values $(C_i,c_{i,0},d_{i,0})$ and all positive-node coefficients zero. Let $e\in X$ have its $d_1$ coordinate equal to one and all other coordinates zero. Define $g_i\in X$ by $$(g_i)_{c,n}=-\frac{K_{p_{3-i}^0}(n)}{Q_n},\qquad
 (g_i)_{d,n}=
 \frac{D_nK_{p_{3-i}^0}(n)-K_{p_{3-i}^0}'(n)}{Q_n}
 -\mathbf1_{\{i=1\}}e_{d,n}.$$ The estimates for the node-zero and constant columns show that these are summable lists. The equations to solve are precisely $$x_1-Sx_2=g_1,\qquad x_2-Sx_1=g_2.$$ Their sum is an equation for $I-S$, and their difference is an equation for $I+S$. Lemma <a href="#interpolation:inverse" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="interpolation:inverse">8</a> gives unique real solutions and hence unique $x_1,x_2$.

It remains to locate these solutions relative to the table. Set $$\rho_i=\widetilde x_i-S\widetilde x_{3-i}-g_i.$$ There are $102$ scalar coordinates in $I_f$, so <a href="#interpolation:finite-residuals" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:finite-residuals">[interpolation:finite-residuals]</a> gives $$\left\lVert(\rho_i)_f\right\rVert_1<1.1\cdot10^{-7}=:R_f.$$ On the tail, both the tabulated positive-node list and the vector $e$ vanish. The density-column estimate, now including node zero, and <a href="#interpolation:atom-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:atom-tail">[interpolation:atom-tail]</a> imply $$\left\lVert(\rho_i)_t\right\rVert_1
 <2.29\cdot5\cdot10^{-10}+.017\cdot3.2\cdot10^{-8}=:R_t.$$ Here $2.29$ bounds the whole tabulated coefficient list, and $.017$ bounds both fixed constants in absolute value.

The errors $x_i-\widetilde x_i$ are recovered by taking half the sum and half the difference of the solutions with residuals $-(\rho_1+\rho_2)$ and $-(\rho_1-\rho_2)$. Applying <a href="#interpolation:inverse-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:inverse-bound">[interpolation:inverse-bound]</a> to both gives, for each list, $$\left\lVert x_i-\widetilde x_i\right\rVert_1
 \le2(91R_f+2540R_t)
 =.00002860012<.00003.$$ This proves <a href="#interpolation:coefficient-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:coefficient-error">[interpolation:coefficient-error]</a>. Since the fixed node-zero coefficients agree, the total exact coefficient norm is less than $2.29+.00003<2.3$. Finally, <a href="#fourier:coefficient-equations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:coefficient-equations">[fourier:coefficient-equations]</a> is equivalent to <a href="#fourier:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:interpolation">[fourier:interpolation]</a> by Lemma <a href="#fourier:equivalence" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fourier:equivalence">5</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:signs"></a>
### Global signs

We now prove the global signs for the exact functions supplied by Proposition <a href="#interpolation:solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="interpolation:solution">9</a>. The strict bounds will also identify their zeros within the respective sign domains. Write $$\sigma_1=-1,\qquad \sigma_2=1,\qquad
  \delta=3\cdot10^{-5}.$$ The difference between each exact coefficient list and its tabulated list has norm less than $\delta$. Their fixed coefficients at zero and their constants $C_i$ agree. Tildes will always denote the functions constructed from the finite tabulated lists, so that $\widetilde H_i=\widetilde p_i+K_{\widetilde p_{3-i}}$. We use the spectral envelopes of Section <a href="#sec:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interpolation">3</a> throughout.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Taylor quotients on half-gaps

Set $\mathcal N_0=\{0\}\cup\mathcal N$. For each $m\in\mathcal N_0\cap[0,40]$, let $m^+$ be its successor in $\mathcal N_0$ and include $$y=\frac{m^+-m}{2}.$$ If $m>0$, also include $y=(m^--m)/2$, where $m^-$ is its predecessor. Thus $y$ is signed, and the associated half-gap is $$I_{m,y}=\{m+yv:0\le v\le1\}.$$ For function index $2$ we use every such interval, with order $\nu=0$ at $m=0$ and $\nu=2$ otherwise. For function index $1$ we use only those intervals contained in $[1,\infty)$, with $\nu=1$ at $m=1$ and $\nu=2$ otherwise. In particular, the only odd order occurs on the rightward half-gap from $1$. There are $22$ centers from $0$ through $40$. They give $43$ half-gaps for index $2$ and, after removing the two intervals not contained in $[1,\infty)$, $41$ for index $1$, for a total of $84$ certified intervals.

Fix one of the specified triples $(i,m,y)$. For $s=m+u$ in its interval, we will prove positivity of $$\frac{\sigma_i H_i(m+u)}{u^\nu}.$$ Here $u^0=1$, and for $\nu>0$ the quotient at $u=0$ is interpreted by continuity. The interpolation conditions make the derivatives of $H_i$ of orders below $\nu$ vanish at $m$, so this extension exists. Positivity of the quotient gives the required sign: every order is even except for the rightward half-gap from $1$, where $u\ge0$. Figure <a href="#fig:half-gaps" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:half-gaps">1</a> shows the node-to-midpoint intervals on which these quotients are studied.

<figure id="fig:half-gaps">
<div class="picture">
<p>(14,2.35) (0,1.2)<span>(1,0)<span>13.4</span></span> (13.6,1.05)<span>$s$</span> (0,1.2) (1,1.2) (3,1.2) (4,1.2) (7,1.2) (9,1.2) (12,1.2) (13,1.2) (0,.65)<span>(0,0)<span>$0$</span></span> (1,.65)<span>(0,0)<span>$1$</span></span> (3,.65)<span>(0,0)<span>$3$</span></span> (4,.65)<span>(0,0)<span>$4$</span></span> (7,.65)<span>(0,0)<span>$7$</span></span> (9,.65)<span>(0,0)<span>$9$</span></span> (12,.65)<span>(0,0)<span>$12$</span></span> (13,.65)<span>(0,0)<span>$13$</span></span> (.5,1.05)<span>(0,1)<span>.3</span></span> (2,1.05)<span>(0,1)<span>.3</span></span> (3.5,1.05)<span>(0,1)<span>.3</span></span> (5.5,1.05)<span>(0,1)<span>.3</span></span> (8,1.05)<span>(0,1)<span>.3</span></span> (10.5,1.05)<span>(0,1)<span>.3</span></span> (12.5,1.05)<span>(0,1)<span>.3</span></span> (4,1.8)<span>(1,0)<span>1.5</span></span> (4,1.65)<span>(0,1)<span>.3</span></span> (5.5,1.65)<span>(0,1)<span>.3</span></span> (6,1.8)<span>(0,0)[l]<span>one half-gap</span></span></p>
</div>
<figcaption>Interpolation nodes (dots) and adjacent gap midpoints (short marks) in the squared-radius coordinate. Each node-to-midpoint segment is a half-gap. The finite sign certificates control a Taylor quotient on each whole half-gap; a separate argument covers the remaining unbounded region.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-half-gaps"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:half-gaps`)

Visual witness description of PDF page 16 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A horizontal axis labeled $s$ with an arrow pointing to the right. On the axis, interpolation nodes are marked with filled dots labeled underneath with integers: 0, 1, 3, 4, 7, 9, 12, 13, followed by a dashed line and dots before the terminating arrow. Between each adjacent pair of node dots, midpoints of the gaps are indicated by short vertical tick marks crossing the axis. Above the interval between node 4 and the midpoint between 4 and 7, a horizontal bracket with vertical end-ticks is drawn and labeled "one half-gap".
```


<a id="signs:deleted-jets"></a>
<a id="signs:coefficient-error"></a>
The tabulated function $\widetilde H_i$ approximates $H_i$ but need not reproduce its exact zero and slope at a node. We therefore compare the exact quotient with the Taylor series of $\widetilde H_i$ after deleting the terms of orders below $\nu$. Put $\Delta_i=H_i-\widetilde H_i$. Since $\widetilde H_i$ is entire and the lower derivatives of $H_i$ vanish, this Taylor series gives $$\begin{aligned}
\label{signs:deleted-jets}
 &\frac{\sigma_i H_i(m+u)}{u^\nu}
 -\sigma_i\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}
       \frac{\widetilde H_i^{(r+\nu)}(m)}{(r+\nu)!}u^r
 \nonumber\\
 &\hspace{1cm}=
 \frac{\sigma_i}{u^\nu}
 \left(\Delta_i(m+u)-
       \sum_{j=0}^{\nu-1}\frac{\Delta_i^{(j)}(m)}{j!}u^j\right).
\end{aligned}$$ For $\nu=0$, the sum on the second line is empty and the right-hand side is simply $\sigma_i\Delta_i(m+u)$. For $\nu>0$, Taylor’s integral remainder gives the exact identity $$\frac{\Delta_i(m+u)-\sum_{j=0}^{\nu-1}
             \Delta_i^{(j)}(m)u^j/j!}{u^\nu}
 =\frac{1}{(\nu-1)!}\int_0^1(1-t)^{\nu-1}
                \Delta_i^{(\nu)}(m+tu)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt.$$ It holds for either sign of $u$ and extends continuously to $u=0$. Since $m+tu$ stays in the same half-gap, the absolute value of the right-hand side of <a href="#signs:deleted-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:deleted-jets">[signs:deleted-jets]</a> is at most $$\label{signs:coefficient-error}
  \frac{1}{\nu!}\sup_{s\in I_{m,y}}
       \left\lvert\Delta_i^{(\nu)}(s)\right\rvert.$$ The same bound applies at $u=0$ by continuity and for either orientation of the half-gap. Deleting the lower Taylor terms is essential here: dividing the raw table error by $u^\nu$ could be singular at the node.

After writing $u=yv$, the first $29$ terms of the tabulated series in <a href="#signs:deleted-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:deleted-jets">[signs:deleted-jets]</a> form a polynomial of degree at most $28$ in $v$. We bound it on $[0,1]$ using the standard range enclosure by Bernstein coefficients (Titi and Garloff 2019, sec. 3) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[TitiGarloff2019](#ref-TitiGarloff2019)], retaining its elementary proof below.

<a id="stmt-signs-bernstein"></a>
<div id="signs:bernstein" class="lemma">

<a id="signs:power-coefficients"></a>
<a id="signs:bernstein-bound"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Finite Bernstein certificate). *For every specified triple $(i,m,y)$, put $$\label{signs:power-coefficients}
  T_r=\sigma_i y^r
       \frac{\widetilde H_i^{(r+\nu)}(m)}{(r+\nu)!},
  \qquad 0\le r\le28.$$ Then, for $0\le k\le28$, $$\label{signs:bernstein-bound}
  B_k:=\sum_{r=0}^k
       \frac{\binom{k}{r}}{\binom{28}{r}}T_r
  >\begin{cases}
      0.74,&m=0,\\
      0.18,&m=1,\\
      0.02,&m=3,4,\\
      0.009,&4<m\le40.
    \end{cases}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-signs-bernstein"><summary>Proof of stmt-signs-bernstein</summary>

*Proof.* The primary interval calculation in Appendix <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">6</a> encloses the exact derivatives of the tabulated functions in <a href="#signs:power-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:power-coefficients">[signs:power-coefficients]</a>, using the quadrature assertion of Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a> for the analytic moment remainder. Its exact grouped bounds <a href="#cert:bernstein-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:bernstein-table">[cert:bernstein-table]</a> imply the stated thresholds. ◻

</details>

<a id="signs:bernstein-identity"></a>
The relevance of this finite statement is the identity $$\label{signs:bernstein-identity}
  \sum_{r=0}^{28}T_r v^r
  =\sum_{k=0}^{28} B_k\binom{28}{k}v^k(1-v)^{28-k}.$$ Indeed, for $0\le r\le k\le d=28$, the binomial identity $$\frac{\binom{k}{r}}{\binom{d}{r}}\binom{d}{k}
 =\binom{d-r}{k-r}$$ gives $$\sum_{k=r}^{d}\frac{\binom{k}{r}}{\binom{d}{r}}
       \binom{d}{k}v^k(1-v)^{d-k}
 =v^r\sum_{l=0}^{d-r}\binom{d-r}{l}v^l(1-v)^{d-r-l}=v^r.$$ Multiply by $T_r$, sum over $r$, and interchange the two finite sums to obtain <a href="#signs:bernstein-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:bernstein-identity">[signs:bernstein-identity]</a>. For $0\le v\le1$, the basis functions on the right are nonnegative and sum to one. Hence the lower bounds in <a href="#signs:bernstein-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:bernstein-bound">[signs:bernstein-bound]</a> hold on the whole interval for the polynomial on the left. To obtain a lower bound for the exact quotient, it remains to control the coefficient error in <a href="#signs:coefficient-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:coefficient-error">[signs:coefficient-error]</a> and the terms beyond degree $28$ in the tabulated series.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Bounds for the two errors

<a id="signs:perturbation-envelope"></a>
<a id="signs:perturbation-caps"></a>
The density bounds and <a href="#interpolation:moment-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:moment-bound">[interpolation:moment-bound]</a>, together with the separate coefficient error bounds $\delta$ for the two functions, give, for $s\ge s_*\ge0$, $$\label{signs:perturbation-envelope}
 \left\lvert\Delta_i^{(\nu)}(s)\right\rvert
 \le \delta\sum_{j=1}^6 M_j
       \left(\frac{(\pi t_j)^\nu}{6}
            +L_j(s_*)(\pi a_{j-1}^*)^\nu\right).$$ There are no atom contributions because the constants $C_i$ have not changed. Substitution of the envelopes proves the following uniform bounds for <a href="#signs:coefficient-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:coefficient-error">[signs:coefficient-error]</a>: $$\label{signs:perturbation-caps}
 \frac{1}{\nu!}\sup_{I_{m,y}}\left\lvert\Delta_i^{(\nu)}\right\rvert
 <\begin{cases}
    0.004,&m=0,\\
    0.011,&m=1,\\
    0.002,&m\ge3.
   \end{cases}$$ Here one uses $s_*=0$ for $m=0$, $s_*=1/2$ for $m=1$ and both possible orders, and $s_*=2$ for $m\ge3$. For example the first bound is $\delta\sum_{j=1}^6(M_j/6+M_jL_j(0))<0.004$. All these are bounds on intervals, obtained from the derivative envelopes.

<a id="signs:taylor-bound"></a>
It remains to truncate the infinite series in <a href="#signs:deleted-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:deleted-jets">[signs:deleted-jets]</a>. Put $Y=\left\lvert y\right\rvert$ and $q=29+\nu$. The total variation of the direct spectral measure of either tabulated function is less than $80$, since $$2.29\sum_{j=1}^6\frac{M_j}{6}
       +0.017\sum_{j=0}^6 m_j^*<80.$$ For the transformed part, use coefficient norm less than $2.3$ and $\left\lvert C_i\right\rvert\le0.017$. Expanding each spectral exponential at $m$ gives the following bound for the omitted quotient terms: $$\begin{aligned}
\label{signs:taylor-bound}
 \mathcal E(m,Y,\nu)
 := {}&\frac{Y^{-\nu}}{q!}
       \frac{1}{1-\pi a_0^*Y/(q+1)}
 \Bigg(80(\pi Y)^q
 \nonumber\\[-2pt]
 &\qquad+2.3\sum_{j=1}^6
       M_jL_j(m)(\pi a_{j-1}^*Y)^q
       +0.017\sum_{j=0}^6
       L_j^P(m)(\pi a_j^*Y)^q\Bigg).
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed the first omitted exponential term has degree $q$ and subsequent absolute-value ratios are at most $\pi a_0^*Y/(q+1)<1$. After division by $u^\nu$, all powers still have degree at least $29$, so their maximum for $\left\lvert u\right\rvert\le Y$ is bounded by evaluating these absolute-value majorants at $Y$. This proves <a href="#signs:taylor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:taylor-bound">[signs:taylor-bound]</a> also when $y<0$.

<a id="signs:taylor-representatives"></a>
The five representative substitutions are $$\label{signs:taylor-representatives}
 (m,Y,\nu)=(0,\tfrac12,0),\ (1,1,1),\ (1,1,2),\ (3,1,2),\
           (4,\tfrac32,2).$$ In each case <a href="#signs:taylor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:taylor-bound">[signs:taylor-bound]</a> is less than $0.001$. Increasing $m$ or decreasing $Y$ only decreases the bound. The first three cases handle centers $0$ and $1$, the fourth handles $m=3$, and the last handles every center $m\ge4$. Thus the truncation error is less than $0.001$ on every required half-gap.

<a id="signs:finite-quotient"></a>
Combining <a href="#signs:bernstein-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:bernstein-identity">[signs:bernstein-identity]</a>, <a href="#signs:deleted-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:deleted-jets">[signs:deleted-jets]</a>, <a href="#signs:perturbation-caps" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:perturbation-caps">[signs:perturbation-caps]</a>, and <a href="#signs:taylor-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:taylor-bound">[signs:taylor-bound]</a>, we obtain $$\label{signs:finite-quotient}
  \frac{\sigma_i H_i(s)}{(s-m)^\nu}
  >\begin{cases}
     0.735,&m=0,\\
     0.168,&m=1,\\
     0.017,&m=3,4,\\
     0.006,&4<m\le40,
    \end{cases}
  \qquad s\in I_{m,y},$$ with endpoint values interpreted by continuity. Since the only odd order has $s-m\ge0$, these inequalities imply the desired signs on all of these intervals. Their union is $[0,41.5]$ for index $2$ and $[1,41.5]$ for index $1$: adjacent half-gaps meet at their common midpoint, and the last interval runs from $40$ halfway to $43$.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The unbounded region

For $s\ge s_0:=41.5$, split the exact direct part as <a id="signs:tail-split"></a>$$\label{signs:tail-split}
 \begin{aligned}
 p_i(s)&=P(s)R_{i,0}(s)+p_{i,1}(s),\\
 R_{i,0}(s)&=C_i+
   \sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal N_0\\n\le40}}
   \left(\frac{c_{i,n}}{(s-n)^2}+\frac{d_{i,n}}{s-n}\right),\\
 \widetilde R_{i,0}(s)&=C_i+
   \sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal N_0\\n\le40}}
   \left(\frac{\widetilde c_{i,n}}{(s-n)^2}
         +\frac{\widetilde d_{i,n}}{s-n}\right).
 \end{aligned}$$ Here $p_{i,1}$ is the cardinal contribution from exact coefficients at nodes greater than $40$. The function $R_{i,0}$ uses the exact coefficients, whereas $\widetilde R_{i,0}$ uses the finite table; their constants and node-zero coefficients agree. All denominators in these two rational functions are at least $1.5$. Write the corresponding decomposition of the exact function as $$E_i:=p_{i,1}+K_{p_{3-i}},\qquad H_i=P R_{i,0}+E_i.$$ For each $s\ge s_0$, the final comparison will use a nearest node $m\ge43$. At such a node, the exact interpolation conditions and the double zero of $P$ make $E_i$ have zero value and slope; we verify this at the comparison step below. The useful scale for both terms is therefore $(s-m)^2$. We will bound $\sigma_iR_{i,0}$ below, bound $P(s)$ below by a multiple of $(s-m)^2$, and bound $|E_i(s)|$ above at the same scale through a uniform second-derivative estimate.

<a id="signs:rational-tail"></a>
<a id="signs:rational-lower-sum"></a>
We first show $$\label{signs:rational-tail}
  \sigma_i R_{i,0}(s)>0.011\qquad(s\ge s_0,\ i=1,2).$$ For the tabulated coefficients put $u_n^*=\sigma_i\widetilde c_{i,n}$, $v_n^*=\sigma_i\widetilde d_{i,n}$, and $[w]_-:=\min\{w,0\}$. Using $$\frac{1}{s-n}=\frac1s+\frac{n}{s(s-n)}$$ and discarding positive terms gives the following lower bound for $\sigma_i\widetilde R_{i,0}(s)$, uniformly for $s\ge s_0$: $$\label{signs:rational-lower-sum}
 \sigma_i C_i+
 \frac{[\sum_{n\le40}v_n^*]_-}{s_0}
 +\sum_{n\le40}
    \left(\frac{[u_n^*]_-}{(s_0-n)^2}
          +\frac{[v_n^*]_-n}{s_0(s_0-n)}\right),$$ where both sums include $n=0$. Each negative reciprocal term is smallest at $s=s_0$. The exact rational sums in <a href="#signs:rational-lower-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:rational-lower-sum">[signs:rational-lower-sum]</a> exceed $0.012$ for index $1$ and $0.016$ for index $2$. To pass to the exact rational part, use $s-n\ge1.5>1$ and the coefficient enclosure to obtain $$\begin{split}
 \left\lvert R_{i,0}(s)-\widetilde R_{i,0}(s)\right\rvert
 &\le\sum_{n\le40}\left(
   \frac{\left\lvert c_{i,n}-\widetilde c_{i,n}\right\rvert}{(s-n)^2}
  +\frac{\left\lvert d_{i,n}-\widetilde d_{i,n}\right\rvert}{s-n}\right)\\
 &<\frac{\delta}{s_0-40}=0.00002.
 \end{split}$$ The finite lower sums therefore imply the asserted exact bound <a href="#signs:rational-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:rational-tail">[signs:rational-tail]</a>.

<a id="signs:tail-second-derivative"></a>
The coefficient norm of the tabulated part with $n>40$ is less than $0.00002$ for either function. Hence the exact coefficient norm for $p_{i,1}$ is less than $0.00005$. The derivative envelopes imply $$\begin{aligned}
\label{signs:tail-second-derivative}
 \sup_{s\ge s_0}\left\lvert E_i''(s)\right\rvert
 \le {}&0.00005\cdot77
       +2.3\sum_{j=1}^6 M_jL_j(s_0)(\pi a_{j-1}^*)^2
 \nonumber\\
       &+0.017\sum_{j=0}^6 L_j^P(s_0)(\pi a_j^*)^2
       <0.006.
\end{aligned}$$ For the direct part we used $\sum_{j=1}^6 M_j(\pi t_j)^2/6<77$; for the transformed part we used the all-node coefficient norm less than $2.3$ from Proposition <a href="#interpolation:solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="interpolation:solution">9</a>.

It remains to give the quadratic lower bound for $P$.

<a id="stmt-signs-p-lower-bound"></a>
<div id="signs:P-lower-bound" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11**. *If $m\in12\mathbb Z+A$ is a nearest zero of $P$ to a real number $s$, then $$P(s)\ge0.68(s-m)^2.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-signs-p-lower-bound"><summary>Proof of stmt-signs-p-lower-bound</summary>

*Proof.* On either half-gap from $m$, consider $P(m+u)/u^2$, with value $Q_m$ at $u=0$. Put $a=\pi/12$. For a sine factor whose residue is $a'\ne m$ modulo $12$, the second derivative of its logarithm is $-2a^2\csc^2(a(m+u-a'))<0$. These factors stay nonzero on the half-gap. The factor vanishing at $m$, after division by $u^2$, has the same logarithmic concavity because $$\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm d^2}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm du^2}\log\left\lvert\frac{\sin(au)}{u}\right\rvert
 =\frac1{u^2}-\frac{a^2}{\sin^2(au)}\le0.$$ Here $\left\lvert\sin(au)\right\rvert\le\left\lvert au\right\rvert$, and the logarithm has a smooth limiting value at $u=0$. Squaring the divided sine only multiplies this second derivative by two. The quotient is therefore logarithmically concave on each half-gap, including its continuous value at the center. Its minimum is bounded below by the smaller of its endpoint values. At the center $Q_m>1.75$. At the gap midpoints, direct substitution gives the values listed below; periodicity reduces the calculation to one period. To evaluate them, put $\theta=\pi(s-2)/12$ and $c=\cos(2\theta)$. The identity $4\sin v\sin w=2(\cos(v-w)-\cos(v+w))$ shows that the paired unsquared factors for residues $(0,4)$, $(1,3)$, and $(7,9)$ are, respectively, $$1-2c,\qquad \sqrt3-2c,\qquad \sqrt3+2c.$$ Consequently $$P(s)=(4c^2-3)^2(1-2c)^2,
 \qquad c=\cos\frac{\pi(s-2)}6,$$ which evaluates every entry directly.

<div class="center">

| Gap between consecutive zeros |  Midpoint quotient $P(s)/(s-m)^2$  |
|:-----------------------------:|:----------------------------------:|
|        $[0,1],\ [3,4]$        |            $12-8\sqrt2$            |
|            $[1,3]$            |                $1$                 |
|            $[7,9]$            |                $9$                 |
|       $[4,7],\ [9,12]$        | $\dfrac89(3+\sqrt2+\sqrt3+\sqrt6)$ |

</div>

The distances to the two ends of a gap agree at its midpoint, so this table supplies the endpoint value for both half-gaps. The smallest value is $12-8\sqrt2>0.68$: indeed $(283/200)^2>2$, so $\sqrt2<283/200$ and $12-8(283/200)=0.68$. The other three values are at least $1$. This proves the claim. ◻

</details>

<a id="signs:tail-margin"></a>
For any $s\ge41.5$, choose a nearest node $m\ge43$; at $s=41.5$, choose $43$ rather than the tied node $40$. Both $H_i$ and $P R_{i,0}$ vanish to order at least two at $m$, by <a href="#fourier:interpolation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:interpolation">[fourier:interpolation]</a> and the absence of a pole of $R_{i,0}$ there. Thus $E_i=H_i-P R_{i,0}$ vanishes to order at least two at $m$. The segment between $s$ and $m$ lies in $[41.5,\infty)$, and <a href="#signs:tail-second-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:tail-second-derivative">[signs:tail-second-derivative]</a> gives $$\left\lvert E_i(s)\right\rvert\le\frac{0.006}{2}(s-m)^2.$$ Using <a href="#signs:rational-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:rational-tail">[signs:rational-tail]</a> and Lemma <a href="#signs:P-lower-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:P-lower-bound">11</a>, we obtain on the entire unbounded region $$\label{signs:tail-margin}
 \sigma_iH_i(s)
 \ge\left(0.011\cdot0.68-\frac{0.006}{2}\right)(s-m)^2
 =0.00448(s-m)^2\ge0.$$

<a id="stmt-signs-global"></a>
<div id="signs:global" class="proposition">

<a id="signs:zero-sets"></a>
**Proposition 12** (Global signs and zeros). *The exact functions have the zero sets $$\label{signs:zero-sets}
 \{s\ge1:H_1(s)=0\}=\mathcal N,\qquad
 \{s\ge0:H_2(s)=0\}=\mathcal N,$$ and satisfy the strict inequalities $$H_1(s)<0\quad(s\ge1,\ s\notin\mathcal N),\qquad
 H_2(s)>0\quad(s\ge0,\ s\notin\mathcal N).$$ The zero of $H_1$ at $1$ is simple. Its zeros at $n\in\mathcal N$ with $n>1$, and the zeros of $H_2$ at every $n\in\mathcal N$, have order exactly two as zeros of the entire functions of $s$. Consequently $F(x)\le0$ when $b\left\lvert x\right\rvert^2\ge1$, and $\widehat F(\xi)\ge0$ for every $\xi\in\mathbb R^2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-signs-global"><summary>Proof of stmt-signs-global</summary>

*Proof.* At every node $n\in\mathcal N$, the exact interpolation conditions give $H_1(n)=H_2(n)=0$. Away from the nodes, the quotient bounds <a href="#signs:finite-quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:finite-quotient">[signs:finite-quotient]</a> are strict on the finite region, and <a href="#signs:tail-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:tail-margin">[signs:tail-margin]</a> is strict on the remaining region. This proves <a href="#signs:zero-sets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:zero-sets">[signs:zero-sets]</a> and the asserted strict signs.

The interpolation slope $H_1'(1)=-Q_1\ne0$ makes its zero at $1$ simple. For $H_2$ at every node in $\mathcal N\cap[1,40]$, and for $H_1$ at those nodes greater than $1$, the chosen order is $\nu=2$. The endpoint value in <a href="#signs:finite-quotient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:finite-quotient">[signs:finite-quotient]</a> is then $\sigma_iH_i''(m)/2>0$, so the zero has order exactly two. For a node $m\in\mathcal N$ with $m\ge43$, take $u$ sufficiently small that $m$ remains the nearest node to $m+u$ and $m+u\ge41.5$. The exact value and first derivative at $m$ vanish, so <a href="#signs:tail-margin" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="signs:tail-margin">[signs:tail-margin]</a> yields $$\frac{\sigma_iH_i''(m)}2
 =\lim_{u\to0}\frac{\sigma_iH_i(m+u)}{u^2}
 \ge0.00448>0.$$ These zeros also have order exactly two. Finally, the factors $e^{-\pi h s}$ in $F$ and $\widehat F$ are positive, which gives their non-strict sign inequalities. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:normalization"></a>
### Poisson normalization

The prescribed first-shell derivative determines the remaining scalar normalization. Poisson summation first equates the values at the origin; its differentiated dilation identity then proves that their common value is positive, without estimating that value numerically.

<a id="normalization:jets"></a>
Let the exact coefficient lists be those of Proposition <a href="#interpolation:solution" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="interpolation:solution">9</a>, and put $$G_i(s)=e^{-\pi hs}H_i(s),\qquad F(x)=G_1(b|x|^2).$$ Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a> proves that $F$ is real, radial, and Schwartz, with $\widehat F(\xi)=G_2(b|\xi|^2)$. The interpolation conditions give $$\label{normalization:jets}
 \begin{split}
 G_1(n)&=G_2(n)=0,\\
 G_2'(n)&=0,\qquad
 G_1'(n)=-Q_1e^{-\pi h}\mathbf1_{\{n=1\}}
 \qquad(n\in\mathcal N).
 \end{split}$$ Indeed, $G_i'(n)=e^{-\pi hn}H_i'(n)$ because $H_i(n)=0$.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The dilation identity

Use the lattice $\Lambda$ of covolume one and the shell coordinate $s_a$ from <a href="#fourier:lattice" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:lattice">[fourier:lattice]</a>–<a href="#fourier:shell-coordinate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:shell-coordinate">[fourier:shell-coordinate]</a>. Every positive $s_a$ lies in $\mathcal N$. Exactly six lattice vectors have $s_a=1$: from $4(j^2+jk+k^2)=(2j+k)^2+3k^2=4$, one obtains $$(j,k)=(1,0),\ (-1,0),\ (0,1),\ (0,-1),\ (1,-1),\ (-1,1).$$

<a id="normalization:poisson"></a>
As shown in Section <a href="#sec:fourier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:fourier">2</a>, $\Lambda^*=\mathcal R\Lambda$. Apply Poisson summation on $\Lambda$ to $F_t(x)=F(\sqrt t\,x)$, whose Fourier transform is $t^{-1}\widehat F(\xi/\sqrt t)$. Radiality makes the sums over $\Lambda^*$ and $\Lambda$ equal, and gives, for every $t>0$, $$\label{normalization:poisson}
 \sum_{a\in\Lambda}G_1(ts_a)
 =\frac1t\sum_{a\in\Lambda}G_2(s_a/t).$$

We will differentiate this identity twice. Here is the needed convergence justification. On a compact subinterval of $(0,\infty)$, each of the first two $t$ derivatives of $F(\sqrt t\,x)$ is a finite sum of spatial derivatives of $F$ multiplied by polynomials in $x$ and bounded functions of $t$. The same statement holds for $t^{-1}\widehat F(x/\sqrt t)$. The Schwartz bounds therefore imply, for every $M$, a common bound $C_M(1+|x|)^{-M}$ for these functions and their first two $t$ derivatives. The number of points of a fixed planar lattice in a radius-$R$ disk is $O(R^2)$, so $\sum_{a\in\Lambda}(1+|a|)^{-M}$ converges for $M>2$. Uniform convergence on compact $t$ intervals now permits both termwise differentiations of <a href="#normalization:poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="normalization:poisson">[normalization:poisson]</a>.

<a id="stmt-normalization-origin-lemma"></a>
<div id="normalization:origin-lemma" class="lemma">

**Lemma 13** (Exact origin value). *The Fourier pair constructed above satisfies <a id="normalization:origin"></a>$$\label{normalization:origin}
 F(0)=\widehat F(0)=6Q_1e^{-\pi h}>0.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-normalization-origin-lemma"><summary>Proof of stmt-normalization-origin-lemma</summary>

*Proof.* At $t=1$, all nonzero summands in <a href="#normalization:poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="normalization:poisson">[normalization:poisson]</a> vanish by <a href="#normalization:jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="normalization:jets">[normalization:jets]</a>. Hence $G_1(0)=G_2(0)$. The derivative of its left side at $1$ is $$\sum_{a\in\Lambda}s_aG_1'(s_a)=-6Q_1e^{-\pi h},$$ because only the six vectors on the first shell contribute. On the right, $$\left.\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm d}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt}\left(t^{-1}G_2(s/t)\right)\right|_{t=1}
 =-G_2(s)-sG_2'(s).$$ For $s=s_a>0$, both terms vanish by the exact jets, while $s_a=0$ contributes $-G_2(0)$. Equality of the derivatives gives $G_2(0)=6Q_1e^{-\pi h}$. The positivity follows from $Q_1>0$ in <a href="#fourier:node-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-bounds">[fourier:node-bounds]</a>. This calculation uses the Fourier pairing and the exact interpolation conditions, but not the global sign estimates. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### The final dilation

<details id="proof-stmt-normalization-origin-lemma-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-normalization-origin-lemma</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#intro:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="intro:main">1</a>.* Define <a id="normalization:scaling"></a>$$\label{normalization:scaling}
 f(x)=\frac{F(x/\sqrt b)}{bF(0)},\qquad
 \widehat f(\xi)=\frac{\widehat F(\sqrt b\,\xi)}{F(0)}.$$ The second identity is the two-dimensional Fourier scaling rule. Since $F(0)>0$, the dilation and normalization preserve real-valuedness, radiality, and Schwartz regularity. They give $\widehat f(0)=1$ and $f(0)=1/b=2/\sqrt3$. By Proposition <a href="#signs:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:global">12</a> and the positive factor $e^{-\pi hs}$, $G_2(s)=e^{-\pi hs}H_2(s)\ge0$ for $s\ge0$ and $G_1(s)=e^{-\pi hs}H_1(s)\le0$ for $s\ge1$. Thus $\widehat f\ge0$ everywhere. If $|x|\ge1$, then $b|x/\sqrt b|^2=|x|^2\ge1$, so $f(x)\le0$. These are exactly the four assertions of <a href="#intro:conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="intro:conditions">[intro:conditions]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="normalization:zero-sets"></a>
The scaling also records the prescribed contact data in the final radial coordinates. Write $f_{\mathrm{rad}}(r)=f(r,0)$ and $\widehat f_{\mathrm{rad}}(\rho)=\widehat f(\rho,0)$ for $r,\rho\ge0$. Then $$f_{\mathrm{rad}}(r)=\frac{G_1(r^2)}{bF(0)},\qquad
 \widehat f_{\mathrm{rad}}(\rho)=\frac{G_2(b^2\rho^2)}{F(0)}.$$ Proposition <a href="#signs:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:global">12</a> therefore gives the exact zero sets in the respective sign domains: $$\label{normalization:zero-sets}
 \begin{aligned}
 \{r^2:r\ge1,\ f_{\mathrm{rad}}(r)=0\}&=\mathcal N,\\
 \{\rho^2:\rho\ge0,\ \widehat f_{\mathrm{rad}}(\rho)=0\}
   &=b^{-2}\mathcal N=\frac43\mathcal N.
 \end{aligned}$$ The physical zero at $r=1$ is simple, every other listed physical zero is exactly double, and every listed Fourier zero is exactly double. Indeed, the Gaussian and scalar factors do not vanish, while the changes of variable $s=r^2$ and $s=b^2\rho^2$ have nonzero derivatives at these positive radii, so they preserve the orders established for $H_1,H_2$. At the exclusion radius, the radial derivative is <a id="normalization:radial-slope"></a>$$\label{normalization:radial-slope}
 f_{\mathrm{rad}}'(1)
 =\frac{2G_1'(1)}{bF(0)}=-\frac{1}{3b}=-\frac{2}{3\sqrt3}.$$

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The packing consequence

The relation between the signs and packing density is particularly visible for a periodic packing. Suppose the center set is $\bigcup_{j=1}^N(L+t_j)$, where $L$ has covolume $V$, the $t_j$ are distinct modulo $L$, and distinct centers have distance at least one. Absolute convergence follows from the Schwartz bounds. Every summand below except the $N$ diagonal terms at the origin is nonpositive, so $$\mathcal S:=\sum_{j,k=1}^N\sum_{v\in L}f(v+t_j-t_k)\le Nf(0).$$ Poisson summation on each translated lattice gives $$\mathcal S=\frac1V\sum_{w\in L^*}\widehat f(w)
       \left|\sum_{j=1}^N e^{2\pi i w\cdot t_j}\right|^2
 \ge\frac{N^2}{V}\widehat f(0),$$ because every Fourier summand is nonnegative. It follows that $N/V\le f(0)/\widehat f(0)=2/\sqrt3$. The disks have area $\pi/4$, hence their covered-area density is at most $\pi/(2\sqrt3)$.

For an arbitrary, not necessarily periodic, packing, the same implication is Theorem 3.1 of Cohn and Elkies (Cohn and Elkies 2003) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[CohnElkies2003](#ref-CohnElkies2003)] <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[CohnElkies2003](#ref-CohnElkies2003)]. Its admissibility hypotheses hold because both $f$ and $\widehat f$ are Schwartz. For disks of radius $1/2$, the Cohn–Elkies center-density normalization is one quarter of the center-number density in their upper-density convention. Multiplying it by the unit-disk area $\pi$ therefore gives the corresponding covered-area density, equivalently $\pi/4$ times that center-number density. Their upper density takes a supremum over observation centers, so it bounds the fixed-origin limsup used in the introduction. For the fixed-origin convention and separated centers, the covered area inside $B_R$ lies between $(\pi/4)$ times the number of centers in $B_{R-1/2}$ and $(\pi/4)$ times the number in $B_{R+1/2}$; division by $\pi R^2$ and passage to the limsup matches the convention in the introduction. The lattice generated by $(1,0)$ and $(1/2,b)$ has one center per area $b$ and attains this value. This is the classical packing-density theorem recovered from the new Fourier certificate, not a new density claim.

The restriction of the physical zero set to integer squared radii also recovers the classical periodic equality case. We use the even integral lattice argument of Cohn and Elkies (Cohn and Elkies 2003, sec. 8).

<a id="stmt-normalization-periodic-uniqueness"></a>
<div id="normalization:periodic-uniqueness" class="corollary">

**Corollary 14** (Periodic equality case). *If a periodic packing of disks of radius $1/2$ has covered-area density $\pi/(2\sqrt3)$, then its center set is the image of $\mathbb Z(1,0)+\mathbb Z(1/2,b)$ under a Euclidean isometry.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-normalization-periodic-uniqueness"><summary>Proof of stmt-normalization-periodic-uniqueness</summary>

*Proof.* Write the center set as $\mathcal C=\bigcup_{j=1}^N(L+t_j)$, with the notation of the periodic packing argument above. Equality of its density with $\pi/(2\sqrt3)$ gives $N/V=f(0)/\widehat f(0)$. Thus both inequalities in the Poisson comparison are equalities. Absolute convergence permits us to write the defect in the physical inequality as $$0=Nf(0)-\mathcal S
 =\sum_{\substack{1\le j,k\le N,\ v\in L\\
                   v\ne0\ \text{or}\ j\ne k}}
       -f(v+t_j-t_k).$$ Every summand is nonnegative, so every one is zero. Any difference of distinct centers occurs in this sum, and <a href="#normalization:zero-sets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="normalization:zero-sets">[normalization:zero-sets]</a> therefore gives $$|x-y|^2\in\mathcal N\subset\mathbb Z
 \qquad(x,y\in\mathcal C,\ x\ne y).$$ Only the physical zero set is used here.

Translate one center to the origin and dilate by $\sqrt2$. Put $$L_0=\sqrt2\,L,\qquad
 \mathcal C_0=\sqrt2\,(\mathcal C-t_1),\qquad
 \Gamma=\operatorname{span}_{\mathbb Z}\mathcal C_0.$$ Here $\Gamma$ consists of finite integer linear combinations of the scaled centers, and $L_0\subset\mathcal C_0$ because the first coset becomes $L_0$. All squared distances in $\mathcal C_0$ are even integers. Since $0\in\mathcal C_0$, polarization gives $$\langle x,y\rangle
 =\frac{|x|^2+|y|^2-|x-y|^2}{2}\in\mathbb Z
 \qquad(x,y\in\mathcal C_0).$$ Integer linear combinations preserve these integral pairings, and their squared norms remain even because the cross terms carry a factor of two. Thus every pairing in $\Gamma$ is integral and every squared norm in $\Gamma$ is even.

In particular, pairing with a basis of $L_0$ shows $L_0\subset\Gamma\subset L_0^*$. Both $L_0$ and $L_0^*$ are lattices of rank two, so $L_0^*/L_0$ is finite. Hence $\Gamma$ is a finite union of $L_0$ cosets and is itself a lattice of rank two. Put $d:=[\Gamma:L_0]$. A basis of $\Gamma$ has a Gram matrix $$\begin{pmatrix}a&m\\m&c\end{pmatrix},
 \qquad a,c\in2\mathbb Z_{>0},\quad m\in\mathbb Z.$$ Its determinant $ac-m^2=\operatorname{covol}(\Gamma)^2$ is a positive integer congruent to $0$ or $3$ modulo $4$. It is therefore at least $3$, and $\operatorname{covol}(\Gamma)\ge\sqrt3$. On the other hand, $\mathcal C_0$ consists of $N$ distinct $L_0$ cosets, all among the $d$ cosets of $\Gamma$. Since $\operatorname{covol}(L_0)=2V$, we obtain $$\sqrt3\le\operatorname{covol}(\Gamma)
 =\frac{2V}{d}\le\frac{2V}{N}=\sqrt3.$$ Equality holds throughout, so $d=N$ and the two sets of cosets coincide: $\mathcal C_0=\Gamma$.

It remains to identify this lattice of Gram determinant $3$. Choose a shortest nonzero vector $w_1\in\Gamma$ and extend it to a lattice basis $w_1,w_2$; this is possible because a shortest vector is primitive. Put $a=|w_1|^2$, and replace $w_2$ by an integer translate along $w_1$ so that $m=\langle w_1,w_2\rangle$ satisfies $|m|\le a/2$. With $c=|w_2|^2\ge a$ and the even integer $a\ge2$, $$3=ac-m^2\ge\frac34a^2.$$ Hence $a=2$. The equation $2c-m^2=3$, with $c$ even and $|m|\le1$, forces $c=2$ and $m=\pm1$. This is the Gram matrix of the triangular lattice with shortest length $\sqrt2$. Scaling back by $1/\sqrt2$ proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### A second-derivative consistency identity

The same dilation identity contains an additional relation among the exact curvatures. It is not needed for the construction or its sign proof. For $n\in\mathcal N$, let $r_\Lambda(n)=\#\{a\in\Lambda:s_a=n\}$, allowing the value zero for nodes that are not lattice radii; in particular, $r_\Lambda(1)=6$.

<a id="stmt-normalization-second-poisson"></a>
<div id="normalization:second-poisson" class="proposition">

<a id="normalization:curvature-identity"></a>
<a id="normalization:curvature-inequality"></a>
**Proposition 15** (Second differentiated Poisson identity). *The exact pair satisfies the absolutely convergent identity $$\label{normalization:curvature-identity}
 \sum_{n\in\mathcal N}r_\Lambda(n)n^2
       \bigl(G_1''(n)-G_2''(n)\bigr)=2G_2(0).$$ Using the global signs, it follows that $$\label{normalization:curvature-inequality}
 H_1''(1)-H_2''(1)\ge2(1-\pi h)Q_1.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-normalization-second-poisson"><summary>Proof of stmt-normalization-second-poisson</summary>

*Proof.* The convergence argument above permits a second differentiation of <a href="#normalization:poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="normalization:poisson">[normalization:poisson]</a>. At $t=1$, $$\left.\frac{\mathop{}\!\mathrm d^2}{\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt^2}\left(t^{-1}G_2(s/t)\right)\right|_{t=1}
 =2G_2(s)+4sG_2'(s)+s^2G_2''(s).$$ On the left the corresponding term is $s^2G_1''(s)$. At every positive node, the first two terms on the right vanish by <a href="#normalization:jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="normalization:jets">[normalization:jets]</a>; at the origin they contribute $2G_2(0)$. Grouping the remaining absolutely convergent lattice sums by their squared radii proves <a href="#normalization:curvature-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="normalization:curvature-identity">[normalization:curvature-identity]</a>.

For a positive node $n>1$, $G_1$ has a local maximum equal to zero and $G_2$ has a local minimum equal to zero, by Proposition <a href="#signs:global" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:global">12</a> and $e^{-\pi hs}>0$. Therefore $G_1''(n)\le0\le G_2''(n)$. All terms in <a href="#normalization:curvature-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="normalization:curvature-identity">[normalization:curvature-identity]</a> beyond the first shell are nonpositive. Since $r_\Lambda(1)=6$ and $G_2(0)=6Q_1e^{-\pi h}$, $$G_1''(1)-G_2''(1)\ge2Q_1e^{-\pi h}.$$ Finally, differentiating $G_i=e^{-\pi hs}H_i$ twice and inserting the exact value and slope at $1$ gives $$G_1''(1)-G_2''(1)
 =e^{-\pi h}\bigl(H_1''(1)-H_2''(1)+2\pi hQ_1\bigr).$$ This yields <a href="#normalization:curvature-inequality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="normalization:curvature-inequality">[normalization:curvature-inequality]</a>. Unlike the origin calculation, this last inequality is conditional on the global signs that were proved in Section <a href="#sec:signs" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:signs">4</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:certificates"></a>
### Finite certificates

This appendix completes the finite evidence used in Lemma <a href="#interpolation:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="interpolation:finite">7</a> and Lemma <a href="#signs:bernstein" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:bernstein">10</a>. We give the exact inputs, prove a uniform error bound for the finite quadrature sums, and state the interval comparisons for the matrices, residuals, and Bernstein coefficients. The final subsection records the scalar substitutions used for the infinite tails and continuum signs. All terminating decimals denote exact rational numbers.

The primary calculation uses interval evaluation of the $N=256$ quadrature sums, enlarged by the analytic moment remainder proved below. It therefore reduces the required numerical evidence to bounded finite sums, without assuming convergence of a numerical integration or optimization procedure. The infinite inverse and the signs between and beyond interpolation nodes remain consequences of the analytic arguments in the main text. Appendix <a href="#ind:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:section">7</a> gives the rational alternatives and their separately stated scope.

<a id="cert:inputs"></a>
#### Exact input lists and matrices

<a id="cert:fixed-inputs"></a>
<a id="cert:table-norms"></a>
Table <a href="#tab:coefficients" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="tab:coefficients">2</a> gives the coefficients at every node of $I_f=\mathcal N\cap[1,100]$. Its four coefficient columns are multiplied by $10^{10}$: an integer $a$ in the table means the coefficient $a\,10^{-10}$. At all nodes $n>100$ both approximate lists are zero. The data at zero and the constant terms are, exactly, $$\label{cert:fixed-inputs}
 (\widetilde c_{1,0},\widetilde d_{1,0},C_1)
   =(1,.44,-.013),\qquad
 (\widetilde c_{2,0},\widetilde d_{2,0},C_2)
   =(0,-.368,.017).$$ The zero-node data are included in all evaluations of the tabulated functions. They are excluded when forming a column of the operator $S$. Direct integer addition gives $$\label{cert:table-norms}
\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{n\ge0}(|\widetilde c_{1,n}|+|\widetilde d_{1,n}|)
   &=2.2516666247<2.29,\\
 \sum_{n\ge0}(|\widetilde c_{2,n}|+|\widetilde d_{2,n}|)
   &=.8927477314<2.29,\\
 \sum_{n>40}(|\widetilde c_{1,n}|+|\widetilde d_{1,n}|)
   &=.0000197471<.00002,\\
 \sum_{n>40}(|\widetilde c_{2,n}|+|\widetilde d_{2,n}|)
   &=.0000161623<.00002.
\end{aligned}$$ Here and below an index $n\ge0$ in a coefficient sum ranges over $\{0\}\cup\mathcal N$.

<div id="tab:coefficients">

|                                                    $n$ | $10^{10}\widetilde c_{1,n}$ | $10^{10}\widetilde d_{1,n}$ | $10^{10}\widetilde c_{2,n}$ | $10^{10}\widetilde d_{2,n}$ |
|-------------------------------------------------------:|----------------------------:|----------------------------:|----------------------------:|----------------------------:|
|                                     Table  (continued) |                             |                             |                             |                             |
|                                                    $n$ | $10^{10}\widetilde c_{1,n}$ | $10^{10}\widetilde d_{1,n}$ | $10^{10}\widetilde c_{2,n}$ | $10^{10}\widetilde d_{2,n}$ |
|                             Continued on the next page |                             |                             |                             |                             |
| 1 & -670689128 & -6398628799 & -250018302 & 4107732082 |                             |                             |                             |                             |
