</div>

Write $J=\{1,3,4\}$ and $E=I_f\setminus J$. Matrix rows and columns on a node set always list all $c$ entries first and all $d$ entries second, with nodes increasing within each group. In particular the order on $J$ is $(c_1,c_3,c_4,d_1,d_3,d_4)$. The two rational matrices used to certify the small inverses are <a id="cert:Wplus"></a>$$\label{cert:Wplus}
 W_+=10^{-4}\begin{pmatrix}
 \input{data/W_plus.tex}
 \end{pmatrix},$$ <a id="cert:Wminus"></a>$$\label{cert:Wminus}
 W_-=10^{-4}\begin{pmatrix}
 \input{data/W_minus.tex}
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ These matrices are exact inputs. The $W$-based inverse argument below uses their products and column sums; it does not require a computed matrix inverse. We describe the supplied program’s additional inverse checks in Section <a href="#cert:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:evaluation">6.3</a>.

<a id="cert:quadrature"></a>
#### Moments and the integration rule

<a id="cert:moments"></a>
For a finite input $p=PR$ supported on nodes at most $100$, put $$\label{cert:moments}
 U_{p,l}(m,y)=\frac{y^l p^{(l)}(m)}{l!},\qquad
 X_{p,l}(m,y)=\frac{y^l K_p^{(l)}(m)}{l!}.$$ The domain for the quadrature error estimate is stated explicitly in Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a>.

<a id="cert:rule"></a>
We use the first Fejér quadrature rule, whose cosine-sum weights are described in (Waldvogel 2006, sec. 2, formula (2.3)) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Waldvogel2006](#ref-Waldvogel2006)]. Our weights are half the mass-two weights there, normalized to average over an interval; for even $N=256$ the terminal cosine term in that formula vanishes. We prove the exactness, positivity and error bound needed here. Set $N=256$, and for $0\le v<N$ define $$\label{cert:rule}
\begin{aligned}
 \alpha_v&=\frac{v+1/2}{N},\qquad
 o_v=\frac1N\left(1-2\sum_{a=1}^{127}
             \frac{\cos(2a\pi\alpha_v)}{4a^2-1}\right),\\
 \tau_{jv}&=\frac{2j-1+\cos(\pi\alpha_v)}{12}
       \qquad(1\le j\le6).
\end{aligned}$$ Integration over $(t_{j-1},t_j)$ is replaced by evaluation at $\tau_{jv}$ with weights $o_v/6$. To check exactness, expand a polynomial in the Chebyshev basis $T_k(\cos\theta)=\cos(k\theta)$. Discrete cosine orthogonality at $\theta_v=\pi\alpha_v$ recovers its coefficients through degree $255$. The integral averages of this basis on $[-1,1]$ are zero at odd $k$ and $1/(1-k^2)$ at even $k$. These are exactly the coefficients in <a href="#cert:rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:rule">[cert:rule]</a>, so the rule is exact through degree $255$. Moreover, $$o_v\ge\frac1{256}\left(1-\sum_{a=1}^{127}\frac2{4a^2-1}\right)
     =\frac1{256\cdot255}>0,\qquad \sum_v o_v=1.$$ Thus the six pieces together have total weight one.

<a id="cert:density"></a>
<a id="cert:analytic-factors"></a>
For clarity, the complete density on piece $j$ used in this appendix is $$\label{cert:density}
 f_j(t)=2\pi\sum_n e^{-i\pi tn}
 \left\{-\pi c_n\sum_{l=j}^6(t_l-t)P_l e^{i\pi t_ln}
       +i d_n\sum_{l=j}^6 P_l e^{i\pi t_ln}\right\}.$$ It includes node $0$ when that coefficient is part of the input. The folded atoms have masses $e_jCP_j$ at $t_j$, where $e_0=1$ and $e_j=2$ for $j>0$. The factors multiplying this density and these masses for $U$ and $X$, respectively, are $$\label{cert:analytic-factors}
 e^{i\pi tm}\frac{(i\pi ty)^l}{l!},\qquad
 \lambda(t)e^{i\pi z(t)m}\frac{(i\pi z(t)y)^l}{l!}.$$ The real part is taken after summation.

<a id="stmt-cert-moment-error"></a>
<div id="cert:moment-error" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Quadrature moment error). *Let $p=PR$ have a real coefficient list supported in $\{0\}\cup I_f$, with $\|(c,d)\|_1\le3$, and let $C\in\mathbb R$ satisfy $|C|\le1$. Let $m,y\in\mathbb R$ and $l\in\{0,\ldots,30\}$ satisfy $0\le m\le100$ and $|y|\le3/2$. The quadrature rule <a href="#cert:rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:rule">[cert:rule]</a>, with exact evaluations at its nodes and at the atoms, has error less than $10^{-22}$ in each moment $U_{p,l}(m,y)$ and $X_{p,l}(m,y)$.*

</div>

This domain contains all basis inputs and the two tabulated inputs used below.

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-moment-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-moment-error</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\rho=1/6$, $c_j=(2j-1)/12$, $a_j=c_j+ih$, and $\Delta_j=|a_j|^2-\rho^2$. These last quantities increase from $\Delta_1=167/1200$ to $\Delta_6=389/400$, so the disk $|t-c_j|\le\rho$ does not contain the pole $-ih$. All factors in <a href="#cert:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:density">[cert:density]</a> and <a href="#cert:analytic-factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:analytic-factors">[cert:analytic-factors]</a> are analytic there. The reciprocal-disk calculation is exact: for $\zeta=1/(t+ih)$, $$|1-a_j\zeta|^2-\rho^2|\zeta|^2
 =\Delta_j\left|\zeta-\frac{\overline a_j}{\Delta_j}\right|^2
   -\frac{\rho^2}{\Delta_j}.$$ Thus inversion maps the disk for $t+ih$ onto the disk with center $(c_j-ih)/\Delta_j$ and radius $\rho/\Delta_j$. In particular, $$|\zeta|\le\frac{|a_j|+\rho}{\Delta_j}
       =\frac1{|a_j|-\rho}.$$ The right side decreases with $c_j$, and $|a_1|=\sqrt{601}/60$. Consequently the following are analytic bounds on every disk: $$\begin{aligned}
 |t|&\le\frac{13}{12}<1.1,\\
 |\lambda(t)|&\le\frac{120}{\sqrt3(\sqrt{601}-10)}<4.774<5,\\
 |z(t)|&\le\frac{80}{\sqrt{601}-10}+\frac25<5.912<6.2.
\end{aligned}$$ For the two finer comparisons, it suffices to square the rational lower bounds $1.732^2=2.999824<3$ and $24.515^2=600.985225<601$, and then use $$\begin{gathered}
 4.774(1.732)(24.515-10)=120.01826452>120,\\
 (5.912-.4)(24.515-10)=80.00668>80.
\end{gathered}$$ The image disk for $z=-B\zeta-ih$ has center $-B(c_j-ih)/\Delta_j-ih$ and radius $B\rho/\Delta_j$. Its lowest imaginary part is therefore $B(h-\rho)/\Delta_j-h$. Since $h>\rho$, this decreases with $\Delta_j$, giving <a id="cert:disk-imaginary"></a>$$\label{cert:disk-imaginary}
 \operatorname{Im}z(t)\ge
 B\frac{h-1/6}{c_j^2+h^2-(1/6)^2}-h
 \ge-\frac{1402}{17505}>-.081.$$ Since $\sum_{l=1}^6|P_l|=10$ and $|t_l-t|\le13/12$ for $l\ge j$, the analytic continuation of <a href="#cert:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:density">[cert:density]</a> on this disk is bounded by $$3\max\{2\pi^2\cdot10\cdot13/12,\,2\pi\cdot10\}
       e^{100\pi/6}<1000e^{100\pi/6}.$$ Use $|a^l/l!|\le e^{|a|}$ for the divided powers. Including all factors, the direct and transformed integrands are bounded, respectively, by $$\begin{aligned}
 1000e^{200\pi/6+1.5\pi\cdot1.1}&<5.38\cdot10^{50},\\
 5000e^{100\pi/6+100\pi\cdot.081+1.5\pi\cdot6.2}
       &<1.51\cdot10^{50}.
\end{aligned}$$ We may therefore use the common bound $M=10^{53}$. The real interval has half-length $1/12$, half the disk radius. Cauchy’s coefficient estimate bounds the tail of the Taylor polynomial of degree $255$ by $M\sum_{k=256}^{\infty}2^{-k}=M2^{-255}$. Exactness, positivity and total mass one for the integral and quadrature rule give discrepancy at most <a id="cert:quadrature-error"></a>$$\label{cert:quadrature-error}
 2^{-254}10^{53}<3.455\cdot10^{-24}<10^{-22}.$$ Atoms are evaluated separately and incur no quadrature error. ◻

</details>

<a id="cert:evaluation"></a>
#### Interval evaluation

<a id="cert:pair"></a>
For a finite input $p$ in the real finite-input domain of Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a> and a node row $m\in I_f$, form the two real numbers $$\label{cert:pair}
 \mathcal S_m(p)=
 \left(-\frac{X_{p,0}(m,1)}{Q_m},\;
 \frac{D_mX_{p,0}(m,1)-X_{p,1}(m,1)}{Q_m}\right).$$ With input $c_n=1$ or $d_n=1$ and all other data zero, these are the entries of the corresponding column of $S$ at row node $m$.

Let $D=S_{J,J}$ and $U=S_{J,E}$.

<a id="cert:power-from-moments"></a>
For the Bernstein certificates, use exactly the triples $(i,m,y)$ and orders $\nu$ specified in Lemma <a href="#signs:bernstein" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:bernstein">10</a>; in particular $y$ is signed and no leftward interval at $m=1$ is used for index $1$. Express its power coefficients in terms of the moments as $$\label{cert:power-from-moments}
 T_r=\sigma_i y^{-\nu}
 \left(U_{\widetilde p_i,r+\nu}(m,y)
             +X_{\widetilde p_{3-i},r+\nu}(m,y)\right).$$ The signed scaling is exact. Definition <a href="#cert:moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:moments">[cert:moments]</a> gives $U_{p,l}(m,y)=y^lU_{p,l}(m,1)$ and the same identity for $X$. Therefore, for positive or negative $y$, $$T_r=\sigma_i y^r\left(U_{\widetilde p_i,r+\nu}(m,1)
                 +X_{\widetilde p_{3-i},r+\nu}(m,1)\right).$$ This is the identity used when the implementation caches unscaled moments at $y=1$; it does not discard the orientation of a half-gap.

Equations <a href="#cert:rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:rule">[cert:rule]</a>, <a href="#cert:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:density">[cert:density]</a>, <a href="#cert:analytic-factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:analytic-factors">[cert:analytic-factors]</a>, <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a>, and <a href="#cert:power-from-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:power-from-moments">[cert:power-from-moments]</a> already specify all sums in the finite certificates below. The following pseudocode records their algebraic organization. A measure is a list of pairs $(t,g)$ in the order used by the supplied interval implementation: first the $6\cdot256$ quadrature pairs $(\tau_{jv},o_v f_j(\tau_{jv})/6)$, with $j=1,\ldots,6$ outermost and $v=0,\ldots,255$ innermost, and then the seven atom pairs $(t_j,e_jCP_j)$ for $j=0,\ldots,6$. The atom masses are zero for a basis input. The pseudocode is not a byte-for-byte execution trace. The supplied source fixes the detailed arithmetic and summation order for an actual interval run, including the factoring and caching described below.

```
Moments(measure, m, y, L):
    U[0..L] = 0; X[0..L] = 0
    for (t,g) in measure:
        lam = i/(b*(t+i*h))
        z = -B/(t+i*h)-i*h
        u = g*exp(i*pi*t*m)
        v = g*lam*exp(i*pi*z*m)
        for r = 0..L:
            U[r] += real(u); X[r] += real(v)
            u *= (i*pi*t*y)/(r+1)
            v *= (i*pi*z*y)/(r+1)
    return U,X

Pair(measure,m):
    U,X = Moments(measure,m,1,1)
    return -X[0]/Q[m], (D[m]*X[0]-X[1])/Q[m]

for basis column c[n] or d[n], n in I_f:
    measure = Measure(basis column; all other data zero)
    column = stack Pair(measure,m), m in J
    if n in J:
        also form rows m in E intersect [7,16]
    test column sums of W[eta] and the defect/product matrices

for i in {1,2}, m in I_f:
    c,d = Pair(Measure(full table input 3-i),m)
    test abs(table_c[i,m]-c)
    test abs(table_d[i,m]+indicator(i==1 and m==1)-d)

for (i,m,y,nu) in the prescribed half-gap cases:
    U,X = Moments for the two full table inputs, through L=30
    T[r] = sigma[i]*(U[i,r+nu]+X[3-i,r+nu])/y**nu
    for k = 0..28:
        Bk = T[0]; a = 1
        for r = 1..k:
            a *= (k+1-r)/(29-r)
            Bk += a*T[r]
        test the appropriate lower bound for Bk
```

Here `stack` means all first entries followed by all second entries. The recurrence for $a$ equals $\binom{k}{r}/\binom{28}{r}$. `Measure` denotes this finite list and introduces no additional approximation. The fixed data at zero are used only for the two full table inputs. Reusing a measure means reusing its whole finite list.

For the accompanying certified evaluation, every real or complex quantity in these sums is enclosed using interval arithmetic. The pair calculation forms the moments at $y=1$. For the Bernstein calculation, the implementation also forms and caches the unscaled moments at $y=1$, then uses the signed $y^r$ identity following <a href="#cert:power-from-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:power-from-moments">[cert:power-from-moments]</a>. Each unscaled real moment enclosure is enlarged by $[-10^{-22},10^{-22}]$, as justified by Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a>. Consequently the analytic-remainder contribution to the enclosure radius of a Bernstein coefficient is bounded by $$\begin{aligned}
 2\cdot10^{-22}\sum_{r=0}^k
   \frac{\binom{k}{r}}{\binom{28}{r}}|y|^r
 &\le2\cdot10^{-22}\sum_{r=0}^{28}(3/2)^r\\
 &=4\cdot10^{-22}\bigl((3/2)^{29}-1\bigr)<5.2\cdot10^{-17}.
\end{aligned}$$ The last inequality is an exact rational comparison. It is not correct to regard $10^{-22}$ as an unamplified per-Bernstein error. Arb rounding and dependency effects can further widen the balls; all of them are propagated by the subsequent products, absolute values, column sums, and Bernstein combinations. An upper bound is accepted only when the complete enclosure lies strictly below the asserted rational bound, and a lower bound only when it lies strictly above. This procedure uses enclosures of the exact integrals, not a quadrature error inferred from observed numerical agreement.

The ball-arithmetic inclusion methodology is described in (Johansson 2017) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Johansson2017](#ref-Johansson2017)]. The supplied implementation uses Arb through python-flint at $192$ bits of precision; the citation does not by itself certify this local environment, its call domains, or a run. The entry point runs two verifiers in the same directory, and . Their output records, and , and the aggregate record are placed in its subdirectory. The first verifier checks the exact bounds in <a href="#cert:matrix-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:matrix-table">[cert:matrix-table]</a>, <a href="#cert:exterior-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:exterior-block">[cert:exterior-block]</a>, <a href="#cert:residual-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:residual-bounds">[cert:residual-bounds]</a>, and <a href="#cert:bernstein-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:bernstein-table">[cert:bernstein-table]</a>, as well as all 2436 individual Bernstein inequalities in Lemma <a href="#signs:bernstein" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:bernstein">10</a>. It also asserts four finite brackets: the two defect brackets, the exterior bracket, and the bracket for $(2,16,-1/2,28)$. All its comparisons and extrema use complete enclosures.

The mathematical $W$ argument in the next subsection uses only products and column sums. The supplied program also calls a certified inverse for $I-\eta D$ and tests its inverse and cross-product norms as additional checks; these calls are not needed for the $W$ deduction.

They refer to the exact operator entries in <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a>. $$\label{cert:matrix-table}
\begin{array}{c|cc|ccc}

 [.00025422624910652544,\ .00025422624910652546],\\

The three printed product ranges partition $E$, so $\|W_\eta U\|$ is the maximum of the three grouped product norms. The exact bounds in <a href="#cert:matrix-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:matrix-table">[cert:matrix-table]</a> and <a href="#cert:exterior-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:exterior-block">[cert:exterior-block]</a> give the primary matrix conclusion directly. Put $A_\eta=I-\eta D$. Since $\|I-W_\eta A_\eta\|<.001$, the finite square matrix $W_\eta A_\eta$ is invertible by a geometric series, hence so is $A_\eta$, and $$A_\eta^{-1}=(W_\eta A_\eta)^{-1}W_\eta.$$ It follows from <a href="#cert:matrix-table" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:matrix-table">[cert:matrix-table]</a> that $$\|A_\eta^{-1}\|<\frac{30.84}{1-.001}<32,\qquad
 \|A_\eta^{-1}U\|<\frac{3.54}{1-.001}<3.7.$$ Equation <a href="#cert:exterior-block" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:exterior-block">[cert:exterior-block]</a> gives the remaining bound $<.09$ in Lemma <a href="#interpolation:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="interpolation:finite">7</a>.

\begin{aligned}

      &<3\cdot10^{-10},\\

       +\frac{X_{\widetilde p_{3-i},1}(m,1)

Since $3\cdot10^{-10}<10^{-9}$, these inequalities, the preceding primary matrix conclusion, and <a href="#cert:table-norms" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:table-norms">[cert:table-norms]</a> prove every finite assertion of Lemma <a href="#interpolation:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="interpolation:finite">7</a>.

#### Scalar envelope and sign bounds

The remaining finite substitutions involve elementary real expressions. For the endpoint and density envelopes in <a href="#interpolation:piece-envelopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:piece-envelopes">[interpolation:piece-envelopes]</a>, it suffices to test the finitely many endpoints and residues specified there. The endpoint formulas for $|\lambda|$, $|z|$, and $\operatorname{Im}z$ use rational numbers and square roots. The density expressions additionally use sine and cosine at rational multiples of $\pi$. The endpoint tests are certified by the same elementary enclosures used above; their monotonicity and convexity extensions to whole intervals were proved in Section <a href="#sec:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interpolation">3</a>.

\begin{array}{c|c}
 (m,Y,\nu)&\text{upper bound}\\ \hline
 (0,1/2,0)&1.578\cdot10^{-10}\\
 (1,1,1)&2.007\cdot10^{-5}\\

All the other displayed scalar bounds follow by substitution and outward enclosure of the explicit exponential, trigonometric and rational expressions. The scalar verifier records 22 named strict comparisons: the four row substitutions, the atom tail, the nine sign-table rows, the five Taylor representatives, and the three disk-integrand and quadrature comparisons. It also checks the endpoint and residue envelopes, the coarser row-tail comparisons, the finite-inverse substitution giving a bound below $90$, and the distance substitution using $91$ and $2540$. The derivation of these whole-space coefficients from the Schur estimates remains the analytic argument in Section <a href="#sec:interpolation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:interpolation">3</a>. Its disk assertions use the coarser bounds $5$ and $6.2$ for $|\lambda|$ and $|z|$; the finer $4.774$ and $5.912$ bounds were proved analytically above and are not literal scalar-program assertions. At the six gap midpoints the program evaluates the product defining $P$ and checks that each quotient is greater than $.68$; it does not assert the symbolic radical identities for those values. No sampling of an unknown function between interpolation nodes is involved. The logarithmic concavity argument and exact midpoint values in Lemma <a href="#signs:P-lower-bound" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:P-lower-bound">11</a> supply the global lower bound for $P$.

<a id="ind:section"></a>
### Alternative certification methods

The primary certificate does not use the methods in this appendix. We develop several ways to assess or reproduce its finite quantities. The first gives a fixed rational evaluation of every $N=256$ moment and proves a uniform error theorem. A separate exact-rational program checks the first $6\times6$ block with an $N=64$ rule. Rational-center Taylor integration gives a different, arbitrarily refinable enclosure method, and the final proposition gives a local formula for the direct cardinal derivatives.

All terminating decimals denote exact rational numbers. The full $N=256$ rational recipe and the Taylor method specify computations and their error bounds without asserting unreported finite values. The $N=64$ program has the narrower scope stated with its proof.

<a id="cert:rational-primitives"></a>
#### Rational exponential primitives

<a id="cert:estar"></a>
The fixed rational procedures below use rational arithmetic alone, with a complex number represented by a pair of rationals. Put $$\begin{aligned}
 p_*&=3.141592653589793238462643383279502884,\\
 b_*&=.866025403784438646763723170752936183,
 \end{aligned}$$ and retain $B=4/3$ and $h=2/5$ exactly. Both $|p_*-\pi|$ and $|b_*-b|$ are less than $10^{-36}$. For example, the identity $\pi/4=4\arctan(1/5)-\arctan(1/239)$ and alternating series through power $57$ certify the first assertion; comparing $(b_*\pm10^{-36})^2$ with $3/4$ certifies the second. Define $$[w]_{40}=10^{-40}\lfloor10^{40}w\rfloor$$ for real $w$, and apply it componentwise to complex numbers. Define an approximation $e_*(u)$ to $e^{i\pi u}$ by the following finite recursion: $$\label{cert:estar}
 v_0=\left[\sum_{q=0}^{32}\frac{(ip_*u/1024)^q}{q!}\right]_{40},
 \qquad v_{a+1}=[v_a^2]_{40}\ (0\le a<10),\qquad e_*(u)=v_{10}.$$ Real and imaginary parts of $e_*(u)$ give the cosine and sine replacements when $u$ is real.

<a id="stmt-cert-rounded-exponential"></a>
<div id="cert:rounded-exponential" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (Rounded exponential). *For every $u\in\mathbb Q(i)$ satisfying $\Re(ip_*u)\le0$ and $|p_*u|\le1000$, the recursion <a href="#cert:estar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:estar">[cert:estar]</a> satisfies $$|e_*(u)-e^{ip_*u}|<3.542\cdot10^{-34}<10^{-32}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-rounded-exponential"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-rounded-exponential</summary>

*Proof.* The reduced argument has modulus less than one, so the Taylor remainder in the first step of <a href="#cert:estar" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:estar">[cert:estar]</a> is less than $3/33!$. A componentwise floor contributes less than $2\cdot10^{-40}$ in complex absolute value. Each exact exponential subsequently squared has modulus at most one. Thus the recurrence $$e_0=3/33!+2\cdot10^{-40},\qquad
 e_{a+1}=2e_a+e_a^2+2\cdot10^{-40}$$ bounds the accumulated error. Exact rational evaluation gives $e_{10}<3.542\cdot10^{-34}$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
###### Positive exponential upper bounds.

<a id="ind:positive-exponential"></a>
For $x\ge0$ and an integer $n\ge0$ with $x<n+2$, define $$\label{ind:positive-exponential}
 \mathcal T_n(x)=\frac{x^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\frac1{1-x/(n+2)},\qquad
 \mathcal E_n(x)=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{x^k}{k!}+\mathcal T_n(x).$$ After the first omitted term, the ratio of each term to its predecessor is at most $x/(n+2)<1$, so $e^x\le\mathcal E_n(x)$. These are rational upper bounds when $x$ is rational. They will be used both in the fixed first-block check and in the arbitrary-refinement Taylor method.

<a id="cert:rational"></a>
#### A fixed rational evaluation

We now apply the fixed primitives to the full $N=256$ rule, using rational coefficients and rational $C,m,y$ in the finite domain of Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a>. After defining the rounded sums, we prove their uniform approximation error. No matrix, residual, or Bernstein values are asserted by that error theorem.

<a id="cert:lambda-z-star"></a>
In <a href="#cert:rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:rule">[cert:rule]</a>, replace each cosine $\cos(\pi u)$ by $\Re e_*(u)$, with $u=\alpha_v$ or $u=2a\alpha_v$ as appropriate, and then apply $[\ ]_{40}$ to the final weight $o_v$ only. Denote the results by $o_v^{\#},\tau_{jv}^{\#}$. In the displayed coefficient list for $P_l$, replace $b$ by $b_*$; call the resulting numbers $P_l^{\#}$. Evaluate <a href="#cert:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:density">[cert:density]</a> by replacing $\pi$ by $p_*$, $P_l$ by $P_l^{\#}$, and every exponential by $e_*$, at the approximate point $t=\tau_{jv}^{\#}$. Call the resulting density $f_j^{\#}(t)$. For either these points or the exact atom points $t=t_j$, use $$\label{cert:lambda-z-star}
 \lambda_*=[i/(b_*(t+ih))]_{40},\qquad
 z_*=[-B/(t+ih)-ih]_{40},$$ and set <a id="cert:rational-factors"></a>$$\label{cert:rational-factors}
 E_l(t)=e_*(tm)\frac{(ip_*ty)^l}{l!},\qquad
 G_l(t)=\lambda_*e_*(z_*m)\frac{(ip_*z_*y)^l}{l!}.$$ The rational moment sums are therefore <a id="cert:rational-moment-sums"></a>$$\label{cert:rational-moment-sums}
\begin{aligned}
 U_{p,l}^{\#}
 &=\Re\left\{\frac16\sum_{j=1}^6\sum_{v=0}^{255}
    o_v^{\#}f_j^{\#}(\tau_{jv}^{\#})E_l(\tau_{jv}^{\#})
       +C\sum_{j=0}^6e_jP_j^{\#}E_l(t_j)\right\},\\
 X_{p,l}^{\#}
 &=\Re\left\{\frac16\sum_{j=1}^6\sum_{v=0}^{255}
    o_v^{\#}f_j^{\#}(\tau_{jv}^{\#})G_l(\tau_{jv}^{\#})
       +C\sum_{j=0}^6e_jP_j^{\#}G_l(t_j)\right\}.
\end{aligned}$$ No additional floors are used. For $Q_n^{\#},D_n^{\#}$ use the product and cotangent formulas defining $Q_n,D_n$, with the same sine and cosine replacements and residue $a=n\bmod12\in A$; cotangent means the ratio of the replaced cosine to the replaced sine.

For the finite pseudocode in Section <a href="#cert:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:evaluation">6.3</a>, the rational prescription uses the same measure order: the $6\cdot256$ quadrature pairs followed by the seven atom pairs. Put $\#$ on the prescribed quantities and make exactly the replacements above; no other rounding is performed.

<a id="stmt-cert-rational-moment-error"></a>
<div id="cert:rational-moment-error" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Rational moment and node errors). *Let $p=PR$ have a rational coefficient list supported in $\{0\}\cup I_f$, with $\|(c,d)\|_1\le3$, and let $C\in\mathbb Q$ satisfy $|C|\le1$. Let $m,y\in\mathbb Q$ and $l\in\{0,\ldots,30\}$ satisfy $0\le m\le100$ and $|y|\le3/2$. The prescription in Section <a href="#cert:rational" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:rational">7.2</a> gives rational numbers $U_{p,l}^{\#}$ and $X_{p,l}^{\#}$ such that $$|U_{p,l}^{\#}-U_{p,l}|<10^{-14},\qquad
 |X_{p,l}^{\#}-X_{p,l}|<10^{-14}.$$ Separately, for every node $n\in12\mathbb Z+A$, using its residue $a\in A$ in <a href="#fourier:node-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-data">[fourier:node-data]</a>, the prescribed rational values satisfy $$|Q_n^{\#}-Q_n|<10^{-25},\qquad |D_n^{\#}-D_n|<10^{-25}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-rational-moment-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-rational-moment-error</summary>

*Proof.* We verify the call hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#cert:rounded-exponential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:rounded-exponential">17</a> before using its error bound, and then propagate the errors through the finite sums. The cosine calls defining the nodes and weights have real arguments $u=\alpha_v$ or $u=2a\alpha_v$, with $|u|<254$; hence $|p_*u|<3.142\cdot254<1000$ and $\Re(ip_*u)=0$. For real $u$, replacing $p_*$ by $\pi$ adds at most $|u|10^{-36}$. The telescoping coefficient sum in the weights is less than one, so $$\begin{aligned}
 |\tau_{jv}^{\#}-\tau_{jv}|
  &<\frac{3.542\cdot10^{-34}+10^{-36}}{12}<9\cdot10^{-34},\\
 |o_v^{\#}-o_v|
  &<\frac{3.542\cdot10^{-34}+254\cdot10^{-36}}{256}
       +10^{-40}<\frac{10^{-29}}{256}.
\end{aligned}$$ The exact nodes have a uniform distance from their piece endpoints: $$\begin{aligned}
 \min\{\tau_{jv}-t_{j-1},t_j-\tau_{jv}\}
 &\ge\frac{1-\cos(\pi/512)}{12}
  =\frac{\sin^2(\pi/1024)}6\\
 &\ge\frac1{6\cdot512^2}=\frac1{1572864}>9\cdot10^{-34}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\sin x\ge2x/\pi$ on $[0,\pi/2]$ follows from concavity. It follows that every real rounded node $\tau_{jv}^{\#}$ remains strictly inside $(t_{j-1},t_j)$. The density is therefore evaluated on its stated piece, and all rounded nodes and exact atoms lie in $[0,1]$. The remaining real exponential calls in the density and direct factors now have $|u|\le100$; the residue arguments for $Q_n,D_n$ have $|u|<1$. They too satisfy the call hypotheses.

For real $t\in[0,1]$, the exact identities $$\begin{gathered}
 |\lambda(t)|\le\frac5{\sqrt3}<3,\\
 |z(t)|^2=h^2+\frac{B^2-2Bh^2}{t^2+h^2}\le\left(\frac{44}{15}\right)^2,\\
 \operatorname{Im}z(t)=h\left(\frac B{t^2+h^2}-1\right)
       \ge\frac{26}{435}>.0597
\end{gathered}$$ use $B^2-2Bh^2=304/225>0$. They apply in particular at every rounded node before the floor in <a href="#cert:lambda-z-star" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:lambda-z-star">[cert:lambda-z-star]</a>. That floor lowers the imaginary part by less than $10^{-40}$ and changes the complex value by less than $2\cdot10^{-40}$. Thus $$\operatorname{Im}z_*\ge\frac{26}{435}-10^{-40}>0,
 \qquad |z_*|<\frac{44}{15}+2\cdot10^{-40}<3.$$ For the exact transformed arguments, $100\pi|z(t)|<922$; for the rounded ones, $|p_*z_*m|<100(3.142)(3)<1000$ and $\Re(ip_*z_*m)=-p_*m\operatorname{Im}z_*\le0$. The exponential estimate already proved therefore applies to every transformed call as well.

For a quadrature node write $t=\tau_{jv}$ and $t^{\#}=\tau_{jv}^{\#}$; at an atom put $t=t^{\#}=t_j$. Along the intervening real segment, $|t+ih|\ge h$ and $b,b_*>.86$. The derivative bounds for the reciprocal factors and the change of $b$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 |\lambda_* -\lambda(t)|
  &<8(9\cdot10^{-34})+4\cdot10^{-36}+2\cdot10^{-40}<10^{-29},\\
 |z_*-z(t)|
  &<\frac{25}{3}(9\cdot10^{-34})+2\cdot10^{-40}<10^{-29}.
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed $1/(b_*h^2)<8$, $B/h^2=25/3$, and the change of $1/b$ contributes less than $10^{-36}/(.86^2h)<4\cdot10^{-36}$. At atoms the node-error terms vanish. Both transformed exponential arguments lie in the closed left half-plane after multiplication by $i$. The integral first-difference formula there gives, for example, $$|e_*(z_*m)-e^{i\pi z(t)m}|
 <10^{-32}+100(3.142\cdot10^{-29}+3\cdot10^{-36})<4\cdot10^{-27}.$$ The direct exponential-factor error is smaller. The arguments of the divided powers differ by less than $1.5(3.142\cdot10^{-29}+3\cdot10^{-36})<5\cdot10^{-29}$. The divided powers and their derivatives are bounded along the connecting segment by $e^{1.5\cdot3.142\cdot3}<1.5\cdot10^6$; this also gives the stated bound $e^{1.5\pi\cdot3}<1.5\cdot10^6$. One rational check of this exponential bound uses $1.5(3.142)(3)/16<.884$ and $$\begin{gathered}
 \sum_{q=0}^{8}\frac{.884^q}{q!}
 +\frac{.884^9}{9!}\frac1{1-.884/10}<2.421,\\
 2.421^{16}<1.5\cdot10^6.
\end{gathered}$$ The first expression majorizes the exponential series because every successive ratio in its tail is at most $.884/10$. The divided-power errors are therefore less than $7.5\cdot10^{-23}<1.5\cdot10^{-22}$. Using $|\lambda|<3$, modulus at most one for the exact exponentials, and modulus less than two for their approximations, the transformed-factor error is at most $$2(1.5\cdot10^6)10^{-29}
 +3(1.5\cdot10^6)(4\cdot10^{-27})
 +3(1.5\cdot10^{-22})<3\cdot10^{-20}.$$ The same bound applies to the direct factor.

For completeness, the density estimate can be obtained without relying on cancellation. Its real exponential factors, including evaluation at a rounded node, have error less than $3.542\cdot10^{-34}+100(3.142(9\cdot10^{-34})+10^{-36})<3\cdot10^{-31}$. Let $A_n,B_n$ denote the inner sums in <a href="#cert:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:density">[cert:density]</a> with and without the factor $t_l-t$, respectively, and put $\#$ on their replacements. On the common piece, $|t_l-t|,|t_l-t^{\#}|\le1$ for $l\ge j$. The displayed list for $P_l$ gives $\sum_{l=1}^6|P_l^{\#}-P_l|<7\cdot10^{-36}$ and $\sum_{l=1}^6|P_l^{\#}|<11$. Hence $|A_n^{\#}|,|B_n^{\#}|<22$ and $$\begin{aligned}
 |A_n^{\#}-A_n|,\ |B_n^{\#}-B_n|
 &<22(9\cdot10^{-34})+2(7\cdot10^{-36})\\
 &\quad+10(3\cdot10^{-31})<4\cdot10^{-30}.
\end{aligned}$$ The bracket in <a href="#cert:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:density">[cert:density]</a> has approximate magnitude at most $70(|c_n|+|d_n|)$ and error less than $1.3\cdot10^{-29}(|c_n|+|d_n|)$. Expanding the outer product once and using list norm at most three gives the pointwise bound $$\begin{aligned}
 |f_j^{\#}(t^{\#})-f_j(t)|
 &<2(10^{-36})(3)(2)(70)\\
 &\quad+2(3.142)(3)\bigl(70(3\cdot10^{-31})+1.3\cdot10^{-29}\bigr)\\
 &<7\cdot10^{-28}<10^{-22}.
\end{aligned}$$

On the real segment $|f_j|<1000$, and each atom has magnitude at most six and error less than $10^{-28}$. The latter error also follows directly from the displayed $P_l$ list. The exact transformed factor has magnitude below $3\cdot1.5\cdot10^6$, so every approximate factor has magnitude below $4.6\cdot10^6$. The exact quadrature weights have total mass one, and the sum of the absolute weight errors over all six pieces is less than $10^{-29}$. Separating weight, density, factor, and atom errors, and then adding the analytic quadrature error, bounds either moment error by $$\begin{split}
 &10^{-29}(1001)(4.6\cdot10^6)+10^{-22}(4.6\cdot10^6)
   +1000(3\cdot10^{-20})\\
 &\quad+7\bigl(10^{-28}(4.6\cdot10^6)+6(3\cdot10^{-20})\bigr)
   +10^{-22}<5\cdot10^{-16}<10^{-14}.
\end{split}$$

Finally, for the residue arguments the sine and cosine replacement errors are less than $3.542\cdot10^{-34}+10^{-36}<4\cdot10^{-34}$. Every nonzero exact sine has magnitude at least $\sin(\pi/12)>1/4$, and its replacement has magnitude greater than $1/5$. Each squared factor $(2\sin)^2$ in $Q_n$ and its replacement is less than five in magnitude, and their difference is less than $5\cdot10^{-33}$. Telescoping the five factors and allowing for $(p_*/6)^2-(\pi/6)^2$ gives a $Q_n$ error less than $2\cdot10^{-29}<10^{-25}$. For a cotangent, division by the two sine lower bounds gives an error less than $5(4\cdot10^{-34})+20(4\cdot10^{-34})=10^{-32}$. Summing five terms and allowing for the prefactor gives a $D_n$ error less than $6\cdot10^{-32}<10^{-25}$, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="cert:propagation"></a>
#### Propagation to finite certificates

We record stability estimates before applying any numerical margin. The first controls the two operator coordinates under errors in the moments and node constants. The second controls Bernstein sums from errors in the signed-$y$ moments.

<a id="ind:pair-stability"></a>
To propagate a moment radius, abbreviate $X_\ell=X_{p,\ell}(m,1)$ and write $\mathcal S_{m,c},\mathcal S_{m,d}$ for the two components of <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a>, with hats for their rational approximations. Suppose $|X_0|,|X_1|\le H$, $|D_m|\le d$, and rational approximations have errors at most $\epsilon,\epsilon,\eta_Q,\eta_D$ in $X_0,X_1,Q_m,D_m$, respectively. If both exact and approximate $Q_m$ exceed $q>0$, expanding the numerator and then the reciprocal in <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a> gives $$\label{ind:pair-stability}
 \max\{|\widehat{\mathcal S}_{m,c}-\mathcal S_{m,c}|,
        |\widehat{\mathcal S}_{m,d}-\mathcal S_{m,d}|\}
 \le \frac{(d+1+\eta_D)\epsilon+H\eta_D}{q}
       +\frac{(d+1)H\eta_Q}{q^2}.$$ For example, the perturbed second numerator differs by at most $(d+\eta_D)\epsilon+H\eta_D+\epsilon$, and the exact numerator is bounded by $(d+1)H$; this proves the inequality, which also dominates the first entry’s error. The Taylor budgets and the separated and common-kernel first-block estimates below use $q=7/4$ and $d=2.12$. The separate-rounding comparison uses $q=7/4$ and $d=4.3$. The rational lower bound $Q_m>197192/112500$ proved after <a href="#fourier:node-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-bounds">[fourier:node-bounds]</a> exceeds $7/4$ by $1268/450000$, so $\eta_Q\le10^{-25}$ also ensures that the approximate $Q_m$ exceeds $q$.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
###### Analytic pair-error allowance.

For an input $p$ in the rational finite-input domain of Lemma <a href="#cert:rational-moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:rational-moment-error">18</a> and a row $m\in I_f$, define the rational version $\mathcal S_m^\#$ by using the quantities with superscript $\#$ throughout <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a>. We record the division estimate explicitly for both basis and full-table inputs. By <a href="#interpolation:piece-envelopes" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="interpolation:piece-envelopes">[interpolation:piece-envelopes]</a> and the atom masses there, the total variation of the folded measure for any input in the real finite-input domain of Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a> is at most $$3\sum_{j=1}^6\frac{M_j}{6}+\sum_{j=0}^6m_j^*=119.642<133.$$ On the real segment, $|\lambda|,|z|<3$ and $|e^{i\pi z m}|\le1$ for $m\ge0$. Thus, using $\pi<22/7$, $$|X_{p,0}(m,1)|<133(3)<400,\qquad
 |X_{p,1}(m,1)|<133(3)(3)(22/7)=3762<4000.$$ For this calculation abbreviate $X_j=X_{p,j}(m,1)$, $Q=Q_m$, and $D=D_m$. Write $\varepsilon=10^{-14}$ and $\delta=10^{-25}$. By Lemma <a href="#cert:rational-moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:rational-moment-error">18</a>, the errors in $X_0,X_1$ are less than $\varepsilon$ and those in $Q,D$ are less than $\delta$. Equation <a href="#fourier:node-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-bounds">[fourier:node-bounds]</a> gives $Q>1.75$, $|D|<2.12$, and hence $Q^{\#}>1.7$. For $\mathcal A=DX_0-X_1$ and $\mathcal A^{\#}=D^{\#}X_0^{\#}-X_1^{\#}$, $$|\mathcal A|<4848,\qquad
 |\mathcal A^{\#}-\mathcal A|<2.12\varepsilon+(400+\varepsilon)\delta+\varepsilon
             <3.121\cdot10^{-14}.$$ The two quotient differences are consequently bounded by $$\begin{aligned}
 \left|\frac{X_0^{\#}}{Q^{\#}}-\frac{X_0}{Q}\right|
 &<\frac{10^{-14}}{1.7}+\frac{400\cdot10^{-25}}{1.75(1.7)}
   <6\cdot10^{-15},\\
 \left|\frac{\mathcal A^{\#}}{Q^{\#}}-\frac{\mathcal A}{Q}\right|
 &<\frac{3.121\cdot10^{-14}}{1.7}
   +\frac{4848\cdot10^{-25}}{1.75(1.7)}
   <1.84\cdot10^{-14}<2\cdot10^{-14}.
\end{aligned}$$ In particular, the uniform error in each entry of $\mathcal S_m^{\#}$ is strictly less than $10^{-12}$; we retain this looser allowance below.

<a id="ind:bernstein"></a>
##### Bernstein multipliers

For every signed half-gap in Lemma <a href="#signs:bernstein" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:bernstein">10</a>, $y\ne0$ and $|y|^{-\nu}\le4$. Directly from <a href="#cert:moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:moments">[cert:moments]</a>, $$y^{-\nu}\bigl(U_{p,r+\nu}(m,y)+X_{q,r+\nu}(m,y)\bigr)
 =y^r\bigl(U_{p,r+\nu}(m,1)+X_{q,r+\nu}(m,1)\bigr).$$ This identity also holds for negative $y$ and gives exactly the power coefficients of <a href="#cert:power-from-moments" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:power-from-moments">[cert:power-from-moments]</a>; it does not replace their derivatives by interpolated jets. Two moment errors less than $10^{-14}$ give a $T_r$ error less than $8\cdot10^{-14}$. For fixed $0\le k\le28$, the rational recurrence $a_0=1$ and $a_r=a_{r-1}(k+1-r)/(29-r)$ satisfies <a id="ind:bernstein-multiplier"></a>$$\label{ind:bernstein-multiplier}
 a_r=\prod_{j=1}^r\frac{k+1-j}{29-j}
 =\frac{k!}{(k-r)!}\frac{(28-r)!}{28!}
 =\frac{\binom{k}{r}}{\binom{28}{r}}\qquad(0\le r\le k).$$ Each factor is in $[0,1]$. There are $\sum_{k=0}^{28}k=28\cdot29/2=406$ nonconstant multipliers, in addition to $a_0=1$ for each $k$. Every Bernstein sum has at most $29$ terms, so its error is strictly less than $29(8\cdot10^{-14})=2.32\cdot10^{-12}$.

<a id="cert:rational-small"></a>
#### An independent rational check of the first block

The optional program checks the $6\times6$ block on $J=\{1,3,4\}$ by exact rational arithmetic. It uses the cosine rule <a href="#cert:rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:rule">[cert:rule]</a> with $N=64$ and the sum over $1\le a\le31$, and otherwise the rounded exponential, kernels and basis densities specified above. There are no atom terms for these basis inputs. The following error bound makes this smaller check independent of the full Arb calculation.

<a id="stmt-cert-rational-small-error"></a>
<div id="cert:rational-small-error" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19**. *For each basis input $c_n=1$ or $d_n=1$, $n\in J$, with all other data zero, and each output node $m\in J$, the rational program computes both entries of $\mathcal S_m(p)$ with absolute error less than $10^{-12}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cert-rational-small-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-cert-rational-small-error</summary>

<a id="cert:rational-small-quadrature"></a>
*Proof.* We separate quadrature error from rounding in the finite sum. Use the same complex disks of radius $1/6$ as in Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a>. The basis density on any disk is bounded by $$D_0=2\pi^2\cdot10\cdot\frac{13}{12}e^{4\pi/6}.$$ This also bounds a simple-pole basis density. The bounds $|\lambda|<5$, $|z|<6.2$ and $\operatorname{Im}z>-.081$ proved there show that the transformed value and first-derivative integrands are bounded respectively by $$M_0=5D_0e^{4\pi\cdot.081}<24028,
 \qquad M_1=6.2\pi M_0<468015.$$ Use the rational upper bound $e^x\le\mathcal E_n(x)$ from <a href="#ind:positive-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:positive-exponential">[ind:positive-exponential]</a>. Put $\overline\pi=p_*+10^{-36}>\pi$. Here $x=743\overline\pi/750$ lies in $[0,4)$, so the formula applies with $n=40$. Using $M_0=(325/3)\pi^2e^{743\pi/750}$, the exact rational comparisons $$\frac{325}{3}\overline\pi^2 \mathcal E_{40}(743\overline\pi/750)<24028,
 \qquad
 \frac{31}{5}\overline\pi\,
 \frac{325}{3}\overline\pi^2 \mathcal E_{40}(743\overline\pi/750)<468015$$ prove the displayed bounds for $M_0,M_1$. Each comparison is a fraction inequality after clearing positive denominators; no rounded decimal evaluation of an exponential is used. Discrete cosine orthogonality now gives exactness through degree $63$. The weights sum to one and each is at least $1/(64\cdot63)>0$ by the same telescoping identity. Cauchy’s estimate on the half-radius real segments bounds the degree-$63$ Taylor tails by $M_i2^{-63}$. The integral and quadrature have total mass one, after combining all six pieces. Thus their discrepancies in the two moments are at most $$\epsilon_0=2^{-62}M_0<5.211\cdot10^{-15},\qquad
 \epsilon_1=2^{-62}M_1<1.015\cdot10^{-13}.$$ Using $Q_m>1.75$ and $|D_m|<2.12$, the quadrature error in either entry of <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a> is consequently less than $$\label{cert:rational-small-quadrature}
 \frac{2.12\epsilon_0+\epsilon_1}{1.75}<6.432\cdot10^{-14}.$$

For completeness, two changes in the rational implementation require care in reusing the rounding estimates. First, for a real argument $u$ the program evaluates $e_*(r)$ with $r=u\bmod2\in[0,2)$. Periodicity is used for the exact target: $e^{i\pi r}=e^{i\pi u}$. The approximation $p_*$ is not treated as giving an exactly periodic exponential. Since $r$ is real and $|p_*r|<2(3.142)<1000$, Lemma <a href="#cert:rounded-exponential" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:rounded-exponential">17</a>, followed by $|e^{ip_*r}-e^{i\pi r}|\le2\cdot10^{-36}$, gives an absolute error less than $2\cdot10^{-32}$ for each such replacement. When $u$ contains an approximate quadrature node, first compare at that approximate argument and then use the first-difference bound for the exact exponential. This avoids any assumption of continuity of the modulo operation.

<a id="cert:rational-small-weight-total"></a>
Second, with $N=64$, the sum of the absolute cosine coefficients in one weight is less than one. Including the final floor, the weight error is therefore at most $$\frac{2\cdot10^{-32}}N+10^{-40}
 <\frac{10^{-29}}N.$$ Summing the stronger first expression, rather than the relaxed bound on the right, gives the total weight error $$\label{cert:rational-small-weight-total}
 N\left(\frac{2\cdot10^{-32}}N+10^{-40}\right)
 =2\cdot10^{-32}+64\cdot10^{-40}<3\cdot10^{-32}.$$ The node error is less than $2\cdot10^{-32}/12<2\cdot10^{-33}$. The exact nodes are at distance at least $1/98304$ from the endpoints of their own interval $[(j-1)/6,j/6]$, since $1-\cos(\pi/128)\ge2/128^2$. Thus the approximate nodes remain in their own intervals, in particular in $[0,1]$. Their transformed frequencies have positive imaginary part even after the floor, because $\operatorname{Im}z\ge26/435$ there. The real-segment estimates in the proof of Lemma <a href="#cert:rational-moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:rational-moment-error">18</a> also give $|z_*|<3$. Thus every transformed call has its argument in the closed left half-plane and satisfies $|p_*z_*m|<3.142(3)(4)<1000$.

Here is a direct propagation bound for these smaller inputs. The real-segment estimates are $|\lambda|<3$, $|z|<3$, and basis-density modulus less than $214$. For one piece put $$A_n=\sum_{l=j}^6P_le^{i\pi t_ln},\qquad
 B_n=\sum_{l=j}^6t_lP_le^{i\pi t_ln}.$$ Their moduli are at most ten. Replacing $b$ and the real exponentials changes each sum by less than $3\cdot10^{-31}$; including the node movement changes $B_n-tA_n$ by less than $7\cdot10^{-31}$. The phase $e^{-i\pi tn}$ changes by less than $5\cdot10^{-32}$, since $n\le4$. Substitution in <a href="#fourier:folded" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:folded">[fourier:folded]</a> therefore gives a density error less than $3\cdot10^{-29}$. The derivatives of the rational kernels, using $b,b_*>4/5$ and $|t+ih|\ge2/5$, give errors less than $2\cdot10^{-32}$ in both $\lambda$ and $z$, including their floors. The first-difference formula for exponentials in the closed left half-plane, with $m\le4$, then bounds the errors in $$g_0(t)=\lambda(t)e^{i\pi mz(t)},\qquad
 g_1(t)=g_0(t)i\pi z(t)$$ by $2\cdot10^{-30}$ and $3\cdot10^{-29}$, respectively. The exact factors have moduli below $3$ and $36$; their approximations have moduli below $4$ and $37$. Product differences bound each derivative-integrand error by $$3\cdot10^{-29}\cdot37+214\cdot3\cdot10^{-29}
 <8\cdot10^{-27};$$ the value-integrand bound is smaller. By <a href="#cert:rational-small-weight-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:rational-small-weight-total">[cert:rational-small-weight-total]</a>, the total absolute weight error is below $3\cdot10^{-32}$, and the approximate integrands have modulus below $10^4$. Hence each rounded moment differs from its exact finite quadrature moment by less than $10^{-26}$.

Finally, for residues $1,3,4$, the exact polynomial forms are $$\begin{gathered}
 Q_1=Q_3=\frac{4\pi^2}{3}(1-b),\qquad Q_4=\frac{\pi^2}{3},\\
 D_1=\frac{\pi(3+2b)}{18},\qquad
 D_3=-\frac{\pi(3+2b)}{18},\qquad D_4=\frac{7\pi b}{9}.
\end{gathered}$$ The program replaces $\pi,b$ by $p_*,b_*$ in these expressions. Using $|p_*^2-\pi^2|<6.3\cdot10^{-36}$, $\pi^2<10$, and $b,b_*<1$ gives $$\begin{aligned}
 |Q_m^\#-Q_m|
 &<\max\{(4/3)(6.3+10),\,6.3/3\}10^{-36}<3\cdot10^{-35},\\
 |D_m^\#-D_m|
 &<\max\{(5+44/7)/18,\,29/9\}10^{-36}<4\cdot10^{-36}.
\end{aligned}$$ The exact rational lower bound used after <a href="#ind:pair-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:pair-stability">[ind:pair-stability]</a> therefore shows that both denominators $Q_m,Q_m^\#$ exceed $7/4$. The moments have modulus below $10^4$, so the additional division and $D_m$ errors are less than $4\cdot10^{-31}$. Together with <a href="#cert:rational-small-quadrature" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:rational-small-quadrature">[cert:rational-small-quadrature]</a>, these bounds give an error less than $7\cdot10^{-14}<10^{-12}$ in either matrix entry, as asserted. ◻

</details>

If every entry of this $6\times6$ matrix has error below $\varepsilon=10^{-12}$, its induced column-norm error is below $6\varepsilon$. The program accordingly enlarges the computed defect norm by $\|W_\eta\|_1\,6\varepsilon$, then verifies that the result is below $.003$ and that the resulting inverse bound is below $32$. This is the same perturbation-of-identity argument as in Section <a href="#cert:matrix-bounds" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:matrix-bounds">6.4</a>. It checks both small inverse certificates without asserting that the full $N=256$ rational prescription has been executed.

<a id="ind:small"></a>
#### Comparison of first-block majorants

The preceding $N=64$ proof separates the value and derivative integrands. Two coarser arguments instead use one majorant for both. They concern the same basis inputs $n\in J$, row nodes $m\in J$, $y=1$, and orders zero and one, with no atoms. The table compares their disk and node bounds with the resulting entry errors: $$\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
 \text{route}&\text{disk majorant}&|D_m|&\text{entry error}\\ \hline
 \text{separated}&M_0<24028,\ M_1<468015&<2.12&<7\cdot10^{-14}\\
 \text{common kernel}&M=10^6&<2.12&<4.1\cdot10^{-13}\\
 \text{separate rounding}&M=750000&<4.3&<5.23\cdot10^{-13}
\end{array}$$ The separated route uses the explicit density bound $D_0$ from Section <a href="#cert:rational-small" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:rational-small">7.4</a>. The two common routes use density and exponential bounds $(1800,2.8)$ and $(2500,3)$, respectively.

<a id="ind:small-kernel"></a>
##### A common kernel majorant

<a id="ind:small-exponentials"></a>
The rational comparisons $$\label{ind:small-exponentials}
 \mathcal E_{32}(44/21)<42/5,\qquad
 \mathcal E_{32}(891/875)<14/5$$ follow by clearing positive denominators in <a href="#ind:positive-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:positive-exponential">[ind:positive-exponential]</a>; both arguments are nonnegative and less than $34$. Since $4\pi/6<44/21$ and $4\pi(.081)<891/875$, the two basis densities on the complex disks satisfy $$\begin{aligned}
 |u_n(t)|&<2(22/7)^2(65/6)(42/5)=12584/7<1800,\\
 |v_n(t)|&<2(22/7)10(42/5)=3696/7<1800.
\end{aligned}$$ The bounds $|\lambda|<5$, $|e^{i\pi zm}|<2.8$, and $\pi|z|<6.2\pi<20$ therefore bound both transformed integrands by $1800(5)(2.8)(20)=504000<10^6$. The positive degree-63 rule and the half-radius Cauchy estimate give, for each moment, <a id="ind:small-kernel-quadrature"></a>$$\label{ind:small-kernel-quadrature}
 |X_\ell-X_{\ell,\mathrm{quad}}|\le10^6 2^{-62}<2.17\cdot10^{-13}.$$ The factor two here compares the integral and quadrature averages; a direct Taylor average has only one tail, as in Lemma <a href="#ind:taylor-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:taylor-average">20</a>.

The rounding proof in Section <a href="#cert:rational-small" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:rational-small">7.4</a> applies to these same inputs and gives finite moment-rounding error below $10^{-26}$, hence below $10^{-14}$. It also gives $|X_0|,|X_1|<10^4$, $|\delta Q|<3\cdot10^{-35}$, $|\delta D|<4\cdot10^{-36}$, and both denominators above $7/4$. Thus the total moment allowance is $2.27\cdot10^{-13}$. In <a href="#ind:pair-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:pair-stability">[ind:pair-stability]</a>, the additional $Q,D$ contribution is less than $$\frac{10^4(4\cdot10^{-36})+(4\cdot10^{-36})(2.27\cdot10^{-13})}{7/4}
 +\frac{3.12\cdot10^4(3\cdot10^{-35})}{(7/4)^2}<4\cdot10^{-31}.$$ Consequently <a id="ind:small-kernel-entry"></a>$$\label{ind:small-kernel-entry}
 \frac{3.12(2.27\cdot10^{-13})}{1.75}+4\cdot10^{-31}
 <4.1\cdot10^{-13}<10^{-12}.$$

<a id="ind:small-rounding"></a>
##### A separate rounding majorant

The weaker bounds $e^{4\pi/6}<9$ and $e^{4\pi(.081)}<3$ also follow from <a href="#ind:small-exponentials" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:small-exponentials">[ind:small-exponentials]</a>. They bound either basis density by $2500$; for the larger density, $2(22/7)^2(65/6)9<2500$. With the kernel bound $5$ and derivative factor $6.2\pi<20$, this gives the distinct common majorant $M=2500(5)(3)(20)=750000$. Its moment quadrature radius is <a id="ind:small-rounding-quadrature"></a>$$\label{ind:small-rounding-quadrature}
 \varepsilon_q=750000\,2^{-62}
 <1.626303258728257\cdot10^{-13}.$$ Using $|D_m|<4.3$ and $Q_m>7/4$, the pair quadrature error is less than $(5.3/(7/4))\varepsilon_q<4.926\cdot10^{-13}$. The same finite rounding allowance below $10^{-14}$ applies because the schedule and inputs have not changed. Substitution of the same polynomial $Q,D$ errors in <a href="#ind:pair-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:pair-stability">[ind:pair-stability]</a>, now with $d=4.3$, bounds their additional contribution by $6\cdot10^{-31}$. Therefore <a id="ind:small-rounding-entry"></a>$$\label{ind:small-rounding-entry}
 \frac{5.3}{7/4}\bigl(750000\,2^{-62}+10^{-14}\bigr)
       +6\cdot10^{-31}<5.23\cdot10^{-13}<10^{-12}.$$

All three routes support the program’s $10^{-12}$ entry radius. They do not extend its coverage: the public rational algorithm still checks only the 36 first-block entries and the two inverse certificates, not the residual or Bernstein tables.

<a id="ind:taylor"></a>
#### Rational-center Taylor integration

For a finite input in the real moment domain of Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a>, let $F^U_{j,\ell}$ and $F^X_{j,\ell}$ be the density <a href="#cert:density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:density">[cert:density]</a> multiplied by the respective factors in <a href="#cert:analytic-factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:analytic-factors">[cert:analytic-factors]</a>. The input is supported on nodes at most $100$, has coefficient-list norm at most $3$ and $|C|\le1$, and the parameters satisfy $0\le m\le100$, $|y|\le3/2$, and $\ell\in\{0,\ldots,30\}$. The coefficients, $C,m,y$ are rational when a rational enclosure is requested. The six centers and the two radii are <a id="ind:centers"></a>$$\label{ind:centers}
 c_j=\frac{2j-1}{12},\qquad R=\frac16,\qquad a=\frac1{12}
 \quad(1\le j\le6).$$ Each $F^U_{j,\ell}$ and $F^X_{j,\ell}$ is analytic on a neighborhood of $|t-c_j|\le R$: the input list is finite and the only rational-kernel pole, $-ih$, is outside that disk. This analytic assertion is made for finite inputs. An arbitrary $\ell^1$ list need not give a complex-frequency density analytic on these disks.

<a id="stmt-ind-taylor-average"></a>
<div id="ind:taylor-average" class="lemma">

<a id="ind:taylor-average-error"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Taylor averages with rational coefficient radii). *Suppose $F(c_j+x)=\sum_{k\ge0}A_kx^k$ is analytic on a neighborhood of $|x|\le R$ and $|F|\le M$ there. Let $D\ge0$ be an integer. For $0\le k\le D$, suppose $\widehat A_k\in\mathbb Q(i)$ and $e_k\in\mathbb Q_{\ge0}$ satisfy $|A_k-\widehat A_k|\le e_k$. Then $$\begin{aligned}
\label{ind:taylor-average-error}
 \left|6\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_j}F(t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm dt-
       \sum_{2r\le D}\widehat A_{2r}\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}\right|
 \le M2^{-D}+\sum_{2r\le D}e_{2r}\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}.
\end{aligned}$$ If the final sum of radii is at most $\delta$ for each of the six pieces, their combined integral has error at most $M2^{-D}+\delta$. Discrete atoms are not included in this estimate and are to be enclosed separately.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ind-taylor-average"><summary>Proof of stmt-ind-taylor-average</summary>

*Proof.* Since $2a=1/6$, the expression $6\int F$ is the interval average. The average of $x^k$ on $[-a,a]$ is zero for odd $k$ and is $a^k/(k+1)$ for even $k$. Thus the degree-$D$ Taylor polynomial has the exact average <a id="ind:even-average"></a>$$\label{ind:even-average}
 \sum_{2r\le D} A_{2r}\frac{a^{2r}}{2r+1}.$$ Cauchy’s estimate gives $|A_k|\le M R^{-k}$. Bounding the average of the absolute tail by its supremum on $[-a,a]$ gives the conservative bound $$M\sum_{k>D}(a/R)^k=M\sum_{k>D}2^{-k}=M2^{-D}.$$ Replacing the even coefficients adds the displayed weighted sum of their radii. Finally, each of the six integrals is $1/6$ times its average, so the six factors $1/6$ sum to one. Taking a real part cannot increase the error. Atoms have no integral tail of this kind. ◻

</details>

<a id="ind:ball-operations"></a>
Here are explicit rational operations for obtaining the coefficient enclosures in this lemma. Write $\mathcal B(a,r)=\{w\in\mathbb C:|w-a|\le r\}$, where $a\in\mathbb Q(i)$ and $r\in\mathbb Q_{\ge0}$, and put $\langle a\rangle_1=|\Re a|+|\Im a|$. The following enclosure operations use rational centers and radii: $$\begin{aligned}
\label{ind:ball-operations}
 \mathcal B(a,r)+\mathcal B(b,s)&\subseteq\mathcal B(a+b,r+s),\nonumber\\
 \mathcal B(a,r)\mathcal B(b,s)&\subseteq
 \mathcal B\bigl(ab,\langle a\rangle_1s+\langle b\rangle_1r+rs\bigr),\nonumber\\
 \mathcal B(a,r)^{-1}&\subseteq\mathcal B(a^{-1},r/\ell_0^2)
 \quad\text{if }0<\ell_0\le |a|-r.
\end{aligned}$$ In the last line $a^{-1}\in\mathbb Q(i)$, and the condition on a rational $\ell_0$ can be checked by squaring rational quantities. The bound follows from $|w^{-1}-a^{-1}|=|w-a|/(|w||a|)$. These operations allow repeated use of the same enclosure; possible dependence between its occurrences does not invalidate inclusion.

<a id="ind:constant-exponential"></a>
Constant exponentials also admit arbitrarily small rational radii. Use the rational bounds $\mathcal T_n,\mathcal E_n$ from <a href="#ind:positive-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:positive-exponential">[ind:positive-exponential]</a>. If $w\in\mathcal B(w_0,r)$, choose rational $q\ge|w_0|$, for example $q=\langle w_0\rangle_1$, and nonnegative integers $n,s$ with $q<n+2$ and $q+r<s+2$. Then $$\label{ind:constant-exponential}
 e^w\in\mathcal B\left(\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{w_0^k}{k!},
       \mathcal T_n(q)+r\mathcal E_s(q+r)\right).$$ Indeed, the first radius bounds the complex Taylor tail at $w_0$. The integral first-difference formula for $e^w$ bounds the change from $w_0$ to $w$ by $r e^{q+r}$, giving the second radius. Both preconditions in <a href="#ind:positive-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:positive-exponential">[ind:positive-exponential]</a> are explicit; no estimate of a positive exponential by a truncated sum alone is being used. For bounded $q$, $\mathcal T_n(q)\to0$ as $n\to\infty$.

<a id="ind:convolution"></a>
For clarity, the coefficient recurrences needed for the present integrands are as follows. If $a(x)=\sum a_kx^k$ and $b(x)=\sum b_kx^k$, multiplication is convolution, $$\label{ind:convolution}
 (ab)_k=\sum_{v=0}^k a_v b_{k-v}.$$ If $a_v\in\mathcal B(\widehat a_v,e_v)$ and $b_v\in\mathcal B(\widehat b_v,f_v)$, a rational radius for the center $\sum_{v=0}^k\widehat a_v\widehat b_{k-v}$ is explicitly $$\sum_{v=0}^k\left(\langle\widehat a_v\rangle_1f_{k-v}
       +\langle\widehat b_{k-v}\rangle_1e_v+e_vf_{k-v}\right).$$ For a reciprocal series $q(x)=a(x)^{-1}$ with $a_0\ne0$, and for an exponential series $E(x)=e^{g(x)}$, the recurrences are <a id="ind:reciprocal-exponential-recurrences"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
\label{ind:reciprocal-exponential-recurrences}
 q_0&=a_0^{-1},&
 q_k&=-q_0\sum_{v=1}^k a_vq_{k-v}\quad(k\ge1),\nonumber\\
 E_0&=e^{g_0},&
 E_k&=\frac1k\sum_{v=1}^k v g_v E_{k-v}\quad(k\ge1).
\end{aligned}$$ The second line follows by comparing coefficients in $E'=g'E$. Use <a href="#ind:constant-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:constant-exponential">[ind:constant-exponential]</a> for $E_0$ and <a href="#ind:ball-operations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:ball-operations">[ind:ball-operations]</a> in these recurrences. Along with the displayed convolution radius, this specifies a rational radius for every coefficient, not merely a formal power series.

<a id="ind:divided-power-recurrence"></a>
More particularly, $d_j=c_j+ih\in\mathbb Q(i)$ and <a id="ind:kernel-recurrences"></a>$$\label{ind:kernel-recurrences}
 r_0=d_j^{-1},\quad r_{k+1}=-r_k/d_j,\qquad
 \lambda_k=ir_k/b,\quad z_0=-Br_0-ih,\quad z_k=-Br_k\ (k\ge1)$$ are the coefficients of $(t+ih)^{-1}$, $\lambda(t)$, and $z(t)$ at $t=c_j+x$. The $r_k,z_k$ are rational complex numbers; an enclosure for $b^{-1}$ gives the radii for $\lambda_k$. On piece $j$, put $$L_{n,j}=\sum_{v=j}^6P_v e^{i\pi t_vn},\qquad
 H_{n,j}=\sum_{v=j}^6t_vP_v e^{i\pi t_vn}.$$ For $\Phi_n(x)=e^{-i\pi n(c_j+x)}$, use $\phi_{n,0}=e^{-i\pi nc_j}$ and $\phi_{n,k+1}=(-i\pi n)\phi_{n,k}/(k+1)$. The density is then the finite series expression <a id="ind:density-recurrence"></a>$$\label{ind:density-recurrence}
 f_j(c_j+x)=2\pi\sum_n\Phi_n(x)
   \left\{-\pi c_n\bigl(H_{n,j}-(c_j+x)L_{n,j}\bigr)+id_nL_{n,j}\right\}.$$ The constant exponentials in $L_{n,j},H_{n,j},\phi_{n,0}$ are enclosed by <a href="#ind:constant-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:constant-exponential">[ind:constant-exponential]</a>. For a series $v(x)$ equal to $c_j+x$ or $z(c_j+x)$, the divided powers obey $$\label{ind:divided-power-recurrence}
 V_0(x)=1,\qquad V_{\ell+1}(x)=
       \frac{V_\ell(x)\,i\pi y v(x)}{\ell+1}.$$ Finally apply the exponential recurrence to $g=i\pi m(c_j+x)$ or $g=i\pi m z(c_j+x)$, and multiply the resulting series by the density, the divided power, and, in the transformed case, $\lambda$. Truncating these recurrences after degree $D$ gives every coefficient through $D$ exactly at the level of formal series; omitted higher coefficients cannot enter a lower convolution coefficient.

<a id="ind:atom-radii"></a>
The seven possible atoms are handled separately. At $t_j$, enclose their masses $e_jCP_j$ and the appropriate factor in <a href="#cert:analytic-factors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:analytic-factors">[cert:analytic-factors]</a> by the same rational operations and constant exponential bound. If their respective centers and radii are $(a_j,r_j)$ and $(g_j,s_j)$, their product has radius $$\label{ind:atom-radii}
 \eta_j=\langle a_j\rangle_1s_j+\langle g_j\rangle_1r_j+r_js_j.$$ The sum $\eta=\sum_{j=0}^6\eta_j$ is added to the six-piece error; there is no Taylor integration tail for an atom. When $C=0$, all these terms vanish.

All of these radii can be refined effectively. Alternating rational arctangent sums in Machin’s identity enclose $\pi$ to arbitrarily small width, and rational bisection of the positive square root of $3/4$ does the same for $b$. The kernel denominators have $|c_j+ih|\ge h=2/5$, and $b>4/5$. Taking real and imaginary parts of the constant exponential enclosures also encloses cosine and sine with the same radius. The nonzero sine denominators in <a href="#fourier:node-data" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-data">[fourier:node-data]</a> have modulus greater than $1/4$. Consequently sufficiently refined enclosures certify fixed positive lower bounds for the moduli of all denominators used above or in computing $Q_m,D_m$, and separately a positive lower bound for $Q_m$ at pair division. For each fixed $D$ there are finitely many operations in <a href="#ind:convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:convolution">[ind:convolution]</a>–<a href="#ind:divided-power-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:divided-power-recurrence">[ind:divided-power-recurrence]</a>. Their radius formulas tend to zero when their input radii tend to zero, while <a href="#ind:constant-exponential" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:constant-exponential">[ind:constant-exponential]</a> has radius tending to zero by refining its input and increasing its two truncation orders. Thus one may refine the rational inputs and exponential orders, recompute the displayed rational radii, and stop when any prescribed finite collection of positive coefficient and atom budgets holds. The convergence just proved ensures that this rational stopping test terminates. This is a computability statement for each specified finite set of inputs, not a claim that any of its moment sums have been evaluated.

<a id="ind:coefficient-budget"></a>
We next make sufficient budgets explicit. Put $h_D=\lfloor D/2\rfloor+1$. For rational $\delta>0$, the conditions $$\label{ind:coefficient-budget}
 e_{j,2r}\le \frac{\delta(2r+1)12^{2r}}{h_D}
 \quad(0\le2r\le D)$$ on each piece and moment imply that the weighted coefficient-radius sum in <a href="#ind:taylor-average-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:taylor-average-error">[ind:taylor-average-error]</a> is at most $\delta$.

We now combine these integration budgets with the pair estimate <a href="#ind:pair-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:pair-stability">[ind:pair-stability]</a> and the Bernstein estimate from Section <a href="#ind:bernstein" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:bernstein">7.3.1</a>.

<a id="stmt-ind-taylor-budgets"></a>
<div id="ind:taylor-budgets" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (Two conditional Taylor budgets). *The following choices give sufficient rational error enclosures for the same finite quantities used in Appendix <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">6</a>.*

*For a basis input at $n\in J=\{1,3,4\}$, a row $m\in J$, $y=1$, and $\ell=0,1$, take $D=63$, $h_D=32$, $M=10^6$, and $\delta=10^{-14}$ in <a href="#ind:coefficient-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:coefficient-budget">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>. There are no atoms. If $Q_m,D_m$ have rational errors at most $10^{-25}$, either entry of <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a> has error less than $2.13\cdot10^{-13}<10^{-12}$.*

*For the full rational finite-input domain at the start of this subsection, take $D=255$, $h_D=128$, $M=10^{53}$, and $\delta=4\cdot10^{-15}$ in <a href="#ind:coefficient-budget" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:coefficient-budget">[ind:coefficient-budget]</a>, and require each of the seven atom-product radii in <a href="#ind:atom-radii" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:atom-radii">[ind:atom-radii]</a> to be at most $4\cdot10^{-15}/7$. Each $U$ and $X$ moment then has error less than $10^{-14}$. For node rows $m\in I_f$, with rational $Q_m,D_m$ errors at most $10^{-25}$, either entry of <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a> has error less than $1.8\cdot10^{-14}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ind-taylor-budgets"><summary>Proof of stmt-ind-taylor-budgets</summary>

*Proof.* The first-block majorant $10^6$ is proved in Section <a href="#ind:small-kernel" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:small-kernel">7.5.1</a>. Lemma <a href="#ind:taylor-average" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:taylor-average">20</a> gives $$\epsilon\le10^6 2^{-63}+10^{-14}<1.19\cdot10^{-13}.$$ On the real interval a basis density has modulus below $214$, $|\lambda|<3$, and $\pi|z|<10$; thus the two exact moments have modulus below $10^4$. Substitution of $H=10^4$, $q=7/4$, $d=2.12$, and $\eta_Q=\eta_D=10^{-25}$ in <a href="#ind:pair-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:pair-stability">[ind:pair-stability]</a> gives $$\frac{(3.12+10^{-25})1.19\cdot10^{-13}+10^4 10^{-25}}{7/4}
 +\frac{3.12\cdot10^4 10^{-25}}{(7/4)^2}<2.13\cdot10^{-13}.$$ The pointwise majorant $10^{53}$ for the full domain was proved in Lemma <a href="#cert:moment-error" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cert:moment-error">16</a>. In the second case the total moment radius is at most $$10^{53}2^{-255}+8\cdot10^{-15}
 <1.728\cdot10^{-24}+8\cdot10^{-15}<10^{-14}.$$ For the value and first-derivative moments in <a href="#cert:pair" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:pair">[cert:pair]</a>, the real density is bounded by $1000$, the sum of seven atom moduli by $42$, and the two factors by $3$ and $30$. Hence both exact moments are bounded by $H=4\cdot10^4$. The right side of <a href="#ind:pair-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:pair-stability">[ind:pair-stability]</a> is then at most $$\frac{(3.12+10^{-25})10^{-14}+4\cdot10^4 10^{-25}}{7/4}
 +\frac{3.12\cdot4\cdot10^4 10^{-25}}{(7/4)^2}<1.8\cdot10^{-14}.$$ All inequalities here are comparisons of the displayed rational bounds. The refinement argument above proves that their coefficient and atom budgets can be met for any prescribed finite set of these moments. ◻

</details>

For a matrix with $r$ rows and entry error at most $\varepsilon$, the induced column-norm error is at most $r\varepsilon$, and multiplication by $W_\eta$ adds the factor $\|W_\eta\|_1$. In particular $r=6$ for the $J$ row block and $r=12$ for the rows on $E\cap[7,16]$. In a tabulated residual, the other summands in <a href="#cert:residual-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cert:residual-bounds">[cert:residual-bounds]</a> are exact rationals, so its error is just the corresponding pair error. Finally, for the signed half-gaps of Lemma <a href="#signs:bernstein" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="signs:bernstein">10</a>, the two moment errors below $10^{-14}$ give a power-coefficient error below $8\cdot10^{-14}$ and a Bernstein-coefficient error below $2.32\cdot10^{-12}$, as proved in Section <a href="#ind:bernstein" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="ind:bernstein">7.3.1</a>. These bounds specify the margins required in a Taylor-based comparison; they do not themselves assert the matrix, residual, or Bernstein values printed in Appendix <a href="#sec:certificates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:certificates">6</a>.

<a id="ind:node-taylor"></a>
#### Direct node-Taylor representation

There is also a local Taylor representation for the direct cardinal function that does not integrate its spectral density. It applies to the full $\ell^1$ class of <a href="#fourier:cardinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:cardinal">[fourier:cardinal]</a>. Write $\mathcal N_0=\{0\}\cup\mathcal N$ and, for $m\in\mathcal N_0$, put <a id="ind:node-radius"></a>$$\label{ind:node-radius}
 \rho_m=\min_{\substack{n\in\mathcal N_0\\n\ne m}}|m-n|.$$ The consecutive gaps in one period of $\mathcal N_0$ are $1,2,1,3,2,3$. Thus the minimum exists and is positive: $\rho_m=1$ for $m=0$ and for residues $0,1,3,4$, while $\rho_m=2$ for residues $7,9$. This is a guaranteed radius to the nearest other node; it may be smaller than the analytic radius for a particular list whose corresponding coefficients vanish.

<a id="stmt-ind-node-taylor-proposition"></a>
<div id="ind:node-taylor-proposition" class="proposition">

<a id="ind:node-P-coefficients"></a>
<a id="ind:node-regular-coefficients"></a>
<a id="ind:node-derivatives"></a>
**Proposition 22**. *Let $p=PR$ have a real $\ell^1$ coefficient list as in <a href="#fourier:list-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:list-norm">[fourier:list-norm]</a>. Define $$\label{ind:node-P-coefficients}
 A_k(m)=\frac{P^{(k)}(m)}{k!}
 =\frac1{k!}\sum_{v=-6}^6P_v(i\pi t_v)^k e^{i\pi t_vm}.$$ Then $A_0(m)=A_1(m)=0$. The regular series has coefficients $$\label{ind:node-regular-coefficients}
 r_j(m)=C\mathbf1_{\{j=0\}}+
 (-1)^j\sum_{\substack{n\in\mathcal N_0\\n\ne m}}
 \left(\frac{(j+1)c_n}{(m-n)^{j+2}}
       +\frac{d_n}{(m-n)^{j+1}}\right),$$ and converges for $|u|<\rho_m$. For $0<|u|<\rho_m$, the Laurent expansion is $$R(m+u)=\frac{c_m}{u^2}+\frac{d_m}{u}
       +\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}r_j(m)u^j.$$ For every integer $\ell\ge0$ the direct derivative is therefore $$\label{ind:node-derivatives}
 \frac{p^{(\ell)}(m)}{\ell!}
 =c_mA_{\ell+2}(m)+d_mA_{\ell+1}(m)
       +\sum_{k=2}^{\ell}A_k(m)r_{\ell-k}(m),$$ where the last sum is empty for $\ell=0,1$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-ind-node-taylor-proposition"><summary>Proof of stmt-ind-node-taylor-proposition</summary>

*Proof.* For $n\ne m$, the geometric series for $(m-n+u)^{-1}$ and its derivative give $$\frac1{m-n+u}=\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{(-1)^ju^j}{(m-n)^{j+1}},\qquad
 \frac1{(m-n+u)^2}=\sum_{j\ge0}
       \frac{(-1)^j(j+1)u^j}{(m-n)^{j+2}},$$ whenever $|u|<|m-n|$. To justify summing these series over the infinite list, fix $0\le r<\rho_m$ and set $q=r/\rho_m<1$. Since $|m-n|\ge\rho_m$ for every other node, the sum of absolute values on $|u|\le r$ is at most $$\frac{\sum_{n\ne m}|c_n|}{\rho_m^2}
       \sum_{j\ge0}(j+1)q^j
 +\frac{\sum_{n\ne m}|d_n|}{\rho_m}\sum_{j\ge0}q^j
 =
 \frac{\sum_{n\ne m}|c_n|}{\rho_m^2(1-q)^2}
 +\frac{\sum_{n\ne m}|d_n|}{\rho_m(1-q)}<\infty.$$ This proves absolute uniform convergence on every smaller closed disk, permits exchanging the node and Taylor sums, and gives <a href="#ind:node-regular-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:node-regular-coefficients">[ind:node-regular-coefficients]</a>. It also proves precisely why the nearest-other-node distance is a valid common radius.

The finite Fourier expansion <a href="#fourier:polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:polynomial">[fourier:polynomial]</a> gives <a href="#ind:node-P-coefficients" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:node-P-coefficients">[ind:node-P-coefficients]</a>, and the double zero of $P$ gives $A_0=A_1=0$. Multiply its entire Taylor series by the convergent Laurent series for $R$. The two principal terms are canceled by the double zero; collecting the coefficient of $u^\ell$ gives <a href="#ind:node-derivatives" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="ind:node-derivatives">[ind:node-derivatives]</a>. In particular $A_2=Q_m$ and $A_3=Q_mD_m$, so the cases $\ell=0,1$ reproduce <a href="#fourier:node-values" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:node-values">[fourier:node-values]</a>. ◻

</details>

For finite rational input, the constants in this direct formula can be enclosed by the rational operations above. For an arbitrary $\ell^1$ input the formula is an exact convergence statement; a computable error for truncating its node sum additionally requires a bound for that list’s tail. It represents the direct function $p$ only. The transformed function $K_p$ still has the kernel representation <a href="#fourier:kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="fourier:kernel">[fourier:kernel]</a>, and this local identity neither places $K_p$ in the cardinal class nor replaces the exact-derivative certificates for the tabulated functions.
